Hey everyone, thanks for the all the great work on this library. It makes unit testing a breeze! 

I ran into a bit of a strange issue with the Table.put_item() method in a mock DynamoDB table. It looks like if any key inside the item (including nested ones) is the character 'S', we get a SerializationException. 

`botocore.exceptions.ClientError: An error occurred (SerializationException) when calling the PutItem operation: Start of structure or map found where not expected`

While there is an obvious and simple workaround, (not to use 'S' as a key), it would be great to understand why this only occurs with the upper case 'S'. I have tried all letters of the alphabet, upper and lower, and the exception is triggered exclusively with 'S'. It does not matter where in the payload it might be. It could be deeply nested, and still raise the exception. If I replace it with, say an 'A', then it works fine again. My guess is that it might be related to the encoding (the tests I ran used the string.ascii_letters elements), but I cannot really be sure.

Here is a small example to replicate this behavior:
```
import string

import boto3
import moto

KEY_SCHEMA = [{'AttributeName': 'index', 'KeyType': 'HASH'}, ]
ATTRIBUTE_DEFINITIONS = [{'AttributeName': 'index', "AttributeType": "N"}, ]
PROVISIONING = {"ReadCapacityUnits": 1, "WriteCapacityUnits": 1}


@moto.mock_dynamodb
def test():
    dynamodb = boto3.resource('dynamodb')
    dynamodb.create_table(TableName="test",
                          KeySchema=KEY_SCHEMA,
                          AttributeDefinitions=ATTRIBUTE_DEFINITIONS,
                          ProvisionedThroughput=PROVISIONING
                          )
    # payload = {'index': 0, 's': None} # works fine!!!
    # payload = {'index: 0, 'A': None} # also works fine
    payload = {'index': 0, 'S': None}  # Doesn't work
    # payload = {'index': 0, 'A': {'S': None}} # Neither does this, but clearly not because of the 'A'
    table = dynamodb.Table(name='test')
    table.put_item(Item=payload)

test()

```

<#sep#>, if we do find dyscalculia is likely to be the cause of your problems with maths, we do provide, free of charge, some materials you can use with a friend who is not dyscalculic, which can help you improve your maths.

Tony Attwood C.Ed., B.A., M.Phil (Lond), F.Inst.A.M.

Is dyscalculia real, and if so how can those with dyscalculia be helped?

Twenty years ago when my colleagues and I started the Dyscalculia Information Centre we did so because we realised many people were becoming aware of dyscalculia, either because they felt it could explain their own failings at maths, or those of their own children, or indeed the young people they were teaching, but at the same time didn’t have access to much non-technical information on the subject.

Many wanted more information about what dyscalculia is, and what can be done about.  Others wanted to know if it was just an excuse, having heard on social media that it didn’t really exist at all!  Yet others wanted to know about the legal issues: are schools, universities and employers legally obliged to provide exam exemptions or support for people with dyscalculia?

We quickly realised that at the time it was quite hard to find out very much about dyscalculia in order to answer questions such as these.  There were a few books published on the topic, but they tended for the most part to be academic in nature rather than of direct practical benefit to parents, teachers, or adults who felt they might have dyscalculia.

Thus our approach has been to work with everyone who comes across dyscalculia in one way or another, to help them understand what dyscalculia is, help those they teach overcome the problems dyscalculia bring, and also help establish if a person is likely to have dyscalculia, before spending substantial sums on a full-blown assessment undertaken by a psychologist.

Inevitably our work has taken us beyond maths.  For just as a difficulty in using the written language has implications for many aspects of life, so does a difficulty with using maths cause problems throughout daily existence.  Problems with dates, money (even in the days of credit card, the account still needs to be checked), time, distances, learning sequences, even geography…

Over time the Dyscalculia Centre has found its place in the order of things, providing classroom materials, low-cost testing, and information where required.  And where we find we have often been asked the same question, we’ve tried to write a free briefing paper to provide the answer.  The main areas of our work are listed at the top of each page on our website.  Briefing papers are listed under “Latest articles” on the home page.

Of course many people want to know if they themselves, those they teach, or their own children are dyscalculic, and we offer help with this.

For others the prime question relates to colleges and universities which require maths at a certain grade for a student can be admitted on a course, and how students who are dyscalculic can be helped in this regard.

We also get many questions relating to what the law says about dyscalculia and education and employment – hence our article “Special Needs and the Law”.

We like to think that over the past 20 years we’ve provided a fair range of information on dyscalculia on the website but if you can’t find what you are looking for we’ll most certainly try and help.  Just email This email address is being protected from spambots. You need JavaScript enabled to view it. with your enquiry and we’ll get back to you.

Tony Attwood C.Ed., B.A,. M.Phil (Lond) F.Inst.A.M

The Dyscalculia Centre

Part one: What is dyscalculia?

Dyscalculia is the name given to difficulty in learning about, comprehending or using numbers, which is out of line with the individual’s general educational level.

In a typical case we might find a person who is clearly of average or above average intelligence, and yet when it comes to maths is unable to undertake the most basic calculations.

Such people will have difficulty in undertaking mathematical calculations such as adding, subtracting, multiplying and dividing, and can often find they also have difficulty understanding money, time, distances, directions and so on.

Dyscalculia is sometimes called “Number Blindness” and it was first noted in 1919 by Salomon Henschen, a Swedish neurologist, who specifically noted that the people he was considering had lower than expected mathematical abilities but average or above average intelligence in general.

The cause of dyscalculia is now known to be genetic – which means that dyscalculia cannot be cured.  However it is possible, with the right kind of specialist teaching, for dyscalculic people to come to understand and use maths.  It is obviously most helpful if this specialist teaching is undertaken at school, but because this does not happen in many cases, the dyscalculic person can reach adulthood having had no help in overcoming this disability and not having found the support and help he or she quite reasonably feels is needed.

A typical comment from a dyscalculic person who has never had any specialist help can be along the lines of, “I know I’m not stupid, but I just can’t do maths!”

Unfortunately, although dyscalculia is a recognised medical condition, it does not readily come under the ambit of GPs or the NHS, not least because it is genetic in origin and therefore (as already noted) there is no cure that can be offered.  However this does not mean that dyscalculic people cannot be helped.

The Dyscalculia Centre is a private organisation which primarily provides materials to help pupils and students in school, college and university attain a basic level of maths ability.  However, since we were founded in 2010 we have had a growing number of enquiries from adults who feel they may be suffering from dyscalculia – hence this page, and the fact that our tests for dyscalculia can be used by adults as well as pupils and students.

Thus we work in two major fields: one is the testing of individuals to see if it is likely that they have dyscalculia, and the other is the provision of materials to help people who are dyscalculic understand basic maths.  Both of these fields of work can be used by adults as well as children.

The diagnostic test and the materials are not age dependent.  The materials do on occasion make reference to “pupil” and “student” as the majority of people taking our diagnostic test or being taught using our materials are under 18, but this should not be taken to mean that the materials are not suitable for older people.

Part two: Exams and employment

There is a tradition in British exams that where a person taking an exam suffers from a condition that has nothing to do with the issues being studied, but which may affect the taking of the exam, then the examining body should arrange for special provision to be made for the individual.

Thus a person who is dyslexic may be given extra time in a geography exam to write essays, and may also have a person sitting with him/her who will read the questions to the student and perhaps read back the student’s written answers.  These arrangements are there because the aim of the exam is to test the student’s understanding of geography, not the student’s ability to read and write.

However the exact details of how exemptions are arranged is down to each exam board or organisation running the exam, and these not only differ between exam boards, but can differ over time.

But exemptions are special arrangements and are not always applicable.  To give an extreme example, an accountant clearly has to be able to cope with maths at fairly advanced level, and thus the fact that a person is dyscalculic would not be considered an acceptable reason for that person not having the expected ability in maths at the start of the course.

Likewise, when a nurse wishes to become a prescribing nurse she/he has to take a maths test, and no allowance for dyscalculia is made, because being able to prescribe and administer the correct dose of a drug is considered to be the essence of the work.   However it seems that some exam boards do now extend this notion, and are refusing to make any allowance for dyscalculia in any exam.

Part three: Entry requirements and discrimination against dyscalculic people

It is common for universities and colleges to stipulate that students applying for a specific course must first have certain qualifications. And indeed sometimes this is perfectly logical and understandable, as noted above with the case of a course in accountancy.  But the institution is on less solid ground if teaching A level art, or a degree course in art, and requiring that the student has to have a good pass in maths at GCSE.  However that claim is still made in some cases.

Unfortunately an appeal against this which goes to the university will normally be rejected on the grounds that “these are the regulations”.  Taking the appeal further will be very time-consuming and normally the student wants to enter a course now – not in two years’ time after various appeals procedures have been completed.

Part four: The tests for dyscalculia

Testing for dyscalculia comes in<#sep#>Implement a Python class `Book` with the following specifications:

- **Attributes:**
  - `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
  - `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
  - `year_published`: An integer representing the year the book was published.

- **Methods:**
  - `__init__(self, title: str, author: str, year_published: int)`: Initializes the book object with the given title, author, and year published.
  - `get_title(self) -> str`: Returns the title of the book.
  - `set_title(self, title: str) -> None`: Sets a new title for the book.
  - `get_author(self) -> str`: Returns the author of the book.
  - `set_author(self, author: str) -> None`: Sets a new author for the book.
  - `get_year_published(self) -> int`: Returns the year the book was published.
  - `set_year_published(self, year_published: int) -> None`: Sets a new year for the book's publication.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949)
print(book.get_title())  # Output: 1984
book.set_title("Animal Farm")
print(book.get_title())  # Output: Animal Farm
print(book.get_author())  # Output: George Orwell
book.set_author("Eric Arthur Blair")
print(book.get_author())  # Output: Eric Arthur Blair
print(book.get_year_published())  # Output: 1949
book.set_year_published(1945)
print(book.get_year_published())  # Output: 1945
```

```python
class Book:
    """
    A class to represent a Book.

    Attributes:
        title (str): The title of the book.
        author (str): The author of the book.
        year_published (int): The year the book was published.
    """

    def __init__(self, title: str, author: str, year_published: int):
        """
        Constructs all the necessary attributes for the book object.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param year_published: The year the book was published.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year_published = year_published

    def get_title(self) -> str:
        """
        Gets the book title.

        :return: The title of the book.
        """
        return self.title

    def set_title(self, title: str) -> None:
        """
        Sets the book title.

        :param title: The new title of the book.
        """
        self.title = title

    def get_author(self) -> str:
        """
        Gets the book author.

        :return: The author of the book.
        """
        return self.author

    def set_author(self, author: str) -> None:
        """
        Sets the book author.

        :param author: The new author of the book.
        """
        self.author = author

    def get_year_published(self) -> int:
        """
        Gets the year the book was published.

        :return: The year the book was published.
        """
        return self.year_published

    def set_year_published(self, year_published: int) -> None:
        """
        Sets the year the book was published.

        :param year_published: The new year the book was published.
        """
        self.year_published = year_published

Take derivative f’(t):

First term derivative: [ (1)(k t +1) - t (k) ] / (k t +1)^2 = (k t +1 - k t)/ (k t +1)^2 = 1/(k t +1)^2

Second term derivative: -2/(t +k)^2

So, f’(t) = 1/(k t +1)^2 - 2/(t +k)^2

Set f’(t)=0:

1/(k t +1)^2 = 2/(t +k)^2

Take square roots:

1/(k t +1) = sqrt(2)/(t +k)

Cross multiply:

t +k = sqrt(2)(k t +1)

Solve for t:

t +k = sqrt(2)k t + sqrt(2)

Bring terms with t to left:

t - sqrt(2)k t = sqrt(2) -k

Factor t:

t (1 - sqrt(2)k) = sqrt(2) -k

Therefore:

t = (sqrt(2) -k)/(1 - sqrt(2)k)

Simplify numerator and denominator:

Multiply numerator and denominator by -1:

t = (k - sqrt(2))/(sqrt(2)k -1)

But since t must be positive, we need (k - sqrt(2)) and (sqrt(2)k -1) to have the same sign.

Given that k is a natural number >=2, let's check:

For k >=2,

sqrt(2) ~1.414, so for k=2:

sqrt(2)*2 ~2.828 >1, so denominator is positive.

Numerator: 2 -1.414 ~0.586 >0. So t = (0.586)/(2.828 -1) ~0.586/1.828 ~0.32>0. So for k=2, t is positive.

Similarly, for k >=2, sqrt(2)k -1 is positive since k>=2>1/sqrt(2). And numerator k - sqrt(2) is positive since k>=2>1.414. So t is positive.

Therefore, the critical point is at t=(k - sqrt(2))/(sqrt(2)k -1). Let me compute f(t) at this t.

But this seems complicated. Maybe there's a better approach. Alternatively, if we set t=1, which would correspond to b=1, a=1, c=1. Then f(t)=1/(k+1)+2/(1 +k)=3/(k+1). But earlier for k=2, the maximum was higher when a=2, b=c=1/sqrt(2), so maybe t=0.5 in that case. Wait, when a=2, then a=1/(b^2) => b^2=1/2 => t=1/2. Let's check k=2, t=1/2:

f(1/2) = (1/2)/(2*(1/2) +1) + 2/(1/2 +2) = (1/2)/(2) + 2/(2.5) = 1/4 + 4/5 = 0.25 + 0.8 = 1.05, which is 21/20, which is indeed higher than 1 (when t=1). So the critical point found here might be the maximum.

So in this case, when c = b, we can have a higher value. So perhaps the maximum occurs when c = b and a =1/(b²), and with t chosen such that this critical point is achieved. But this is getting complicated.

Alternatively, maybe the maximum occurs when two variables are equal, but not necessarily c = b. Wait, but the other case from the Lagrangian was bc =k. Let's check the other case.

Case 2: bc =k

If bc =k, then from the constraint abc=1, we have a=1/(bc)=1/k.

So, a=1/k, bc=k. Let's see what the expression becomes:

1/(a +k) + 1/(b² +k) +1/(c² +k) =1/(1/k +k) +1/(b² +k) +1/(c² +k)

First term: 1/(k +1/k) = k/(k² +1)

Now, since bc=k, let's set b = t and c =k/t. Then b² +k = t² +k and c² +k = (k²)/(t²) +k

So the expression becomes:

k/(k² +1) +1/(t² +k) +1/(k²/t² +k) =k/(k² +1) +1/(t² +k) + t²/(k² +k t²)

Hmm, this also seems complex. Let's check for t=1, which gives bc=k*1= k, so if t=1, then c=k, and bc=k, so a=1/k. Then expression is k/(k² +1) +1/(1 +k) +1/(k +k²). Wait, that would be k/(k² +1) +1/(k +1) +1/(k(k +1)).

Simplify:

k/(k² +1) + [1/(k +1) +1/(k(k +1))] = k/(k² +1) + [ (k +1)/(k(k +1)) ] = k/(k² +1) + 1/k

Wait, that's k/(k² +1) +1/k. Let's compute this for k=2: 2/(4 +1) +1/2= 2/5 +1/2=0.4 +0.5=0.9 <1.05. So worse than the previous case.

Alternatively, maybe set t such that 1/(t² +k) + t²/(k² +k t²) is maximized. Let's set s = t², so s >0. Then the expression is 1/(s +k) + s/(k² +k s) =1/(s +k) + s/(k(s +k)) =1/(s +k) +1/k * s/(s +k) = (1 +1/k * s )/(s +k)

Let me write it as (1 + s/k)/(s +k). To maximize this with respect to s.

Let’s set f(s) = (1 + s/k)/(s +k). Take derivative:

f’(s) = [ (1/k)(s +k) - (1 + s/k)(1) ]/(s +k)^2

Compute numerator:

If a cluster has instances, the cluster cannot be deleted, but we can do delete cluster even with instances in moto.
I'd like to try on this issue :)

Reference:
https://repost.aws/knowledge-center/rds-error-delete-aurora-cluster

```python
from moto import mock_rds


@mock_rds
def test_delete_rds_instance():
    target_cluster_identifier = "test-cluster"
    target_instance_identifier = "test-instance"
    rds_client = boto3.client("rds")
    rds_client.create_db_cluster(
        DBClusterIdentifier=target_cluster_identifier,
        Engine="aurora-postgresql",
        MasterUsername="test-user",
        MasterUserPassword="password",
    )
    rds_client.create_db_instance(
        DBClusterIdentifier=target_cluster_identifier,
        Engine="aurora-postgresql",
        DBInstanceIdentifier=target_instance_identifier,
        DBInstanceClass="db.t4g.medium",
    )

   # Cluster can be deleted here.
   client.delete_db_cluster(
        DBClusterIdentifier=cluster_identifier,
        SkipFinalSnapshot=True,
    )

```

Part four: The tests for dyscalculia

Testing for dyscalculia comes in various forms, and it is important consider why you want the test done, as well as what the results of the test will tell you.  Tests range from being free, up to costing £350 or more, so it is important to think this issue through before you begin making arrangements.

The simplest type of test is the indicative test, and an example of this is available on this website.  It is available for free.  The results don’t say you have or don’t have dyscalculia, but they do indicate if you are likely to be dyscalculic or not.

The second level of test is the online diagnostic test , and again such a test is available from the Dyscalculia Centre.  This test costs £49.95 and is much more comprehensive than the introductory test and the results are much more detailed, giving insights into where the specific problems with maths are identified as being.  If you take the test and we find that you are likely to be dyscalculic then we will also provide you with materials that can help you overcome your dyscalculia.

However please note that these materials do need you to work with another person who is not dyscalculic.  That person does not have to be a teacher or a mathematician; it just has to be a person who does not have dyscalculia – a person who has passed GCSE maths at any time in the past will be able to help you with these materials.

You can pause the test at any time to give you a break, especially if you feel worried about facing mathematical questions.  You can start again later.

Calculators and other devices that can work out mathematical answers are not permitted at all in the test.  You can, however, work out answers on paper or using fingers, but must not look answers up or get help.  You may well find this short article on the test helpful.

The test costs £49.95 and this price includes the delivery to you of the resources that we feel will help you work with a friend or colleague to overcome your dyscalculia.

To proceed with the test please either send a cheque payable to Websites and Blogs for £49.95 to 1 Oathill Close, Brixworth, Northants NN6 9BE, or call 01604 880 927 where you can pay by credit card.<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BookstoreInventory` class to manage a bookstore's inventory. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add a Book**: Add a book to the inventory. Each book is represented by its `title`, `author`, and `price`.
2. **Remove a Book**: Remove a book from the inventory by its `title`.
3. **List Books**: Return a list of all books currently in the inventory.

Your implementation should include a `Book` class to represent individual books. Each `Book` object should have attributes `title`, `author`, and `price`.

**Input:**
- A series of commands to manipulate the inventory. Each command is on a new line.
- Commands can be:
  - `ADD <title> <author> <price>`: Add a book with the given title, author, and price.
  - `REMOVE <title>`: Remove the book with the given title.
  - `LIST`: List all books in the inventory.

**Output:**
- For each `LIST` command, output the list of books in the format `Book(title='<title>', author='<author>', price=<price>)`, one book per line.

**Sample Input:**
```
ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell 9.99
ADD ToKillAMockingbird HarperLee 8.99
LIST
REMOVE 1984
LIST
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Book(title='1984', author='GeorgeOrwell', price=9.99)
Book(title='ToKillAMockingbird', author='HarperLee', price=8.99)

Book(title='ToKillAMockingbird', author='HarperLee', price=8.99)
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, price):
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.price = price

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Book(title='{self.title}', author='{self.author}', price={self.price})"

class BookstoreInventory:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = []

    def add_book(self, book):
        """Add a book to the inventory."""
        self.books.append(book)

    def remove_book(self, title):
        """Remove a book from the inventory by its title."""
        self.books = [book for book in self.books if book.title != title]

    def list_books(self):
        """Return a list of all books in the inventory."""
        return self.books

# Demonstration of using the BookstoreInventory class
if __name__ == "__main__":
    inventory = BookstoreInventory()
    
    commands = [
        "ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell 9.99",
        "ADD ToKillAMockingbird HarperLee 8.99",
        "LIST",
        "REMOVE 1984",
        "LIST"
    ]
    
    for command in commands:
        parts = command.split()
        if parts[0] == "ADD":
            title, author, price = parts[1], parts[2], float(parts[3])
            book = Book(title, author, price)
            inventory.add_book(book)
        elif parts[0] == "REMOVE":
            title = parts[1]
            inventory.remove_book(title)
        elif parts[0] == "LIST":
            for book in inventory.list_books():
                print(book)
            print()

It is **not possible** to create a purely macro-based quine in C that avoids having any external executable code (like `main()`) or preprocessor directives such as `#include`. The limitations arise because the preprocessor and compiler require certain constructs (like `main()` for program entry) that cannot be entirely encapsulated within macros. However, you can create a quine that is **mostly macro-based** with minimal non-macro code.<#sep#>Jinja2 template name must be string or Template type
Moto [creates template names based on the Python memory `id()`](https://github.com/spulec/moto/blob/c0ce38dba888af7abccec7edec629747f5e15e21/moto/core/responses.py#L112), causing them to be set as integers. Newer versions of [Jinja specify that the name must be a string or a Jinja Template](https://jinja.palletsprojects.com/en/3.1.x/api/#jinja2.Environment.get_template). This can result in unexpected behavior in other packages which are expecting a string.

It seems like wrapping the call to `id()` with `str()` could be enough to remedy this issue.

--- a/moto/core/responses.py
+++ b/moto/core/responses.py
@@ -108,8 +108,17 @@ def environment(self):
     def contains_template(self, template_id):
         return self.environment.loader.contains(template_id)
 
+    @classmethod
+    def _make_template_id(cls, source):
+        """
+        Return a numeric string that's unique for the lifetime of the source.
+
+        Jinja2 expects to template IDs to be strings.
+        """
+        return str(id(source))
+

-        template_id = id(source)
+        template_id = self._make_template_id(source)

                 self.RIGHT_PATTERN, ">", re.sub(self.LEFT_PATTERN, "<", source)

The game world consists of four distinct locations: a forest, a cave, a beach, and a river. Each location has a unique description, a list of objects, and connections to other locations. The player can navigate between locations using commands such as "go north", "go south", "go east", and "go west". Additionally, the player can interact with objects in the current location using the command "take [object]". The game should start in the forest and continue until the player types "quit" or "exit". 

**Sample Input:**
```
go south
take torch
go east
go north
take mushroom
exit
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Welcome to the Adventure Game!
You can navigate by typing 'go [direction]' (north, south, east, west).
You can interact with objects by typing 'take [object]'.

You are in a dense forest with tall trees and wildflowers.
Objects in this area: mushroom

> go south
You are inside a dark cave. It's cold and the walls are damp.
Objects in this area: torch

> take torch
You have taken the torch.

> go east
You can't go east. Please choose a valid direction.

> go north
You are in a dense forest with tall trees and wildflowers.
Objects in this area: mushroom

> take mushroom
You have taken the mushroom.

> exit
Exiting game. Thanks for playing!
```

```python
# Define locations as dictionaries with descriptions, objects, and connected areas
locations = {
    'forest': {
        'description': "You are in a dense forest with tall trees and wildflowers.",
        'objects': ['mushroom'],
        'connected_areas': {'south': 'cave', 'east': 'beach'}
    },
    'cave': {
        'description': "You are inside a dark cave. It's cold and the walls are damp.",
        'objects': ['torch'],
        'connected_areas': {'north': 'forest', 'west': 'river'}
    },
    'beach': {
        'description': "You are on a beautiful sandy beach with a clear blue sea.",
        'objects': ['shell'],
        'connected_areas': {'west': 'forest'}
    },
    'river': {
        'description': "You are standing next to a babbling river.",
        'objects': ['fish'],
        'connected_areas': {'east': 'cave'}
    }
}

# Start in the forest location
current_location = 'forest'

def display_location_details():
    """Display the welcome message, instructions, and current location details."""
    print("\nWelcome to the Adventure Game!")
    print("You can navigate by typing 'go [direction]' (north, south, east, west).")
    print("You can interact with objects by typing 'take [object]'.")
    print("\n" + locations[current_location]['description'])
    print("Objects in this area: " + ', '.join(locations[current_location]['objects']))

def process_command(command):
    """Process the player's command to navigate or interact with objects."""
    command_parts = command.split()
    if len(command_parts) < 2:
        print("Invalid command. At least two words required (e.g., 'go north', 'take mushroom').")
        return False

    action = command_parts[0].lower()
    object_or_direction = command_parts[1].lower()

    if action == 'go':
        if object_or_direction in locations[current_location]['connected_areas']:
            global current_location
            current_location = locations[current_location]['connected_areas'][object_or_direction]
            display_location_details()
        else:
            print(f"You can't go {object_or_direction}. Please choose a valid direction.")
    elif action == 'take':
        if object_or_direction in locations[current_location]['objects']:
            print(f"You have taken the {object_or_direction}.")
            locations[current_location]['objects'].remove(object_or_direction)
        else:
            print(f"You can't take the {object_or_direction} from here.")
    else:
        print("Invalid action. Try 'go [direction]' or 'take [object]'.")
        return False

    return True

def game_loop():
    """Main game loop to handle player input and game progression."""
    display_location_details()
    while True:
        command = input("\n> ")
        if command.lower() in ['quit', 'exit']:
            print("Exiting game. Thanks for playing!")
            break
        process_command(command)

# Start the game
if __name__ == "__main__":
    game_loop()

It is **not possible** to create a purely macro-based quine in C that avoids having any external executable code (like `main()`) or preprocessor directives such as `#include`. The limitations arise because the preprocessor and compiler require certain constructs (like `main()` for program entry) that cannot be entirely encapsulated within macros. However, you can create a quine that is **mostly macro-based** with minimal non-macro code. Here’s how:

---

### Approach
The following code uses a macro to generate most of the program's output, including its own definition, but still requires a small amount of external code to initialize and execute it.

```c
#include <stdio.h>
#define Q(x) #x;x
char *q[] = { "Q(",
"\"#define Q(x) #x;x\n#define q Q(\"\\nchar *q[] = { %s, \n %s };\";);Q\\n*main() { printf(q[0],q[0],q[1]);}\"",
0
};
*main() { printf(q[0],q[0],q[1]); }
```

---

### Key Explanation:
1. **Macro Definition (`Q(x)`):**
   ```c
   #define Q(x) #x;x
   ```
   - The `Q` macro takes an argument `x`, stringifies it (`#x`), and appends the macro arguments (`x`). This allows the macro to generate a string containing its own definition and referenced data.

2. **Array Initialization:**
   ```c
   char *q[] = { "Q(", ... };
   ```
   - The macro `Q` is used to store self-referential strings in the array `q`. The first element `q[0]` contains:
     - A call to `Q` to construct the second element `q[1]` (a stringified representation of the macro and program structure).
     - The closing `)"` which is part of the `Q` macro syntax.

3. **`main()` Invocation:**
   ```c
   *main() { printf(q[0], q[0], q[1]); }
   ```
   - The `main` function (shortened to `*main()` for brevity) uses `printf` to expand `q[0]` with `q[1]`, which contains the escaped strings needed to reconstruct the program.

---

### Limitations:
- The code requires a small non-macro boilerplate (e.g., `main()` and `#include`).
- The preprocessor (`#define` and `#include`) must be used, so true "all macro" is impossible.

---

### Output:
When run, this program prints itself, including the `Q` macro and the `main` function. This is a valid, mostly macro-based quine in C, adhering to the constraints. 

---

### Final Note:
The C preprocessor and language fundamentally require some external scaffolding (like `main()`), so a pure macro-based quine without **any** external code is impossible. The code above achieves the closest possible version by minimizing reliance on external constructs.<#sep#>mock_cloudformation with SNS fails to delete stack

## Reproduction steps:

```python
def test_failure(cloudformation_mock):
    cloudformation_mock.create_stack(
        StackName="test-stack",
        TemplateBody='{"Resources": {"Bucket": {"Type": "AWS::SNS::Topic"}}}'
    )

```

Error is:

E           botocore.exceptions.ClientError: An error occurred (NotFound) when calling the DeleteStack operation: Topic with arn arn:aws:sns:us-east-1:123456789012:arn:aws:sns:us-east-1:123456789012:test-stack-Bucket-RYQRXXZJ9IRT not found
```

=+IF((\$E\$5-(\$E\$5-SUM(\$B\$9:M9)))&lt;=\$E\$5,(\$E\$5-(\$E\$5-M9))*\$F\$5,((\$E\$5*\$F\$5)+((\$E\$5-(\$E\$5-SUM(\$B\$9:M9)))-\$E\$5)*\$<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple genetic algorithm to evolve a population of strings to match a target string "HELLO WORLD". Your program should include the following functionalities:
- Create an initial population of random strings.
- Evaluate the fitness of each individual based on how closely it matches the target string.
- Select parents for reproduction based on their fitness scores.
- Perform crossover between pairs of parents to produce offspring.
- Introduce mutations in the offspring with a given mutation rate.
- Evolve the population over a specified number of generations and output the best individual found.

**Input:**
- No input is required for this problem.

**Output:**
- Print the generation number and the best individual string found at every 100 generations.
- If the target string "HELLO WORLD" is found, print the generation number and the string and terminate the program.

**Sample Output:**
```
Generation 0: Best fitness 2, Best individual HELLO WORLD
Generation 100: Best fitness 5, Best individual HELLO WORLD
Solved in Generation 150: HELLO WORLD
```

```python
import random
import string

target = "HELLO WORLD"
population_size = 100
mutation_rate = 0.01
generations = 1000

def create_individual(length=len(target)):
    """Create a random individual as a string of uppercase letters and spaces."""
    return ''.join(random.choice(string.ascii_uppercase + ' ') for _ in range(length))

def create_initial_population(size=population_size):
    """Create the initial population of individuals."""
    return [create_individual() for _ in range(size)]

def evaluate_fitness(individual):
    """Evaluate the fitness of an individual based on how close it is to the target string."""
    return sum(individual[i] == target[i] for i in range(len(target)))

def select_parents(population, fitnesses):
    """Select a pair of parents based on their fitness scores using roulette wheel selection."""
    total_fitness = sum(fitnesses)
    pick = random.uniform(0, total_fitness)
    current = 0
    for individual, fit in zip(population, fitnesses):
        current += fit
        if current > pick:
            return individual
    return population[-1]

def crossover(parent1, parent2):
    """Perform single point crossover between two parents to produce an offspring."""
    point = random.randint(1, len(target) - 1)
    return parent1[:point] + parent2[point:]

def mutate(individual):
    """Introduce random mutations in an individual with a given mutation rate."""
    return ''.join(
        random.choice(string.ascii_uppercase + ' ') 
        if random.random() < mutation_rate 
        else c 
        for c in individual
    )

initial_population = create_initial_population()

for generation in range(generations):
    fitnesses = [evaluate_fitness(individual) for individual in initial_population]
    new_population = []

    for _ in range(population_size // 2):
        parent1, parent2 = select_parents(initial_population, fitnesses), select_parents(initial_population, fitnesses)
        child1, child2 = crossover(parent1, parent2), crossover(parent2, parent1)
        new_population.extend([mutate(child1), mutate(child2)])

    initial_population = new_population[:]
    best_individual = max(initial_population, key=evaluate_fitness)
    best_fitness = evaluate_fitness(best_individual)
    if best_fitness == len(target):
        print(f"Solved in Generation {generation}: {best_individual}")
        break
    if generation % 100 == 0:
        print(f"Generation {generation}: Best fitness {best_fitness}, Best individual {best_individual}")
```<#sep#>, we need to set b =c. Because for symmetric variables, equality often gives extremum when the function is symmetric in those variables.

So, setting b =c, and then having a=1/b² due to abc=1. Which is exactly what we did in Case1. So yes, that makes sense.

Therefore, assuming b=c, then the problem reduces to two variables: a and b, with a=1/b². The expression becomes 1/(a +k) +2/(b² +k). Substitute a=1/b², get 1/(1/b² +k) +2/(b² +k)=b²/(1 +k b²) +2/(b² +k). Let’s denote t =b², then the expression is t/(1 +k t) +2/(t +k).

This is the same function f(t) we considered earlier, which has a critical point at t=(k - sqrt(2))/(sqrt(2)k -1 ). And the maximum value is (3k - 2√2)/(k² -1 ).

But let's verify if this is indeed the maximum. For k=2, the expression is (6 -2√2)/3≈(6 -2.828)/3≈1.057, which matches the maximum found numerically.

Now, to confirm this is the answer, we need to ensure that this is indeed the global maximum.

Alternatively, check the second derivative in the case where b=c. For the function f(t)= t/(kt +1) +2/(t +k), we found the first derivative f’(t) =1/(kt +1)^2 -2/(t +k)^2. To check if the critical point is a maximum, compute the second derivative.

f''(t)= -2k/(kt +1)^3 +4/(t +k)^3

At the critical point, we can check the sign. For t=(k - sqrt(2))/(sqrt(2)k -1 ), it might be negative, indicating a maximum. However, the calculation could be tedious, but given that for k=2, the critical point does yield a higher value than the other cases, it's likely a maximum.

Therefore, after analyzing all possible cases and using Lagrange multipliers, the maximum occurs when two variables are equal (b=c), and the third variable a=1/b², leading to the maximum value of (3k -2√2)/(k² -1 ).

However, the problem states k is a natural number greater or equal to 2. So, for each such k, the maximum value is (3k -2√2)/(k² -1 ).

But let's check if this expression can be simplified further. Let's factor numerator and denominator:

Denominator: k² -1=(k -1)(k +1)

Numerator: 3k -2√2. It doesn't factor nicely, so perhaps leave it as is.

Therefore, the maximum value is (3k -2√2)/(k² -1 ).

But wait, let me check for another k. Let's take k=3. The value is (9 -2*1.414)/8≈(9 -2.828)/8≈6.172/8≈0.7715. If we set a=2.046, b=c≈0.7, as before, and compute the expression:

1/(a +3)≈1/(2.046 +3)=1/5.046≈0.198

1/(b² +3)=1/(0.489 +3)=1/3.489≈0.286, two terms≈0.572

Total≈0.198+0.572=0.77, which matches. So this seems consistent.

Therefore, after thorough analysis, the maximum value of the given expression under the constraint abc=1 is indeed (3k - 2√2)/(k² -1 ).

**Final Answer**
The maximum value is \boxed{\dfrac{3k - 2\sqrt{2}}{k^2 - 1}}.
</think>To find the maximum value of the expression 
\[ \frac{1}{a+k} + \frac{1}{b^2+k} + \frac{1}{c^2+k} \]
where \(a, b, c\) are positive real numbers such that \(abc = 1\) and \(k \geq 2\) is a natural number, we proceed as follows:

1. **Symmetry and Substitution:**
   We assume \(b = c\) and \(a = \frac{1}{b^2}\) due to the constraint \(abc = 1\). Let \(t = b^2\), then \(a = \frac{1}{t}\).

2. **Transforming the Expression:**
   The expression becomes:
   \[
   \frac{1}{\frac{1}{t} + k} + \frac{1}{t + k} + \frac{1}{t + k} = \frac{t}{1 + kt} + \frac{2}{t + k}.
   \]
   Let \(f(t) = \frac{t}{1 + kt} + \frac{2}{t + k}\).

3. **Finding the Critical Points:**
   We take the derivative of \(f(t)\) with respect to \(t\):
   \[
   f'(t) = \frac{d}{dt} \left( \frac{t}{1 + kt} \right) + \frac{d}{dt} \left( \frac{2}{t + k} \right).
   \]
   Using the quotient rule, we get:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dt} \left( \frac{t}{1 + kt} \right) = \frac{(1 + kt) \cdot 1 - t \cdot k}{(1 + kt)^2} = \frac{1}{(1 + kt)^2},
   \]
   and
   \[
   \frac{d}{dt} \left( \frac{2}{t + k} \right) = -\frac{2}{(t + k)^2}.
   \]
   Therefore,
   \[
   f'(t) = \frac{1}{(1 + kt)^2} - \frac{2}{(t + k)^2}.
   \]
   Setting \(f'(t) = 0\), we have:
   \[
   \frac{1}{(1 + kt)^2} = \frac{2}{(t + k)^2}.
   \]
   Cross-multiplying gives:
   \[
   (t + k)^2 = 2(1 + kt)^2.
   \]
   Expanding both sides:
   \[
   t^2 + 2tk + k^2 = 2(1 + 2kt + k^2t^2) = 2 + 4kt + 2k^2t^2.
   \]
   Rearranging terms:
   \[
   t^2 + 2tk + k^2 = 2 + 4kt + 2k^2t^2,
   \]
   \[
   t^2 + 2tk + k^2 - 2 - 4kt - 2k^2t^2 = 0,
   \]
   \[
   -2k^2t^2 + t^2 - 2kt + k^2 - 2 = 0,
   \]
   \[
   (1 - 2k^2)t^2 - 2kt + (k^2 - 2) = 0.
   \]
   Solving this quadratic equation for \(t\):
   \[
   t = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{(2k)^2 - 4(1 - 2k^2)(k^2 - 2)}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4(1 - 2k^2)(k^2 - 2)}}{2(1 - 2k^2)}.
   \]
   Simplifying under the square root:
   \[
   t = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4(k^2 - 2 - 2k^4 + 4k^2)}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4(k^2 - 2k^4 + 2)}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4k^2 + 8k^4 - 8}}<#sep#>human: You are given N binary strings S_{1}, S_{2}, \dots, S_{N}, each of length M. You want to concatenate all the N strings in some order to form a single large string of length N \cdot M. Find the minimum possible number of inversions the resulting string can have.

A binary string is defined as a string consisting only of '0' and '1'.

An *inversion* in a binary string S is a pair of indices (i, j) such that i < j and S_{i} = '1', S_{j} = '0'. For example, the string S = "01010" contains 3 inversions : (2, 3), (2, 5), (4, 5).

Note that you are not allowed to change the order of characters within any of the strings S_{i} - you are only allowed to concatenate the strings themselves in whatever order you choose. For example, if you have "00" and "10" you can form "0010" and "1000", but not "0001".

------ Input Format ------ 

- The first line of input contains a single integer T denoting the number of test cases. The description of T test cases follows.
- The first line of each test case contains two space-separated integers N, M.
- N lines follow, the i^{th} of which contains the string S_{i} of length M.

------ Output Format ------ 

For each test case, print a single line containing one integer - the minimum possible number of inversions in the resulting string.

------ Constraints ------ 

$1 ≤ T ≤ 10^{3}$
$2 ≤ N ≤ 10^{5}$
$1 ≤ M ≤ 10^{5}$
$2 ≤ N \cdot M ≤ 2\cdot 10^{5}$
$\lvert S_{i} \rvert = M$
$S_{i}$ contains only characters '$0$' and '$1$'.
- Sum of $N \cdot M$ over all test cases does not exceed $10^{6}$.

----- Sample Input 1 ------ 
4
2 1
0
1
3 2
10
00
01
2 4
0010
1011
3 4
1011
1101
0101

----- Sample Output 1 ------ 
0
2
3
11
----- explanation 1 ------ 
Test case $1$: Two strings can be formed : $S_{1} + S_{2} = $"$01$", $S_{2} + S_{1} $= "$10$". The first string does not have any inversion while the second has one inversion : $(1, 2)$.

Test case $2$:  Both $S_{2} + S_{1} + S_{3} = $"$001001$", $S_{2} + S_{3} + S_{1} = $"$000110$" contain $2$ inversions. It can be verified that no other order results in fewer inversions.

gpt: <think> Okay, I need to find the minimum number of inversions possible when concatenating all the given binary strings in some order. Let me think about how inversions work here. 

An inversion is a pair (i,j) where i < j and S_i is '1' and S_j is '0'. So the goal is to arrange the strings such that as few such pairs exist as possible. 

Hmm, the key here is that the order in which we arrange the strings affects how the '1's and '0's are positioned relative to each other across different strings. For instance, if a string with more '1's comes before a string with more '0's, that could create more inversions. But maybe if we order the strings in a way that minimizes the crossings between '1's and '0's from different strings, that would be better.

Wait, but how exactly do we model this? Let's think of each string as a block. Let me see if there's a way to represent each string's contribution based on where it's placed.

Let's consider each string S_i. Maybe for each string, we can precompute some numbers like the number of 1's and 0's in it, or how many inversions are already within the string itself, and how they would interact when placed relative to other strings. 

Wait, inversions can come from two sources: those within the same string, which we can't change since we can't reorder characters within a string, and those between different strings. Since the order of the strings affects the inter-string inversions, that's where our choices come into play. 

So, the total inversions would be the sum of inversions within each individual string plus the inter-string inversions. Since the intra-s inversions are fixed, we only need to minimize the inter-s part. 

Therefore, our problem reduces to arranging the strings to minimize the inversions between different strings. 

The challenge is figuring out how to arrange the strings such that the arrangement minimizes the number of inversions caused by the placement between two strings. Let's think in terms of pairs of strings. Suppose we have two strings A and B. When placed in order A followed by B, the number of cross inversions between them would be the number of 1's in A multiplied by the number of 0's in B. Because every 1 in A will be before every 0 in B, creating an inversion. 

Similarly, if B comes before A, the cross inversions would be the number of 1's in B multiplied by the number of 0's in A.

Ah! That's key. So between two adjacent strings in the permutation, the number of cross inversions between them is directly determined by how many 1s are in the first and 0s in the second. 

This suggests that when comparing two strings, we need to decide which one to place first depending on which choice gives a better (smaller) contribution. 

So, for any two strings A and B, comparing them in two orders:

- If A is first: cross_inversions_A_B = (onesA) * (zerosB)
- If B is first: cross_inversions_B_A = (onesB) * (zerosA)

We want to choose the order between A and B which gives the smaller of the two. 

Comparing those two values: we should place A before B if (onesA * zerosB) < (onesB * zerosA). Otherwise, B before A. 

Hmm, this looks like comparing the ratios or the products. Let me see:

The condition can be rewritten as onesA / zerosA < onesB / zerosB. Wait, actually:

Let me see:

If (onesA * zerosB) < (onesB * zerosA), then that's equivalent to (onesA / zerosA) < (onesB / zerosB), assuming zerosA and zerosB are not zero. 

But perhaps a better way to think about this is in terms of weighted comparisons. So, the relative order of any two strings A and B can be determined by comparing the product of onesA * zerosB vs onesB * zerosA. 

To decide the optimal order of all N strings, we can sort them according to some key, such that when comparing any two strings, they are ordered to minimize the cross product as above.

The question now is: what's a suitable key for sorting the strings such that when sorted, the adjacent pairs will have their optimal order?

The standard approach here is to find a sorting key such that for any two strings A and B, their order in the sorted list is the one that gives better cross inversion. To do this, we can represent each string by some value that can be used in a comparator. The comparator needs to decide whether A should come before B.

The comparator would return "A comes before B" if the cross inversions when A comes first is better than when B comes first. 

Expressed as a comparator function:

def compare(A, B):
    a1, a0 = ones_A, zeros_A
    b1, b0 = ones_B, zeros_B
    cross_A_before_B = a1 * b0
    cross_B_before_A = b1 * a0
    if cross_A_before_B < cross_B_before_A:
        return -1  # prefer A first
    elif cross_A_before_B > cross_B_before_A:
        return 1   # prefer B first
    else:
        return 0   # same

But how can this be converted into a sorting key? Since the decision between A and B may depend on B and A's individual parameters. 

Alternatively, the desired order between A and B is determined by whether A should come before B, so this comparison is transitive? I need to confirm that this is true so that a total ordering exists.

- Adding a vertex to the graph.
- Adding an edge between two vertices.
- Performing a Depth-First Search (DFS) traversal starting from a given vertex.
- Performing a Breadth-First Search (BFS) traversal starting from a given vertex.

Your implementation should be efficient and handle up to 10^4 vertices and 10^5 edges.

**Sample Input:**
```
5 6
0 1
0 2
1 2
2 0
2 3
3 3
2
```
**Sample Output:**
```
2 0 1 3 
2 0 1 3 
```
The first line contains the number of vertices (V) and edges (E). The next E lines contain pairs of integers representing edges. The last line contains the starting vertex for both DFS and BFS traversals.

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque


class Graph:
    def __init__(self):
        # default dictionary to store graph
        self.graph = defaultdict(list)

    def add_vertex(self, vertex):
        """Add a vertex to the graph."""
        if vertex not in self.graph:
            self.graph[vertex] = []

    def add_edge(self, vertex1, vertex2):
        """Add an undirected edge between vertex1 and vertex2."""
        self.graph[vertex1].append(vertex2)
        self.graph[vertex2].append(vertex1)

    def dfs(self, start_vertex, visited=None):
        """Perform a Depth-First Search starting from start_vertex."""
        if visited is None:
            visited = set()
        visited.add(start_vertex)
        print(start_vertex, end=' ')

        for neighbor in self.graph[start_vertex]:
            if neighbor not in visited:
                self.dfs(neighbor, visited)

    def bfs(self, start_vertex):
        """Perform a Breadth-First Search starting from start_vertex."""
        visited = set()
        queue = deque([start_vertex])
        visited.add(start_vertex)

        while queue:
            vertex = queue.popleft()
            print(vertex, end=' ')

            for neighbor in self.graph[vertex]:
                if neighbor not in visited:
                    visited.add(neighbor)
                    queue.append(neighbor)


# Example usage:
if __name__ == "__main__":
    V, E = map(int, input().split())
    g = Graph()
    for _ in range(E):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        g.add_edge(u, v)
    start_vertex = int(input())

    print("Depth First Traversal (starting from vertex {}):".format(start_vertex))
    g.dfs(start_vertex)
    print("\nBreadth First Traversal (starting from vertex {}):".format(start_vertex))
    g.bfs(start_vertex)

   \[
   t = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4(k^2 - 2 - 2k^4 + 4k^2)}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4(k^2 - 2k^4 + 2)}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{4k^2 - 4k^2 + 8k^4 - 8}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm \sqrt{8k^4 - 8}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{2k \pm 2\sqrt{2k^4 - 2}}{2(1 - 2k^2)} = \frac{k \pm \sqrt{2k^4 - 2}}{1 - 2k^2}.
   \]
   The positive root is:
   \[
   t = \frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}.
   \]

4. **Evaluating the Expression:**
   Substituting \(t = \frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}\) back into the expression:
   \[
   f\left(\frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}\right) = \frac{\frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}}{1 + k \cdot \frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}} + \frac{2}{\frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1} + k}.
   \]
   Simplifying each term:
   \[
   \frac{\frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}}{1 + \frac{k^2 - k\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}} = \frac{\frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}}{\frac{\sqrt{2}k - 1 + k^2 - k\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}} = \frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{k^2 - 1},
   \]
   and
   \[
   \frac{2}{\frac{k - \sqrt{2} + k(\sqrt{2}k - 1)}{\sqrt{2}k - 1}} = \frac{2(\sqrt{2}k - 1)}{k^2 - 1}.
   \]
   Therefore, the expression becomes:
   \[
   \frac{k - \sqrt{2}}{k^2 - 1} + \frac{2(\sqrt{2}k - 1)}{k^2 - 1} = \frac{k - \sqrt{2} + 2\sqrt{2}k - 2}{k^2 - 1} = \frac{3k - 2\sqrt{2}}{k^2 - 1}.
   \]

For A and B: A would come before B (so cross A before B is better).

For B and C: B would come before C.

For C and A: C comes before A (so cross C before A is better than A before C).

Suppose:

Alternatively, maybe this is equivalent to sorting the strings in a particular order based on a ratio. 

Hmm. Wait, let's see.

Then, if we sort the strings in increasing order of this score, perhaps their order would be the optimal. 

Let me check:

Suppose two strings A and B. 

Compare their scores (a1/a0) and (b1/b0). 

Suppose S_A = a1/a0, S_B = b1/b0.

If S_A < S_B → then a1/a0 < b1/b0 → rearranged, a1 * b0 < b1 *a0 → which is exactly the condition where placing A before B gives a smaller cross inversion. Therefore, in this case, A should come before B. 

So, the ordering based on increasing order of (a1/a0) would be exactly the right way. 

That makes sense. Therefore, the optimal way to arrange the strings is to sort them in increasing order of (number of '1's divided by number of '0's). 

Wait, but what if a string has zero 0's? Then the ratio would be infinity. Similarly, a string with zero 1's has a zero ratio. 

Let me think. So if a string has no 0s (i.e. all are 1s), then its "score" is infinite, so in increasing order it would come after any other strings. Because, for another string B with any 0's, their scores will be finite. Or wait, if a string has all 1's (zero 0), then a1 / a0 is infinity. So a string with all 1's would have the highest score and should be placed after all others with finite scores. 

Wait, for example, between two strings A and B where A has all 1's (so a0=0), and B has some 0. 

Then, since A's score is infinity, comparing to B's finite score, which is smaller. The correct order between A and B would be which? 

Suppose A is all 1's, so placing B before A would be better. Because the cross for B before A would be (b1 * a0) = b1*0 = 0. 

The cross when putting A before B would be (a1 * b0). 

Since a0 is zero, placing B before A would always be better. So indeed, the A (with infinite score) should come after B. So in the sorted order, B is placed before A, so the infinite score string comes after others. 

Similarly, a string that has all 0's has a score of 0, so would come first. 

Therefore, the key idea is to sort the strings in increasing order of (number_of_1s/(number_of_0s)), but handling the case when number_of_0s is zero (so all 1's). 

However, for those with all zeros (no 1's?), then the ratio is zero, so they would come first. 

Wait but let's check in code terms: how to handle fractions correctly so that division is handled properly. Also, since divisions could be ambiguous with floating-point inaccuracies, perhaps we can represent this comparison as cross_AB < cross_BA? 

Alternatively, when comparing two strings A and B, instead of using the floating point ratio, just use the cross products as a way to compute the ordering. 

So, the comparator between A and B (as before):

if (A_ones * B_zeros) < (B_ones * A_zeros) → A comes first. 

Else, B comes first. 

This is exactly the condition we determined before. So, instead of using a key, we can define a comparator between two strings and then sort the list using that comparator, so that the order is optimal. 

Now, considering that the constraints can be up to N=1e5, the sorting is manageable. Because even with N=1e5, the sort is O(N log N), which for 1e5 is around 1e6 operations, which is acceptable. 

So the plan is:

1. For each string, compute the count of '1's and '0's. Let's call these ones_i and zeros_i.

2. Sort all the strings based on their "compare" function as above. That is, the comparator between S_i and S_j returns:

   compare(S_i, S_j) -> 

   if (S_i.ones * S_j.zeros) < (S_j.ones * S_i.zeros) → place S_i before S_j → return -1. 

   else → return 1 (so S_j comes before S_i)

   If equal → then it doesn't matter, but returns 0.

=IF(AND(M\$14&gt;0,M\$15&gt;0<#sep#>Implement a `BankAccount` class with the following specifications:

- **Constructor**: `__init__(self, account_number, owner, balance=0)` initializes the bank account with an account number, owner's name, and an optional initial balance (default is 0).
- **Deposit Method**: `deposit(self, amount)` adds the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive. Return the new balance or an error message if the amount is not positive.
- **Withdraw Method**: `withdraw(self, amount)` subtracts the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available. Return the new balance or an error message if the amount is not positive or if there are insufficient funds.
- **Get Balance Method**: `get_balance(self)` returns the current balance of the account.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- `account = BankAccount("123456789", "John Doe", 100)`
- `account.deposit(50)` should return `150`
- `account.withdraw(30)` should return `120`
- `account.withdraw(150)` should return `"Insufficient funds."`
- `account.get_balance()` should return `120`

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_number, owner, balance=0):
        """
        Initializes a new bank account.

        :param account_number: A string representing the account number.
        :param owner: A string representing the owner's name.
        :param balance: An optional float representing the initial balance (default is 0).
        """
        self.account_number = account_number
        self.owner = owner
        self.balance = balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits a specified amount into the account.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to deposit.
        :return: The new balance if the deposit is successful, otherwise an error message.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            return self.balance
        else:
            return "Deposit amount must be positive."

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws a specified amount from the account.

        :param amount: A float representing the amount to withdraw.
        :return: The new balance if the withdrawal is successful, otherwise an error message.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                return self.balance
            else:
                return "Insufficient funds."
        else:
            return "Withdrawal amount must be positive."

    def get_balance(self):
        """
        Returns the current balance of the account.

        :return: A float representing the current balance.
        """
        return self.balance
```<#sep#>Given a symmetric matrix \( M = \begin{pmatrix} A & B \\ B^T & D \end{pmatrix} \) where \( M \in M_n(\mathbb{R}) \), let \(\lambda_1\) and \(\lambda_2\) be the largest and smallest eigenvalues of \(M\), respectively. import moto

def test_non_existing_work_group():
    with moto.mock_athena():
        athena_client = boto3.client("athena", region_name="us-east-1")
        athena_client.get_work_group(WorkGroup="nonexisting")


def test_existing_work_group():
    with moto.mock_athena():
        athena_client = boto3.client("athena", region_name="us-east-1")
        athena_client.create_work_group(Name="existing")
        athena_client.get_work_group(WorkGroup="existing")
```

The class should have the following functionalities:

- Initialize the account with a zero balance.
- Deposit a specified amount into the account. Ensure the deposit amount is positive.
- Withdraw a specified amount from the account. Ensure the withdrawal amount is positive and does not exceed the current balance.
- Display the current balance of the account.
- Print the transaction history, which includes all deposits and withdrawals. Each transaction should be recorded as a tuple containing the transaction type ('deposit' or 'withdrawal') and the amount.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
account = BankAccount()
account.deposit(100)
account.withdraw(50)
account.display_balance()  # Output: Current balance: $50.00
account.print_transaction_history()
# Output:
# Transaction History:
# Deposit: $100.00
# Withdrawal: $50.00
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self):
        """
        Initializes a new bank account with a balance of zero and an empty transaction history.
        """
        self.balance = 0
        self.transactions = []

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposits a specified amount into the bank account if the amount is positive.
        
        :param amount: The amount to deposit.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            self.transactions.append(('deposit', amount))
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraws a specified amount from the bank account if the amount is positive and sufficient funds are available.
        
        :param amount: The amount to withdraw.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                self.transactions.append(('withdrawal', amount))
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def display_balance(self):
        """
        Displays the current balance of the bank account.
        """
        print(f"Current balance: ${self.balance:.2f}")

    def print_transaction_history(self):
        """
        Prints the transaction history of the bank account.
        """
        print("Transaction History:")
        for transaction_type, amount in self.transactions:
            print(f"{transaction_type.capitalize()}: ${amount:.2f}")

Now, once the optimal order is determined, the next thing is to compute the total inversions. 

Total inversions is the sum of all cross inversions (the cross_sum) plus the inversions within each individual string (summing the intra inversions). 

So we need two things:

1. Calculate the cross_sum based on the permutation.

2. Compute the sum of intra inversions for all strings. 

Let's first think about the intra inversion calculation. 

For a given string S, its inversion count is the number of pairs (i,j) where i <j, S_i '1' and S_j '0'. To compute this for each S_i is achievable by traversing the string and counting, but given that M can be up to 1e5 and N up to 1e5, the total sum is up to 1e10. Wait, but:

For example, for a string like "0010" which has N*M up to 2e5 per test case. 

So for computing the intra inversions for each string:

For a particular string S, let's traverse from right to left. Keep track of the number of 0's encountered. Wait, here's how: for each position, a '1' in that position adds to the inversion count the number of 0's that are to the right of it. 

Alternatively, here's a standard way to calculate inversions in a binary string:

Initialize count to zero, and zeros_so_far to 0.

We iterate from the end to the start:

for each character c in reversed(S):

    if c == '0':

        zeros_so_far +=1 

    else: 

        count += zeros_so_far

So this way, every '1' contributes the number of 0's that have been already seen (i.e., to the right), and since we are moving backward, yes. 

So this is O(M) per string, which is manageable.

# Problem
This is a follow up to https://github.com/getmoto/moto/issues/7138, but is technically a different issue, so I have opened a new one. 

I noticed when doing some checks for idempotency that the acm cert was being destroyed and recreated each time when the `aws_acm_certificate_validation` resource is used causing multiple resource to be replaced in the run.

I also noticed it when the `aws_acm_certificate_validation` resource was commented out as well, but it's not every time. It seems to be after a short period of time has passed. For example, with it commented out, I could run an apply and run another one right after and it would show no changes, and possibly even a third after that, but then after waiting a short period of time then it would show the changes below even though nothing in the code changed.

It almost seems like the domain validation options are removed after a period of time passes or if they are used in subsequent code, but that could just be coincidence.

Let me know if any questions and thanks in advance!

=IF(L10="Commission",IF(M9&lt;=\$E\$5,M9*\$F\$<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Logger` class in Python that supports logging messages with different severity levels (`info`, `warning`, `error`). Each log entry should include a timestamp and the severity level. The class should also support log rotation, which involves renaming the current log file to a new name with a timestamp suffix and starting a new log file.

**Methods:**
- `__init__(self, file_name="app.log")`: Initializes the logger with a specified log file name.
- `_log(self, level, message)`: Logs a message with the given severity level.
- `info(self, message)`: Logs an informational message.
- `warning(self, message)`: Logs a warning message.
- `error(self, message)`: Logs an error message.
- `rotate_log(self, new_suffix=None)`: Rotates the log file by renaming it with a timestamp suffix and starts a new log file.
- `__del__(self)`: Ensures the log file is properly closed when the logger object is deleted.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: 
  ```python
  logger = Logger("test.log")
  logger.info("System started")
  logger.warning("Disk space low")
  logger.error("Failed to connect to database")
  logger.rotate_log()
  logger.info("System restarted")
  ```
- Output: 
  - `test.log.20231005123456` (content: `2023-10-05 12:34:56 [INFO] System started\n2023-10-05 12:35:00 [WARNING] Disk space low\n2023-10-05 12:35:05 [ERROR] Failed to connect to database\n`)
  - `test.log` (content: `2023-10-05 12:35:10 [INFO] System restarted\n`)

```python
import datetime
import os

class Logger:
    def __init__(self, file_name="app.log"):
        """
        Initializes the logger with a specified log file name.
        """
        self.file_name = file_name
        self.file_handle = open(self.file_name, "a")

    def _log(self, level, message):
        """
        Logs a message with the given severity level.
        """
        timestamp = datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
        log_entry = f"{timestamp} [{level}] {message}\n"
        self.file_handle.write(log_entry)
        self.file_handle.flush()

    def info(self, message):
        """
        Logs an informational message.
        """
        self._log("INFO", message)

    def warning(self, message):
        """
        Logs a warning message.
        """
        self._log("WARNING", message)

    def error(self, message):
        """
        Logs an error message.
        """
        self._log("ERROR", message)

    def rotate_log(self, new_suffix=None):
        """
        Rotates the log file by renaming it with a timestamp suffix and starts a new log file.
        """
        self.file_handle.close()
        if new_suffix is None:
            new_suffix = datetime.datetime.now().strftime("%Y%m%d%H%M%S")
        new_file_name = f"{self.file_name}.{new_suffix}"
        os.rename(self.file_name, new_file_name)
        self.file_name = new_file_name
        self.file_handle = open(self.file_name, "a")

    def __del__(self):
        """
        Ensures the log file is properly closed when the logger object is deleted.
        """
        if self.file_handle:
            self.file_handle.close()

The application should have a single route `/books` which, when accessed, displays an HTML page containing a table of books. Each book entry in the table should include the title, author, and publication year. The books data should be hardcoded within the script. The HTML table should be styled with basic CSS for readability.

**Sample Output:**
When accessing `http://localhost:5000/books`, the page should display a table with the following data:

| Title                | Author             | Publication Year |
|----------------------|--------------------|------------------|
| To Kill a Mockingbird| Harper Lee         | 1960             |
| 1984                 | George Orwell      | 1949             |
| The Great Gatsby     | F. Scott Fitzgerald| 1925             |
| The Catcher in the Rye | J.D. Salinger    | 1951             |

```python
from flask import Flask, render_template_string

app = Flask(__name__)

# Sample data of books hardcoded in the script
books = [
    {"title": "To Kill a Mockingbird", "author": "Harper Lee", "year": 1960},
    {"title": "1984", "author": "George Orwell", "year": 1949},
    {"title": "The Great Gatsby", "author": "F. Scott Fitzgerald", "year": 1925},
    {"title": "The Catcher in the Rye", "author": "J.D. Salinger", "year": 1951},
]

# HTML template for displaying books
html_template = """
<!DOCTYPE html>
<html lang="en">
<head>
    <meta charset="UTF-8">
    <meta name="viewport" content="width=device-width, initial-scale=1.0">
    <title>Book List</title>
    <style>
        table {
            width: 50%;
            margin: 20px auto;
            border-collapse: collapse;
        }
        th, td {
            border: 1px solid #ddd;
            padding: 8px;
            text-align: center;
        }
        th {
            background-color: #f2f2f2;
        }
    </style>
</head>
<body>
    <h2 style="text-align: center;">List of Books</h2>
    <table>
        <tr>
            <th>Title</th>
            <th>Author</th>
            <th>Publication Year</th>
        </tr>
        {% for book in books %}
        <tr>
            <td>{{ book.title }}</td>
            <td>{{ book.author }}</td>
            <td>{{ book.year }}</td>
        </tr>
        {% endfor %}
    </table>
</body>
</html>
"""

@app.route('/books')
def books_page():
    """
    Renders an HTML page displaying a table of books.
    Each book entry includes the title, author, and publication year.
    """
    return render_template_string(html_template, books=books)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    app.run(debug=True)
```<#sep#> Alternatively, maybe I made a mistake in calculation.

Alternatively, let me try a different approach. Maybe consider the case where A and D are 1x1 again, but with some entries, but B is a rectangular matrix. Wait, but in the 2x2 case, it's always zero. Maybe in higher dimensions?

Wait, let me think of the following: The expression λ₁ + λ₂ - μ₁ - μ₂. Since the trace of M is trace(A) + trace(D). The sum of all eigenvalues of M is equal to trace(A) + trace(D). The sum λ₁ + λ₂ is two of the eigenvalues. so it can be anything less than or equal to the trace, but how does that relate to μ₁ + μ₂?

Wait, μ₁ is the largest eigenvalue of A, so μ₁ ≤ λ₁, since A is a submatrix? Not necessarily, because the largest eigenvalue of the whole matrix could be larger or smaller. Wait, actually, according to the Cauchy interlacing theorem, if we consider a principal submatrix, then the eigenvalues of the submatrix interlace with those of the full matrix. But here A is a principal submatrix, so the largest eigenvalue of A is less than or equal to the largest eigenvalue of M. Similarly, the smallest eigenvalue of A is greater than or equal to the smallest eigenvalue of M. Wait, let me recall interlacing properly.

Suppose M is partitioned as [A B; B^T D], then A is a principal submatrix (if we take the first k rows and columns). Then the eigenvalues of A interlace with those of M. So if M is n x n, and A is k x k, then for each i, λ_i(M) ≤ λ_i(A) ≤ λ_{n -k +i}(M). Hmm, maybe?

Wait, interlacing theorem states that if you have a Hermitian matrix M and a principal submatrix A, then for each i, λ_i(A) ≥ λ_i(M) and λ_i(A) ≤ λ_{i + n -k}(M). Wait, maybe I need to check exact statement.

Your task is to write a Python function `summarize_commit_messages` that processes these messages and returns a summary. The summary should include:
- The total number of commits.
- The most frequently occurring prefix in the commit messages. A prefix is defined as the substring of the commit message up to the first space character.
- A list of unique prefixes used in the commit messages.

**Input:**
- A list of strings, where each string is a commit message.

**Output:**
- A dictionary with the following keys:
  - `'num_commits'`: An integer representing the total number of commits.
  - `'most_common_prefix'`: A string representing the most common prefix. If there are multiple prefixes with the same highest frequency, return any one of them.
  - `'unique_prefixes'`: A list of unique prefixes.

**Sample Input:**
```python
commit_messages = [
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Update user interface",
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Add new feature",
    "Update user interface",
    "Refactor code"
]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{
    'num_commits': 6,
    'most_common_prefix': 'Fix',  # or 'Update'
    'unique_prefixes': ['Fix', 'Update', 'Add', 'Refactor']
}
```

```python
from collections import Counter

def summarize_commit_messages(commit_messages):
    """
    Generates a summary of commit messages including the number of commits,
    the most common prefix, and a list of unique prefixes.

    :param commit_messages: List of commit messages as strings.
    :return: Dictionary with keys 'num_commits', 'most_common_prefix', and 'unique_prefixes'.
    """
    num_commits = len(commit_messages)
    prefixes = []
    unique_prefixes = set()
    
    for message in commit_messages:
        prefix = message.split(' ')[0]
        prefixes.append(prefix)
        unique_prefixes.add(prefix)
    
    prefix_counts = Counter(prefixes)
    most_common_prefix = prefix_counts.most_common(1)[0][0] if prefix_counts else None
    
    summary = {
        'num_commits': num_commits,
        'most_common_prefix': most_common_prefix,
        'unique_prefixes': list(unique_prefixes)
    }
    
    return summary

# Test the function with the provided commit messages
commit_messages = [
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Update user interface",
    "Fix bug in login function",
    "Add new feature",
    "Update user interface",
    "Refactor code"
]

summary = summarize_commit_messages(commit_messages)
print(summary)

I need to paginate this API in my application and I'd like to test it with moto.

Pytest suite:

```python
from boto3 import Session
import json
from typing import Iterator
from pytest import fixture, mark
from pytest_mock import MockerFixture
from moto.utilities.paginator import paginate
from moto import mock_organizations


def _create_org(session: Session) -> None:
    org = session.client("organizations")
    org.create_organization(FeatureSet="ALL")


def _create_member(session: Session) -> None:
    org = session.client("organizations")
    org.create_account(Email="account@example.com", AccountName="Example")


@fixture()
def session() -> Iterator[Session]:
    with mock_organizations():
        yield Session()


@fixture()
def paginated_moto(mocker: MockerFixture) -> Iterator[None]:

    model = {
        "list_accounts_for_parent": {
            "input_token": "next_token",
            "limit_key": "max_results",
            "limit_default": 1,
            "result_key": "Accounts",
            "unique_attribute": "JoinedTimestamp",
        }
    }

    @paginate(pagination_model=model)
    def list_accounts_for_parent(self, **kwargs):
        parent_id = self.validate_parent_id(kwargs["ParentId"])
        return [
            account.describe()
            for account in self.accounts
            if account.parent_id == parent_id
        ]
    
    def response_list_accounts_for_parent(self):
        max_results = self._get_int_param("MaxResults")
        next_token = self._get_param("NextToken")
        accounts, next_token = self.organizations_backend.list_accounts_for_parent(
            max_results=max_results, next_token=next_token, **self.request_params
        )
        response = {"Accounts": accounts}
        if next_token:
            response["NextToken"] = next_token
        return json.dumps(response)

    mocker.patch("moto.organizations.utils.PAGINATION_MODEL", model)

    # The mock name must match the key in the pagination model.
    mocker.patch(
        "moto.organizations.models.OrganizationsBackend.list_accounts_for_parent",
        list_accounts_for_parent
    )
    mocker.patch(
        "moto.organizations.responses.OrganizationsResponse.list_accounts_for_parent",
        response_list_accounts_for_parent
    )


@fixture(autouse=True)
def org(session: Session):
    _create_org(session)
    _create_member(session)


def test_without_pagination_list_returns_2_accounts(session: Session):
    org = session.client("organizations")
    root_id = org.list_roots()["Roots"][0]["Id"]
    accounts = org.list_accounts_for_parent(ParentId=root_id)["Accounts"]
    assert len(accounts) == 2


@mark.usefixtures("paginated_moto")
def test_with_pagination_list_returns_1_account(session: Session):
    org = session.client("organizations")
    root_id = org.list_roots()["Roots"][0]["Id"]
    accounts = org.list_accounts_for_parent(ParentId=root_id)["Accounts"]
    assert len(accounts) == 1


@mark.usefixtures("paginated_moto")
def test_with_pagination_list_paginator_first_page_has_1_account(session: Session):
    org = session.client("organizations")
    root_id = org.list_roots()["Roots"][0]["Id"]

    paginator = org.get_paginator("list_accounts_for_parent")
    accounts = next(iter(paginator.paginate(ParentId=root_id)))["Accounts"]
    assert len(accounts) == 1


@mark.usefixtures("paginated_moto")
def test_with_pagination_list_paginator_all_pages_have_1_account(session: Session):
    org = session.client("organizations")
    root_id = org.list_roots()["Roots"][0]["Id"]

    paginator = org.get_paginator("list_accounts_for_parent")
    for page in paginator.paginate(ParentId=root_id):
        accounts = page["Accounts"]
        assert len(accounts) == 1
```
