The class should support the following operations:

- **Addition**: Implement a method `add` that takes another `ComplexNumber` object and returns a new `ComplexNumber` object representing the sum.
- **Subtraction**: Implement a method `subtract` that takes another `ComplexNumber` object and returns a new `ComplexNumber` object representing the difference.
- **Multiplication**: Implement a method `multiply` that takes another `ComplexNumber` object and returns a new `ComplexNumber` object representing the product.
- **Division**: Implement a method `divide` that takes another `ComplexNumber` object and returns a new `ComplexNumber` object representing the quotient. If the divisor is zero, raise a `ValueError`.
- **Magnitude**: Implement a method `magnitude` that returns the magnitude of the complex number.
- **Phase**: Implement a method `phase` that returns the phase angle in radians.
- **String Representation**: Implement a `__str__` method that returns a string representation of the complex number in the form "a + bi".

**Sample Input/Output**:
```
c1 = ComplexNumber(2, 3)
c2 = ComplexNumber(1, -1)
print(c1.add(c2))       # Output: 3 + 2i
print(c1.subtract(c2))  # Output: 1 + 4i
print(c1.multiply(c2))  # Output: 5 + 1i
print(c1.divide(c2))    # Output: -0.1538461538461539 + 2.153846153846154i
print(c1.magnitude())   # Output: 3.605551275463989
print(c1.phase())       # Output: 0.982793723247329
```

```python
import math

class ComplexNumber:
    def __init__(self, real, imaginary):
        self.real = real
        self.imaginary = imaginary

    def add(self, other):
        """Return the sum of two complex numbers."""
        return ComplexNumber(self.real + other.real, self.imaginary + other.imaginary)
    
    def subtract(self, other):
        """Return the difference of two complex numbers."""
        return ComplexNumber(self.real - other.real, self.imaginary - other.imaginary)
    
    def multiply(self, other):
        """Return the product of two complex numbers."""
        real = self.real * other.real - self.imaginary * other.imaginary
        imaginary = self.real * other.imaginary + self.imaginary * other.real
        return ComplexNumber(real, imaginary)
    
    def divide(self, other):
        """Return the quotient of two complex numbers. Raises ValueError if divisor is zero."""
        denominator = other.real ** 2 + other.imaginary ** 2
        if denominator == 0:
            raise ValueError("Cannot divide by zero")
        real = (self.real * other.real + self.imaginary * other.imaginary) / denominator
        imaginary = (self.imaginary * other.real - self.real * other.imaginary) / denominator
        return ComplexNumber(real, imaginary)
    
    def magnitude(self):
        """Return the magnitude of the complex number."""
        return math.sqrt(self.real ** 2 + self.imaginary ** 2)
    
    def phase(self):
        """Return the phase angle of the complex number in radians."""
        return math.atan2(self.imaginary, self.real)
    
    def __str__(self):
        """Return the string representation of the complex number in the form 'a + bi'."""
        return f"{self.real} + {self.imaginary}i"

def count_inv(s):

    count =0 

    zeros=0 

    for c in reversed(s):

        if c == '0':

            zeros +=1 

        else:

            count += zeros 

    return count

import sys 

import functools 

def main():

    T = int(sys.stdin.readline())

    for _ in range(T):

        N, M = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())

        strings = []

        total_intra =0 

        for _ in range(N):

            s = sys.stdin.readline().strip()

            o = s.count('1')

            z = M - o 

            current_inv = count_inv(s)

            total_intra += current_inv 

            strings.append( (o, z ) ) 

        # Sort the list using the comparator

        def cmp(a, b):

            a_o, a_z = a 

            b_o, b_z = b 

            val1 = a_o * b_z 

            val2 = b_o * a_z 

            if val1 < val2:

                return -1 

            elif val1 > val2:

                return 1 

            else:

                return 0 

        # Use the comparator to sort 

        sorted_str = sorted(strings, key=functools.cmp_to_key( cmp ) )

        # Compute suffix_z 

        n = len(sorted_str)

        suffix = [0]*(n+1)

        for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):

            suffix[i] = sorted_str[i][1] + suffix[i+1]

        cross_sum =0 

        for i in range(n):

            o, z = sorted_str[i]

            cross_sum += o * suffix[i+1]

        ans = cross_sum + total_intra 

        print(ans)

- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `published_year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
- `genre`: A string representing the genre of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN of the book.

The class should include the following methods:
- `get_age()`: Returns the age of the book in years from the current date.
- `is_classic()`: Returns `True` if the book is a classic (published more than 50 years ago), otherwise returns `False`.

Additionally, implement a function `book_from_dict` that takes a dictionary with book metadata and returns an instance of the `Book` class.

**Sample Input:**
```python
book_dict = {
    'title': '1984',
    'author': 'George Orwell',
    'published_year': 1949,
    'genre': 'Dystopian',
    'isbn': '9780451524935'
}
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
book = book_from_dict(book_dict)
print(book.get_age())  # Output will vary depending on the current year
print(book.is_classic())  # True

```python
from datetime import datetime

class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, published_year, genre, isbn):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param published_year: The year the book was published.
        :param genre: The genre of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.published_year = published_year
        self.genre = genre
        self.isbn = isbn

    def get_age(self):
        """
        Calculates the age of the book in years from the current date.

        :return: The age of the book in years.
        """
        current_year = datetime.now().year
        return current_year - self.published_year

    def is_classic(self):
        """
        Determines if the book is a classic (published more than 50 years ago).

        :return: True if the book is a classic, False otherwise.
        """
        return self.get_age() > 50

def book_from_dict(book_dict):
    """
    Creates a Book instance from a dictionary containing book metadata.

    :param book_dict: A dictionary with book metadata.
    :return: A Book instance.
    """
    return Book(
        title=book_dict.get('title'),
        author=book_dict.get('author'),
        published_year=book_dict.get('published_year'),
        genre=book_dict.get('genre'),
        isbn=book_dict.get('isbn')
    )

To solve this problem, we need to concatenate given binary strings in an optimal order such that the resulting string has the minimum number of inversions. An inversion is defined as a pair of indices (i, j) where i < j and the character at i is '1' while the character at j is '0'.

### Approach
1. **Understand Inversions**: Inversions can occur within individual strings and between different strings. While the intra-string inversions can't be altered, the inter-string inversions depend on the order of concatenation.
2. **Optimal Order for Strings**: To minimize inter-string inversions, the order of concatenation should be such that the product of the number of '1's in a string and the number of '0's in another following string is minimized. This can be achieved by sorting strings based on a pairwise comparison that compares these products.
3. **Sorting Strategy**: Use a custom comparator to sort the strings. The comparator for two strings A and B will place A before B if the number of inversions when A comes before B is less than when B comes before A. This is determined by comparing (o_A * z_B) and (o_B * z_A), where o and z are counts of '1's and '0's respectively.
4. **Efficient Calculation of Cross Inversions**: After sorting, compute the total cross inversions using suffix sums of zeros from the sorted list. This helps in quickly calculating the sum of zeros encountered after each string, contributing to the inversion count.

### Solution Code
```python
import sys
import functools

def count_inv(s):
    zeros = 0
    count = 0
    for c in reversed(s):
        if c == '0':
            zeros += 1
        else:
            count += zeros
    return count

def main():
    T = int(sys.stdin.readline())
    for _ in range(T):
        N, M = map(int, sys.stdin.readline().split())
        strings = []
        total_intra = 0
        for _ in range(N):
            s = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
            o = s.count('1')
            z = M - o
            current_inv = count_inv(s)
            total_intra += current_inv
            strings.append((o, z))
        
        # Define the comparator
        def comparator(a, b):
            a_o, a_z = a
            b_o, b_z = b
            val1 = a_o * b_z
            val2 = b_o * a_z
            if val1 < val2:
                return -1
            elif val1 > val2:
                return 1
            else:
                return 0
        
        # Sort using the comparator
        sorted_strings = sorted(strings, key=functools.cmp_to_key(comparator))
        
        # Compute suffix sums of zeros
        n = len(sorted_strings)
        suffix = [0] * (n + 1)  # suffix[i] = sum from i to n-1 of z
        for i in range(n-1, -1, -1):
            suffix[i] = sorted_strings[i][1] + suffix[i+1]
        
        # Calculate cross_sum
        cross_sum = 0
        for i in range(n):
            o, z = sorted_strings[i]
            cross_sum += o * suffix[i+1]
        
        ans = cross_sum + total_intra
        print(ans)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

### Explanation
1. **Reading Input**: The input is read line by line. For each string, we compute the number of '1's and '0's and store these values along with their intra-string inversions.
2. **Sorting**: The strings are sorted using a custom comparator that ensures strings are ordered to minimize the product of '1's in one and '0's in another when placed next to each other.
3. **Suffix Array Calculation**: A suffix array is used to efficiently compute the sum of zeros after each string, helping in determining the total cross inversion count.
4. **Result Calculation**: The total inversions are the sum of intra-string inversions and the computed cross inversions from the ordered concatenation.

This approach ensures that the arrangement of strings minimizes inter-string inversions efficiently, leveraging sorting and mathematical properties for optimal performance.<#sep#>EC2: Add support for hibernation APIs in `describe_instances`

Revision 2666 - Directory Listing
Modified Sun Aug 22 00:56:32 2004 UTC (15 years, 6 months ago) by dpvc
```Formulas can now be compared even if their variables are complex
numbers, or even points or vectors!  This makes it possible to ask
students for complex functions like z^2+i and so on.

You can specify limits for variables either via
Context()->variables->set(x=>{limits=>[0,1]})  (for reals) or
Context()->variables->set(z=>{limits=>[[-2,2],[0,1]]}) (for complexes).
Similarly for points and vectors (where the number of sub arrays
depends on the dimension).  You can also give the limits for a
specific formula by assigning to its ->{limits} field:

\$f = Formula("sqrt(x-10)");
\$f->{limits} = [10,12];

There are now named variable types for 'Point2D', 'Point3D',
'Vector2D' and 'Vector3D', in addition to 'Real' and 'Complex'.
You can also specify a variable type by giving an explicit instance of
a value of the given type.  E.g.

Context()->variables->add(r=>Vector(0,0,0));   # a vector in R^3
Context()->variables->add(X=>'Vector3D');      # a vector in R^3

There still needs to be more error checking in processing
user-supplied limits, but I'm not sure how much overhead is worth it.
```

Revision 2660 - Directory Listing
Modified Fri Aug 20 15:52:32 2004 UTC (15 years, 6 months ago) by dpvc
```A number of adjustments to get negative infinities to work better.
They used to be stored as separate Parser strings, but now they are
flagged versions of a single string.

All of the problems are really due to the fact that there is no
Infinity class in the Parser (only a Value version).  That should be
changed, but will require some work, and can be put off for now.

It would be best if the classes within the two packages would match up
nearly 1-to-1, but for historical reasons, they don't.  Since the
introduction of Real, String and Infinity classes to the Value
package, The Infinity class is the one main difference between the
two, and once that is fixed, everything needs to be looked over to
check for unnecessary complications due to the historical lack of classes
in one or the other package.
```

```The answer checkers now work with subclasses of Formulas that are not
part of the Value:: package tree itself (we call Value::isFormula()
rather than checking the class directly).

I also removed the various cmp_* flags that stored method names as
they ended up not needing to be used (they were intended as a means of
overriding the default method names, but subclassing turned out to be
a better approach).

I moved some initialization of the answer checker out of the anonymous
subroutine that is installed in the checker and just have that
subroutine call the correct answer's main checker (which I renamed).

Finally, I changed the student_ans value to be an HTMLized version of
the student answer, since the student could include things like < in
his answer (e.g., for vectors), in which case his whole answer might
disappear.  I wonder if this couldn't be handled better at a higher
level, as it seems wrong to make the student_ans be HTML rather than
the parsed string.  I originally thought Problem.pm could do the
protecting of HTML special characters, but this won't work without
student_ans to mark sytanx errors in the student answer, and you DON'T
want to protect those HTML characters.  The student_ans field is
probably being misused in this, and there might want to be another
field that gets used by Problem.pm in place of student_ans (which can
be HTML protected) when it is non-empty; say student_HTML or something
like that.  The answer checkers could use that field if it wants to
assurance that it's HTML characters will be protected.  That way, if a
second answer checker calls the first (like some list checkers do),
then the second checker doesn't have to worry about removing HTML from
the student ans, and doesn't ahve to wonder whether the student_ans
actually contains an error message or syntax markings rather than the
```

Revision 2634 - Directory Listing

```Changed the string used for the student answer to be taken from the
unevaluated formula rather than the fully-evaluated one.  This leaves
constants unreplaced, for one thing.

Currently the parser combines constants automatically during the parse
[which should be controllable by a flag], so student answers that
produce numbers are fully evaluated at this point already, except for
constant substitution.  This could be changed so that even for
numberic results, the student sees the parsed expression (rather than
the numeric result), which might help them identify where they and the
parser differ in their interpretation.  It is still nice to see the
final computed result, however, but there is no place to show the
parsed input as well as the final result.  One solution would be to
add another column (wasting space).  Another would be to make the
value shown be "parsed-value = computed-value" when they are not the
same thing.  A bit ugly, but works within the current framework.

Modified Tue Aug <#sep#>You are tasked with writing a program to analyze employee data stored in a CSV file. The CSV file contains the following columns: "Employee ID", "Name", "Department", "Position", and "Salary". Your program should read the CSV file and output the following statistics:
- Total number of employees.
- Average salary of all employees.
- Count of employees in each department.
- Count of employees in each position.
- Highest salary and the name of the employee with the highest salary.
- Lowest salary and the name of the employee with the lowest salary.

The program should accept the filename of the CSV file as a command-line argument and print the results to the console.

**Sample Input (employees.csv):**
```
Employee ID,Name,Department,Position,Salary
1,Alice,HR,Manager,70000
2,Bob,Engineering,Developer,80000
3,Charlie,Engineering,Developer,75000
4,David,HR,Assistant,50000
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Total number of employees: 4
Average salary of all employees: $68750.00
Number of employees in each department:
  HR: 2
  Engineering: 2
Number of employees in each position:
  Manager: 1
  Developer: 2
  Assistant: 1
Highest salary: $80000.00 (Employee: Bob)
Lowest salary: $50000.00 (Employee: David)
```

```python
import csv
from collections import defaultdict

def extract_employee_info(filename):
    """
    Reads an employee CSV file and prints various statistics including total number of employees,
    average salary, department and position counts, and highest and lowest salaries with corresponding names.
    
    :param filename: str - The path to the CSV file containing employee data.
    """
    total_employees = 0
    total_salary = 0
    department_counts = defaultdict(int)
    position_counts = defaultdict(int)
    highest_salary = float('-inf')
    lowest_salary = float('inf')
    highest_salary_employee = ''
    lowest_salary_employee = ''
    
    with open(filename, mode='r') as file:
        csv_reader = csv.DictReader(file)
        for row in csv_reader:
            total_employees += 1
            salary = float(row['Salary'])
            total_salary += salary
            
            department_counts[row['Department']] += 1
            position_counts[row['Position']] += 1
            
            if salary > highest_salary:
                highest_salary = salary
                highest_salary_employee = row['Name']
            
            if salary < lowest_salary:
                lowest_salary = salary
                lowest_salary_employee = row['Name']
    
    average_salary = total_salary / total_employees if total_employees > 0 else 0
    
    print(f"Total number of employees: {total_employees}")
    print(f"Average salary of all employees: ${average_salary:.2f}")
    print("Number of employees in each department:")
    for department, count in department_counts.items():
        print(f"  {department}: {count}")
    print("Number of employees in each position:")
    for position, count in position_counts.items():
        print(f"  {position}: {count}")
    print(f"Highest salary: ${highest_salary:.2f} (Employee: {highest_salary_employee})")
    print(f"Lowest salary: ${lowest_salary:.2f} (Employee: {lowest_salary_employee})")

# Example usage:
# extract_employee_info('employees.csv')

You are given n points on the plane, no three of which are collinear. The i-th point initially has a label a_i, in such a way that the labels a_1, a_2, ..., a_n form a permutation of 1, 2, ..., n.

You are allowed to modify the labels through the following operation:

  1. Choose two distinct integers i and j between 1 and n. 
  2. Swap the labels of points i and j, and finally 
  3. Draw the segment between points i and j. 



A sequence of operations is valid if after applying all of the operations in the sequence in order, the k-th point ends up having the label k for all k between 1 and n inclusive, and the drawn segments don't intersect each other internally. Formally, if two of the segments intersect, then they must do so at a common endpoint of both segments.

In particular, all drawn segments must be distinct.

Find any valid sequence of operations, or say that none exist.
Input

The first line contains an integer n (3 ≤ n ≤ 2000) — the number of points.

The i-th of the following n lines contains three integers x_i, y_i, a_i (-10^6 ≤ x_i, y_i ≤ 10^6, 1 ≤ a_i ≤ n), representing that the i-th point has coordinates (x_i, y_i) and initially has label a_i.

It is guaranteed that all points are distinct, no three points are collinear, and the labels a_1, a_2, ..., a_n form a permutation of 1, 2, ..., n.

Output

If it is impossible to perform a valid sequence of operations, print -1.

Otherwise, print an integer k (0 ≤ k ≤ (n(n - 1))/(2)) — the number of operations to perform, followed by k lines, each containing two integers i and j (1 ≤ i, j ≤ n, i≠ j) — the indices of the points chosen for the operation.

Note that you are not required to minimize or maximize the value of k.

If there are multiple possible answers, you may print any of them.

5 2 1

5

Revision 2622 - Directory Listing
Modified Sun Aug 15 22:46:57 2004 UTC (15 years, 6 months ago) by dpvc
```Created the first draft of the Parser's function answer checker.  Some
work still needs to be done, in particular, type mismatches are not
being reported, and lists are being processed only as ordered lists
with no partial credit.

This doesn't implement everything from fun_cmp.  In particular, there
is no support for parameter matching at the moment.  This will have to
be added, but I'm not quite sure how to do it when the function's
return value is not a real number (e.g., an interval).

On the other hand, this checker is more forgiving about domain errors:
if tries a point where the professor's function is not defined, it
ignores that one and looks for another (until it decides it can't find
one).  Once it has these points, it tries them on the student's
answer, and if the student's function is undefined, then the student
answer is wrong (since it is not defined somewhere that the
professor's funciton is).  This avoids the "Error evaluating student
function at ..." messages, which just confused most students anyway.
```

1. Compute the average of the values in the "Age" column.
2. Determine the minimum and maximum values in the "Salary" column.
3. Output the results to a new CSV file named `summary.csv` with the columns "Average Age" and "Salary Range (min-max)".

Your program should handle potential errors such as the file not being found or the specified columns not existing in the CSV. If an error occurs, print an appropriate error message.

**Sample Input (data.csv):**
```
Age,Salary
25,50000
30,60000
35,70000
```

**Sample Output (summary.csv):**
```
Average Age,Salary Range (min-max)
26.666666666666668,50000-70000
```

```python
import csv

def read_csv(file_path):
    """
    Reads a CSV file and returns its contents as a list of dictionaries.
    Handles FileNotFoundError and other exceptions.
    """
    try:
        with open(file_path, newline='') as csvfile:
            reader = csv.DictReader(csvfile)
            data = list(reader)
        return data
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file {file_path} was not found.")
        return None
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred: {e}")
        return None

def calculate_average(data, column):
    """
    Calculates the average of the values in the specified column.
    Handles missing columns and non-numeric data.
    """
    if not data or column not in data[0]:
        print(f"Error: The column {column} does not exist in the data.")
        return None
    
    try:
        total = sum(float(row[column]) for row in data)
        average = total / len(data)
        return average
    except ValueError:
        print(f"Error: Non-numeric data found in the column {column}.")
        return None

def find_min_max(data, column):
    """
    Finds the minimum and maximum values in the specified column.
    Handles missing columns and non-numeric data.
    """
    if not data or column not in data[0]:
        print(f"Error: The column {column} does not exist in the data.")
        return None
    
    try:
        values = [float(row[column]) for row in data]
        minimum = min(values)
        maximum = max(values)
        return minimum, maximum
    except ValueError:
        print(f"Error: Non-numeric data found in the column {column}.")
        return None

def write_summary_csv(file_path, average_age, salary_range):
    """
    Writes the average age and salary range to a new CSV file.
    Handles exceptions during file writing.
    """
    try:
        with open(file_path, 'w', newline='') as csvfile:
            fieldnames = ['Average Age', 'Salary Range (min-max)']
            writer = csv.DictWriter(csvfile, fieldnames=fieldnames)
            writer.writeheader()
            writer.writerow({
                'Average Age': average_age,
                'Salary Range (min-max)': f"{salary_range[0]}-{salary_range[1]}"
            })
        print("Summary written to summary.csv")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"An error occurred while writing to the file {file_path}: {e}")

def main():
    data = read_csv('data.csv')
    if data:
        average_age = calculate_average(data, 'Age')
        salary_range = find_min_max(data, 'Salary')
        if average_age is not None and salary_range is not None:
            write_summary_csv('summary.csv', average_age, salary_range)

if __name__ == '__main__':
    main()
```<#sep#>text{trace}(A) + \text{trace}(D).
   \]
   Since the trace of a matrix is the sum of its eigenvalues, we have:
   \[
   \lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \text{(sum of other eigenvalues of } M) = \text{trace}(A) + \text{trace}(D).
   \]

2. **Eigenvalue Interlacing**:
   By the Cauchy interlacing theorem, the eigenvalues of the submatrices \(A\) and \(D\) interlace with the eigenvalues of \(M\). Specifically:
   \[
   \mu_1 \leq \lambda_1 \quad \text{and} \quad \mu_2 \leq \lambda_1.
   \]
   This implies that \(\lambda_1\) is at least as large as the largest eigenvalues of \(A\) and \(D\).

3. **Expression Analysis**:
   Consider the expression \(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 - \mu_1 - \mu_2\). We need to determine if this expression can be positive, zero, or negative, and find its maximum value.

4. **Example Analysis**:
   - **2x2 Matrix Case**: Suppose \(M\) is a 2x2 matrix:
     \[
     M = \begin{pmatrix} a & b \\ b & d \end{pmatrix}.
     \]
     The eigenvalues of \(M\) are:
     \[
     \lambda_{1,2} = \frac{a + d}{2} \pm \sqrt{\left(\frac{a - d}{2}\right)^2 + b^2}.
     \]
     The largest eigenvalue \(\lambda_1\) is:
     \[
     \lambda_1 = \frac{a + d}{2} + \sqrt{\left(\frac{a - d}{2}\right)^2 + b^2},
     \]
     and the smallest eigenvalue \(\lambda_2\) is:
     \[
     \lambda_2 = \frac{a + d}{2} - \sqrt{\left(\frac{a - d}{2}\right)^2 + b^2}.
     \]
     The sum \(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2\) is:
     \[
     \lambda_1 + \lambda_2 = a + d.
     \]
     The largest eigenvalues of \(A\) and \(D\) are \(a\) and \(d\) respectively, so:
     \[
     \mu_1 + \mu_2 = a + d.
     \]
     Therefore, the expression \(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 - \mu_1 - \mu_2\) is:
     \[
     \lambda_1 + \lambda_2 - \mu_1 - \mu_2 = (a + d) - (a + d) = 0.
     \]

   - **General Case**: For larger matrices, the same principle applies. The sum of the largest and smallest eigenvalues of \(M\) is constrained by the trace of \(M\), which is the sum of the traces of \(A\) and \(D\). The largest eigenvalues of \(A\) and \(D\) (\(\mu_1\) and \(\mu_2\)) are less than or equal to the largest eigenvalue of \(M\). Thus, the expression \(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 - \mu_1 - \mu_2\) cannot exceed zero.

5. **Conclusion**:
   From the analysis of both specific and general cases, we conclude that the maximum possible value of \(\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 - \mu_1 - \mu_2\) is \(\boxed{0}\).<#sep#>.

Continued from #5584. I'm encountering this in a test case that needs both S3 and DynamoDB mocked. Here's the smallest repro I can think of:

```python
$ python -Wall
Python 3.9.12 (main, Oct  8 2022, 00:52:50) 
[Clang 14.0.0 (clang-1400.0.29.102)] on darwin
Type "help", "copyright", "credits" or "license" for more information.
>>> import gc
>>> from moto.s3.models import S3Backend
>>> from moto.dynamodb.models import DynamoDBBackend
>>> from moto import __version__
>>> __version__
'4.1.2'
>>> s3 = S3Backend('us-west-1', '1234')
>>> db = DynamoDBBackend('us-west-1', '1234')
>>> s3.create_bucket('my-bucket','us-west-1')
<moto.s3.models.FakeBucket object at 0x104d57be0>
>>> s3.put_object('my-bucket','my-key', 'y')
<moto.s3.models.FakeKey object at 0x104d57c40>
>>> db.reset()
>>> s3.reset()
>>> gc.collect()
/Users/hannes/.venv/lib/python3.9/site-packages/moto/s3/models.py:349: ResourceWarning: S3 key was not disposed of in time
  warnings.warn("S3 key was not disposed of in time", ResourceWarning)
29269
>>> 
```

The issue seems to be that `BaseBackend.reset()` — via `._reset_model_refs()` — resets each model's `.instances` class attribute to an empty list so that when `S3Backend.reset()` attempts to properly dispose the `FakeKey` instances, that list in `FakeKey.instances` is already empty, and the key leaks. 

Maybe `_reset_model_refs()` should call `dispose()` on every model instance before resetting `.instances` to `[]`. That would mean that the `dispose()` protocol becomes part of every model.  Then again, it seems odd that one backend resets the models of another one. Maybe it should only reset its own models.

answers, and incorrect endpoints and endpoint types in intervals.

Revision 2593 - Directory Listing
Modified Thu Aug 12 16:48:52 2004 UTC (15 years, 6 months ago) by dpvc
```This is a preliminary attempt at adding answer checkers that use the
parser.  If you have a parser object (like a formula or a vector), you
can use the ->cmp method to get an answer checker appropriate for the
object.  For example:

Context('Vector');
\$v = Vector(1,2,3);
#  print text of problem
ANS(\$v->cmp);

would produce an answer checker that matched against the vector <1,2,3>.
Most of the parameters are set in the Context, but additional ones can
be passed to the checker (this still needs work).

See the sample problems for more examples.

The checkers for Reals, Complexes, Points, Vectors, Matrices and
Intervals work pretty well.  The ones for List and Union objects need
more work, as they only do an ORDERED check at the moment (the student
answers must be in the same order).  This needs to be fixed.
The checker for Formula objects (which corresponds to fun_cmp) still
needs to be written.
```

Revision 2579 - Directory Listing
Modified Mon Aug 9 21:38:01 2004 UTC (15 years, 6 months ago) by dpvc
```Significant update to new parser.

New features include:

Better control over format of vector output (you can now
specify ijk-format rather than <...> format)

"Fuzzy" reals, where the relations like == return true when the
two values are "close enough".  (This is controlable using
parameters similar to those used in NUM_CMP).

The fuzzy reals are now used in vectors/points/matrices/complexes/intervals
and so on so that their relations will also be fuzzy.  E.g.,
(1E-13,2) == (0,3) will be true, and norm(Vector(0,1E-13)) will
equal 0.

The two main portions of the parser (the Parser and Value packages)
now share a common context object for configuration purposes.
```

Sort log by:<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class in Python that simulates basic banking operations. The class should support the following functionalities:

1. **Initialization**: Create a bank account with an account holder's name and an optional initial deposit.
2. **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. Raise a `ValueError` if the deposit amount is non-positive.
3. **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. Raise a `ValueError` if the withdrawal amount is non-positive or if there are insufficient funds.
4. **Show Balance**: Return a string representing the current balance of the account.
5. **Transaction History**: Return a string listing all transactions (deposits and withdrawals) with their respective amounts and timestamps.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The `BankAccount` class will be instantiated with a name and an optional initial deposit.
- The `deposit` and `withdraw` methods will be called with positive numeric values.
- The `show_balance` method will return a formatted string with the account balance.
- The `show_transaction_history` method will return a formatted string with all transactions.

**Sample Usage**:
```python
account = BankAccount("John Doe", 100)
account.deposit(50)
account.withdraw(30)
print(account.show_balance())  # Output: Balance for John Doe: $120.00
print(account.show_transaction_history())
# Output:
# Transaction History:
# Deposit of $50.00 on 2023-10-01 12:00:00
# Withdrawal of $30.00 on 2023-10-01 12:01:00
```

```python
from datetime import datetime

class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, account_holder, initial_deposit=0):
        """
        Initialize a new bank account with an account holder's name and an optional initial deposit.
        
        :param account_holder: Name of the account holder.
        :param initial_deposit: Initial deposit amount (default is 0).
        """
        self.account_holder = account_holder
        self.balance = initial_deposit
        self.transactions = []

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Deposit a specified amount into the account.
        
        :param amount: Amount to deposit.
        :raises ValueError: If the deposit amount is non-positive.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Deposit amount must be positive.")
        self.balance += amount
        self.transactions.append(("deposit", amount, datetime.now()))

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Withdraw a specified amount from the account.
        
        :param amount: Amount to withdraw.
        :raises ValueError: If the withdrawal amount is non-positive or if there are insufficient funds.
        """
        if amount <= 0:
            raise ValueError("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")
        if amount > self.balance:
            raise ValueError("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        self.balance -= amount
        self.transactions.append(("withdrawal", amount, datetime.now()))

    def show_balance(self):
        """
        Return a string representing the current balance of the account.
        
        :return: Formatted string with the account balance.
        """
        return f"Balance for {self.account_holder}: ${self.balance:.2f}"

    def show_transaction_history(self):
        """
        Return a string listing all transactions with their respective amounts and timestamps.
        
        :return: Formatted string with all transactions.
        """
        if not self.transactions:
            return "No transactions made yet."
        
        history = ["Transaction History:"]
        for transaction in self.transactions:
            transaction_type, amount, timestamp = transaction
            formatted_time = timestamp.strftime("%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S")
            history.append(f"{transaction_type.capitalize()} of ${amount:.2f} on {formatted_time}")
        return "\n".join(history)

Theorem 17.2 (The sufficient conditions of differentiability) The Cauchy-Riemann conditions (17.4) are also sufficient for the differentiability of f (z) provided the functions u (x, y) and υ ( x , y ) are totally differentiable (all partial derivatives exist) at the considered point. In the field of complex analysis in mathematics, the Cauchy–Riemann equations, named after Augustin Ca<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a class `OnlineStore` that manages an inventory of items and a shopping cart. The class should support the following operations:

1. `add_item(item_name, price)`: Adds an item with a specified price to the store. If the item already exists, update its price.
2. `remove_item(item_name)`: Removes an item from the store if it exists.
3. `list_items()`: Lists all items available in the store along with their prices.
4. `add_to_cart(item_name, quantity)`: Adds a specified quantity of an item to the shopping cart. If the item is not available in the store, do nothing.
5. `remove_from_cart(item_name, quantity)`: Removes a specified quantity of an item from the shopping cart. If the quantity to remove is greater than or equal to the quantity in the cart, remove the item entirely.
6. `cart_total()`: Returns the total cost of the items in the shopping cart.
7. `list_cart()`: Lists all items in the shopping cart along with their quantities and individual costs, and the total cost of the cart.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
store = OnlineStore()
store.add_item("apple", 0.99)
store.add_item("banana", 0.59)
store.list_items()
store.add_to_cart("apple", 3)
store.add_to_cart("banana", 5)
store.list_cart()
store.remove_from_cart("apple", 1)
store.list_cart()
print(f"Final cart total: ${store.cart_total():.2f}")
```

**Expected Output:**
```
Item 'apple' added with price $0.99.
Item 'banana' added with price $0.59.
Items available in the store:
- apple: $0.99
- banana: $0.59
Added 3 of 'apple' to the cart.
Added 5 of 'banana' to the cart.
Items in your shopping cart:
- apple: 3 x $0.99 = $2.97
- banana: 5 x $0.59 = $2.95
Total cost: $5.92
Removed 1 of 'apple' from the cart.
Items in your shopping cart:
- apple: 2 x $0.99 = $1.98
- banana: 5 x $0.59 = $2.95
Total cost: $4.93
Final cart total: $4.93
```

```python
class OnlineStore:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize the store with empty item and cart dictionaries."""
        self.items = {}  # Dictionary to store items and their prices
        self.cart = {}   # Dictionary to store items in the shopping cart and their quantities

    def add_item(self, item_name, price):
        """Add an item with its price to the store. Update price if item already exists."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            print(f"Item '{item_name}' already exists. Updating the price.")
        self.items[item_name] = price
        print(f"Item '{item_name}' added with price ${price:.2f}.")

    def remove_item(self, item_name):
        """Remove an item from the store if it exists."""
        if item_name in self.items:
            del self.items[item_name]
            print(f"Item '{item_name}' removed.")
        else:
            print(f"Item '{item_name}' not found in the store.")

    def list_items(self):
        """List all items available in the store."""
        if not self.items:
            print("No items in the store.")
        else:
            print("Items available in the store:")
            for item, price in self.items.items():
                print(f"- {item}: ${price:.2f}")

    def add_to_cart(self, item_name, quantity=1):
        """Add a quantity of an item to the shopping cart if the item is available in the store."""
        if item_name not in self.items:
            print(f"Item '{item_name}' not available in the store.")
            return

        if item_name in self.cart:
            self.cart[item_name] += quantity
        else:
            self.cart[item_name] = quantity

        print(f"Added {quantity} of '{item_name}' to the cart.")

    def remove_from_cart(self, item_name, quantity=1):
        """Remove a quantity of an item from the shopping cart."""
        if item_name not in self.cart:
            print(f"Item '{item_name}' not found in the cart.")
            return

        if self.cart[item_name] <= quantity:
            del self.cart[item_name]
            print(f"Removed all of '{item_name}' from the cart.")
        else:
            self.cart[item_name] -= quantity
            print(f"Removed {quantity} of '{item_name}' from the cart.")

    def cart_total(self):
        """Calculate and return the total cost of the items in the shopping cart."""
        total = sum(self.items[item] * qty for item, qty in self.cart.items())
        return total

    def list_cart(self):
        """List all items in the shopping cart along with their quantities and individual costs, and the total cost."""
        if not self.cart:
            print("Your shopping cart is empty.")
        else:
            print("Items in your shopping cart:")
            for item, quantity in self.cart.items():
                print(f"- {item}: {quantity} x ${self.items[item]:.2f} = ${self.items[item] * quantity:.2f}")
            print(f"Total cost: ${self.cart_total():.2f}")
```<#sep#> Wait, perhaps parametrize points on the sides.

Let’s parametrize the sides:

Side AB: from A(0,0) to B(1,0). A point on AB can be represented as (t,0), 0 ≤ t ≤1.

Side BC: from B(1,0) to C(0,1). A point on BC can be represented as (1 - t, t), 0 ≤ t ≤1.

Side AC: from A(0,0) to C(0,1). A point on AC is (0,t), 0 ≤ t ≤1.

But the problem is that the quadrilateral can have its four vertices on the three sides. Let me assume that two points are on BC, one on AB, and one on AC. Let’s denote them as P₁ and P₂ on BC, P₃ on AB, and P₄ on AC.

So, let's parameterize:

Let P₁ be (1 - t, t) for some t between 0 and 1.

Similarly, P₂ be (1 - s, s) for some s between 0 and 1.

P₃ on AB is (u, 0) for u between 0 and 1.

P₄ on AC is (0, v) for v between 0 and 1.

So now we have four parameters: t, s, u, v. The challenge is to express the areas A₁, A₂, A₃, A₄ in terms of t, s, u, v and then maximize the sum.

But this might be complicated. Alternatively, maybe consider symmetry. Suppose that the quadrilateral is symmetric with respect to the median of the triangle. For example, if the points are placed such that t = s and u = v. Maybe this symmetry could lead to a maximum.

Alternatively, note that the problem is about maximum total area. Each of the four triangles must be as large as possible. However, when you place points closer to the vertices, the triangles formed might be larger.

Wait, but if you place P₃ near B and P₄ near C, then triangle P₂P₃P₄ might be large. Similarly, triangle P₁P₂P₃ and others.

Alternatively, perhaps the maximum sum occurs when all four triangles are as large as possible. But how?

Alternatively, perhaps the maximum sum is 1, the area of ABC itself, but the problem states that the sum can be larger because overlapping areas are counted multiple times.

Wait, let me consider an extreme case. Suppose that the quadrilateral is such that all four triangles cover the entire area of ABC multiple times. For example, if three of the triangles each have area 1, their sum would be 3, but that seems impossible because the entire triangle is only area 1, and overlapping regions can't be more than the area.

But in reality, each triangle can't cover the entire ABC unless all three vertices of ABC are included. But the quadrilateral's vertices are on the sides, not necessarily at the vertices.

Wait, suppose three of the four points are at the vertices of ABC. For example, if P₁ is at B, P₂ at C, P₃ at A, and P₄ somewhere on AB. Then triangle P₁P₂P₃ would be triangle ABC, area 1. Then triangle P₁P₂P₄ would be triangle BCP₄, which is part of ABC, so area less than 1. Similarly, triangle P₁P₃P₄ is triangle ABP₄, area less than 1. Triangle P₂P₃P₄ is triangle ACP₄, also area less than 1. Then the total sum would be 1 plus three areas each less than 1, so maybe around 1 + something. But is this the maximum?

Wait, but if three of the quadrilateral's vertices are at the corners, then the fourth can be placed to maximize the sum.

But wait, but the problem states that all four vertices lie on the sides of ABC. The vertices of ABC are part of the sides, so placing a point at a vertex is allowed, since vertices are on the sides.

So, for instance, if P₁ is at B, P₂ at C, P₃ at A, and P₄ at some point on side AB. Then:

- A₁ = area of P₁P₂P₃ = area of ABC = 1.

- A₂ = area of P₁P₂P₄ = area of BCP₄. Since P₄ is on AB, say at (t, 0), so coordinates of P₄ are (t,0). Then B is (1,0), C is (0,1). So triangle BCP₄ has vertices (1,0), (0,1), (t,0). The area can be calculated as |(1*(1 - 0) + 0*(0 - 0) + t*(0 - 1))/2| = |(1 - t)/2|. Since t is between 0 and 1, the area is (1 - t)/2.

Similarly, A₃ = area of P₁P₃P₄ = area of ABP₄. AB is from (1,0) to A(0,0), so triangle ABP₄ is actually a line if P₄ is on AB, so the area is zero? Wait, P₁ is B(1,0), P₃ is A(0,0), and P₄ is on AB. So the triangle P₁P₃P₄ is B, A, P₄. But since P₄ is on AB, these three points are colinear, right? So the area would be zero. Similarly, A₄ is area of P₂P₃P₄. P₂ is C(0,1), P₃ is A(0,0), P₄ is (t,0). So triangle CAP₄. The area is |(0*(0 - 0) + 0*(0 - 1) + t*(1 - 0))/2| = |t/2|. So the total sum A₁ + A₂ + A₃ + A₄ = 1 + (1 - t)/2 + 0 + t/2 = 1 + 1/2 = 1.5. So the sum is 1.5.

But wait, that's 1.5, which is 3/2. Maybe that's the maximum. But let's check if this is the case.

Alternatively, if we place three vertices at the corners, and the fourth somewhere else. Suppose P₁ at A, P₂ at B, P₃ at C, and P₄ on BC. Then:

- A₁ = area of ABP₃ = area of ABC = 1.

- A₂ = area of ABP₄. P₄ is on BC, so triangle ABP₄. Coordinates: A(0,0), B(1,0), P₄ is (1 - t, t). The area would be 1/2 * base * height. The base AB is 1, height is the y-coordinate of P₄, which is t. So area is t/2.

- A₃ = area of AP₃P₄. Points A(0,0), C(0,1), P₄(1 - t, t). The area is 1/2 * | (0*(1 - t - 0) + 0*(t - 0) + (1 - t)*(0 - 1) ) | = 1/2 * | - (1 - t) | = (1 - t)/2.

- A₄ = area of BP₃P₄. Points B(1,0), C(0,1), P₄(1 - t, t). This is the same as triangle BCP₄, which is area (1 - t)/2 as before.

So sum A₁ + A₂ + A₃ + A₄ = 1 + t/2 + (1 - t)/2 + (1 - t)/2 = 1 + [t/2 + (1 - t)/2 + (1 - t)/2] = 1 + [ (t + 1 - t + 1 - t)/2 ] = 1 + (2 - t)/2 = 1 + 1 - t/2 = 2 - t/2.

To maximize this sum, we need to minimize t. Since t ≥ 0, the maximum sum is achieved when t = 0, giving 2 - 0 = 2. But wait, if t = 0, then P₄ is at (1,0), which is point B. But then P₄ coincides with B, so quadrilateral P<#sep#> a node u to the root, you can swap all along the path. So, to place things correctly, perhaps we can perform this in a BFS or traversal order.

Alternatively, here's an algorithm:

1. Choose a non-crossing spanning tree. Let’s say choose the convex hull.

Wait, but how to choose a non-crossing spanning tree for given points? 

Well, the convex hull can be part of it. More precisely, any spanning tree can be made non-crossing by building on the convex hull structure. For instance, a BFS/DFS that adds edges in a non-crossing way.

Alternatively, any set of points can have a non-crossing spanning tree (since they can always be triangulated into a planar graph, but a tree is even simpler).

Wait, actually any set of point can form a non-crossing spanning tree: for example, build the tree incrementally by always connecting new nodes on the convex hull in a way that edges don't cross. 

So first, construct the convex hull edges. Then, connect interior points to the hull in a way that the edges don't cross. It's a known that for a planar point set, this is possible.

So, constructing the non-crossing tree is possible.

Once the non-crossing spanning tree is built, the next step is to perform swaps along this tree to reach the desired permutation.

One method for this might be inspired by the "greedy" permutation decomposition along a routing tree. 

A common method is as follows: 

- Start with the root node (e.g., the point which is fixed in the permutation or arbitrary) and for each other node, traverse the shortest path from current position to its destination, swapping elements along the way until it's in place. This method's path moves each element along the path, swapping until it gets to the desired node.

So, let's see. 

Suppose we have a current element x at position A which should go to position B. The path from A to B in the tree is A-P1-P2-...-Pn-B. 

Then, the path's edge sequence is (A-P1), (P1-P2), etc. 

To move x from A to B, we can do the following swaps:

Swap A with P1. This brings x to P1.

Then swap P1 with P2 → x moves to P2.

Continue until x reaches B. 

Each of these steps corresponds to moving x along the path. 

This would take O(l) steps (the length of the path), and the edges are non-crossing since the tree's edges are all non-crossing. 

Therefore, for all elements, doing this would move each element correctly. However, the problem is that each swap may also affect other elements along the path.

For example, when swapping A-P1 moves x from A to P1, the element previously in P1 is moved to A. This may be another element which was being sent elsewhere, so this could interfere.

Therefore, the naive approach of moving element by element won't directly work because swapping affects both nodes.

 Thus, this method could 'back up' previous progress unless we handle the swaps in a specific way.

Another common approach in such permutation routing is known as the "kill one bird at a time", i.e., permuting elements to their positions one by one, or using reverse accumulating approach. 

Alternatively, we can traverse the tree in a manner such that each step is moving an element through the tree while letting others be 'swept' along, but ensuring that each swap doesn't disrupt the later steps. 

Hmm, perhaps the method presented in this paper: "Parallel Permutation by Edge Swapping" or something, but I can't recall. Alternatively, here's an idea based on bubble sort in trees:

Let's imagine that the tree is rooted, and we want to process the nodes in post-order (children first). 

This way, when processing a subtree, the root of the subtree can bubble the element it needs directly towards itself, then process the parent. 

This could ensure that moving elements to their parents first so the path remains intact.

Wait, let me think. Let's see a root R. Each node has to move its "desired element" to its position. 

First, process the leaves first. 

Suppose that each node along a path except the parent has the correct element. When moving an element to its correct spot via swapping along the edges, we might need to swap the current element with the next one. 

Alternatively, here's an algorithm used for sorting permutations on trees using transpositions:

 The "bubble sort on a tree" approach, where we chase an element along its path to the target, swapping step by step. Here is a standard procedure:

For each element x that needs to get to its destination:

Let’s find its current position u, its destination v, then move x along the unique path from u to v in the tree via swaps along the edges of the tree. 

However, doing this directly can cause issues because moving along the path might push other elements. But since the tree is the only path, perhaps we can handle it.

Wait, perhaps the following works: 

We can process the nodes in the reverse order of the traversal (post-order). 

Starting with leaves (the elements whose path is starting at them and goes through other nodes), moving them to their destinations first.

1. Find the spanning tree (non-crossing).

2. Root the tree at any node (say, the root R).

3. Traverse the tree in post-order, moving each node's element to its destination, ensuring along the way that once a deeper node is processed, its element is in the correct place.

Let me think of this again with an example.

Suppose the tree is a chain 1-2-3-4. Let's root at 1. 

Suppose each node's desired label is the node's number itself. Suppose that currently the labels are: 

Label 2 is at 1, label1 is at 4, etc. (making a cycle).

Processing in post-order (leaves first):

- Process node4 first. The destination for the label at 4 is its own position. So if the current label at node4 is already correct, do nothing. But if not, we need to move it to its node.

For each node v in post-order:

- The current label at v is L.

So, for node v's label, if the current label at v is not D (the node v's destination's label), then we need to find where the correct label D is.

We can build the spanning tree. Then the process of routing can be done as follows:

For each node except the root, in some order, ensure that its label reaches it. To do this, move the label to the current node's parent, then swap with the parent's element?

No, perhaps that's confusing.

Alternatively, the following article mentions that permutations can be routed along a tree without crossings by using specific path decompositions. Perhaps the standard routing route is as follows.

Here's an algorithm that may work:

- We need to move the desired label of node v, which is initially in position u, to node v.

- We move the element along the path from u to v in the tree, one step at a time.

- For each edge in the path, swap along that edge until the label reaches its destination.

Each of these swaps will be along the edges of the non-crossing spanning tree, so the segments won't cross.

The problem with this approach is that during the process of moving the element along its path, you may displace other elements not yet processed, causing them to move further away. 

However, perhaps if you process elements in an order such that whenever you're moving an element along its path towards its destination, any elements you have already processed are in their correct places, so their displacement won't be a problem.

A possible way to handle this is to use an in-order traversal that ensures that the paths are processed from leaves inward.

- Start with the root node, and process all children first.

- Once done, process the root.

Assume we have a root, and the tree is rooted as such. 

Suppose you process nodes in post-order. 

Processing node v in post-order means processing all children first.

Proved and its ramifications discussed derivative exists<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Book` class to manage books in a library system. The class should encapsulate the following attributes:
- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the International Standard Book Number of the book.
- `published_year`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
- `available`: A boolean indicating whether the book is available for checkout, defaulting to `True`.

Your class should provide the following methods:
- `checkout`: A method that sets the `available` attribute to `False`.
- `return_book`: A method that sets the `available` attribute to `True`.
- `__str__`: A method that returns a string representation of the book in the format: `"Title: {title}, Author: {author}, ISBN: {isbn}, Published Year: {published_year}, Available: {available}"`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", "9780451524935", 1949)
print(book)  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935, Published Year: 1949, Available: True
book.checkout()
print(book)  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935, Published Year: 1949, Available: False
book.return_book()
print(book)  # Output: Title: 1984, Author: George Orwell, ISBN: 9780451524935, Published Year: 1949, Available: True
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, isbn, published_year, available=True):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance.

        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param isbn: The International Standard Book Number of the book.
        :param published_year: The year the book was published.
        :param available: A boolean indicating whether the book is available for checkout.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.isbn = isbn
        self.published_year = published_year
        self.available = available

    def checkout(self):
        """
        Sets the book's availability to False, indicating it is checked out.
        """
        self.available = False

    def return_book(self):
        """
        Sets the book's availability to True, indicating it is returned.
        """
        self.available = True

    def __str__(self):
        """
        Returns a string representation of the book.

        :return: A string in the format "Title: {title}, Author: {author}, ISBN: {isbn}, Published Year: {published_year}, Available: {available}".
        """
        return (f"Title: {self.title}, Author: {self.author}, "
                f"ISBN: {self.isbn}, Published Year: {self.published_year}, "
                f"Available: {self.available}")

Definition ( complex differentiability of a function complex exponents ; functions of a complex variable its ramifications.!<#sep#>You are given a text file. Your task is to write a Python function `count_words(filename: str) -> dict` that reads the file and returns a dictionary with unique words as keys and their counts as values. The function should ignore common stop words such as "the", "and", "is", etc. Assume the file contains only ASCII characters.

**Sample Input:**
File `example.txt` contains:
```
The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. The dog was not amused.
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
{'quick': 1, 'brown': 1, 'fox': 1, 'jumps': 1, 'over': 1, 'lazy': 1, 'dog': 1, 'was': 1, 'not': 1, 'amused': 1}
```

```python
import string

def count_words(filename: str) -> dict:
    """
    Reads a text file and returns a dictionary with unique words as keys and their counts as values.
    Ignores common stop words.
    
    :param filename: The name of the file to read.
    :return: A dictionary with word counts.
    """
    stop_words = set([
        "the", "and", "is", "in", "to", "of", "a", "an", "on", "at", "that", "for", "with", "as", "by", "from", "up", "about", 
        "into", "over", "after", "before", "between", "under", "through", "during", "without", "against", "among", "throughout", 
        "despite", "across", "towards", "upon", "versus", "via", "down", "out", "off", "over", "further", "then", "once", 
        "here", "there", "when", "where", "why", "how", "all", "any", "both", "each", "few", "more", "most", "other", "some", 
        "such", "no", "nor", "not", "only", "own", "same", "so", "than", "too", "very", "s", "t", "can", "will", "just", "don", 
        "should", "now", "d", "ll", "m", "o", "re", "ve", "y", "ain", "aren", "couldn", "didn", "doesn", "hadn", "hasn", "haven", 
        "isn", "ma", "mightn", "mustn", "needn", "shan", "shouldn", "wasn", "weren", "won", "wouldn", "i", "me", "my", "myself", 
        "we", "our", "ours", "ourselves", "you", "your", "yours", "yourself", "yourselves", "he", "him", "his", "himself", "she", 
        "her", "hers", "herself", "it", "its", "itself", "they", "them", "their", "theirs", "themselves"
    ])
    
    word_count = {}
    
    try:
        with open(filename, "r", encoding="utf-8") as file:
            for line in file:
                words = line.lower().translate(str.maketrans('', '', string.punctuation)).split()
                for word in words:
                    if word in stop_words:
                        continue
                    if word in word_count:
                        word_count[word] += 1
                    else:
                        word_count[word] = 1
    except FileNotFoundError:
        print(f"Error: The file {filename} does not exist.")
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"Error: An unexpected error occurred - {e}")
    
    return word_count

Let’s consider triangle ABC with vertices A(0,0), B(1,0), C(0,1), area 0.5. But since the problem states area 1, we can later scale up the areas.

Let’s parametrize the points:

Let P₁ and P₂ be on BC. Let’s parameterize BC from B(1,0) to C(0,1). Let P₁ divide BC in the ratio t:(1-t), so coordinates (1 - t, t). Similarly, P₂ divides BC in the ratio s:(1-s), coordinates (1 - s, s). Assume t < s so that P₁ is closer to B than P₂.

Point P₃ is on AB: coordinates (u, 0), where 0 ≤ u ≤1.

Point P₄ is on AC: coordinates (0, v), where 0 ≤ v ≤1.

Now, compute the areas of the four triangles.

First, triangle P₁P₂P₃. Coordinates:

P₁ = (1 - t, t)

P₂ = (1 - s, s)

P₃ = (u, 0)

The area can be computed using the determinant formula:

Area = ½ | (x₁(y₂ - y₃) + x₂(y₃ - y₁) + x₃(y₁ - y₂)) |

Plugging in the coordinates:

= ½ | (1 - t)(s - 0) + (1 - s)(0 - t) + u(t - s) |

= ½ | (1 - t)s - (1 - s)t + u(t - s) |

= ½ | s - ts - t + st + ut - us |

Simplify terms:

s - t + ut - us = s - t + u(t - s)

So Area = ½ |s - t + u(t - s)|

Similarly, triangle P₁P₂P₄:

Points P₁(1 - t, t), P₂(1 - s, s), P₄(0, v).

Area = ½ |(1 - t)(s - v) + (1 - s)(v - t) + 0(t - s)|

= ½ | (1 - t)(s - v) + (1 - s)(v - t) |

Expand:

= ½ | s - v - ts + tv + v - t - sv + st |

Simplify:

s - v - ts + tv + v - t - sv + st =

s - ts + st - t - sv + tv =

s(1 - t + t) - t(1) - v(s - t) =

s - t - v(s - t) =

(s - t)(1 - v)

So Area = ½ |(s - t)(1 - v)| = ½ (s - t)(1 - v) since s > t and 0 ≤ v ≤1.

Triangle P₁P₃P₄:

Points P₁(1 - t, t), P₃(u, 0), P₄(0, v).

Area = ½ | (1 - t)(0 - v) + u(v - t) + 0(t - 0) |

= ½ | -v(1 - t) + u(v - t) |

= ½ | -v + vt + uv - ut |

= ½ | -v + vt + uv - ut |

Factor:

= ½ | -v(1 - t) + u(v - t) |

Not sure if it simplifies further.

Triangle P₂P₃P₄:

Points P₂(1 - s, s), P₃(u, 0), P₄(0, v).

Area = ½ | (1 - s)(0 - v) + u(v - s) + 0(s - 0) |

= ½ | -v(1 - s) + u(v - s) |

= ½ | -v + vs + uv - us |

= ½ | -v + vs + uv - us |

So, steps to implement this in the program:

First, construct a non-crossing spanning tree.

- How to do this efficiently?

The non-crossing spanning tree can be constructed using a convex hull approach: first build the convex hull, and then pick edges connecting inward in a way that do not cross.

Alternatively, here’s a simple approach to build a non-crossing spanning tree:

Pick an arbitrary root node (e.g. first in list). Then, arrange all nodes in radial order around it. Then connect each node to its nearest node in the radial order, but I'm not sure. 

Alternatively, start with a convex hull. The convex hull contains the outermost points. The convex hull edges are part of a non-crossing set. The remaining points are inside the convex hull. We can connect each interior point to the convex hull by a non-crossing edge.

There is an algorithm called "monotonic chain" could be used for sorting the convex hull points. Alternatively, build a Delaunay triangulation but cutting down to only the edges needed for the tree. 

Wait perhaps the easiest way for code would be:

Build a minimum spanning tree, but such that the resulting tree is non-crossing. However, any edges have a cost of zero (1?), so the MST isn't helpful. 

Alternatively, the following method can construct a non-crossing spanning tree:

1. Compute the convex hull of the points.

2. Remove one point from the convex hull, say the rightmost vertex (or any arbitrary), creating a chain.<#sep#> parameters

•  » » » » » » » » 7 months ago, # ^ |   0 Well if you’re convinced<#sep#>You are given a list of tuples, where each tuple consists of two strings representing the start and end times of a meeting in a 24-hour format (HH:MM). Your task is to merge overlapping or contiguous meetings and return a list of merged meetings sorted by their start times. Each meeting in the output should be represented as a tuple of strings in the same format.

**Input:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a meeting.

**Output:**
- A list of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a merged meeting.

**Sample Input:**
```python
[("09:00", "10:30"), ("10:30", "11:00"), ("12:00", "13:00"), ("13:30", "14:00"), 
 ("14:00", "15:00"), ("15:00", "16:00"), ("10:00", "11:00"), ("11:00", "11:30"), 
 ("11:30", "12:00"), ("09:30", "10:00")]
```

**Sample Output:**
```python
[("09:00", "11:00"), ("12:00", "16:00")]
```

```python
def merge_meetings(meetings):
    """
    Merges overlapping or contiguous meetings and returns a list of merged meetings sorted by start times.
    
    :param meetings: List of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a meeting.
    :return: List of tuples, where each tuple contains two strings representing the start and end times of a merged meeting.
    """
    def time_to_minutes(time_str):
        """
        Converts time in 'HH:MM' format to minutes from the start of the day.
        
        :param time_str: String representing time in 'HH:MM' format.
        :return: Integer representing minutes from the start of the day.
        """
        hours, minutes = map(int, time_str.split(':'))
        return hours * 60 + minutes

    def minutes_to_time(minutes):
        """
        Converts minutes from the start of the day to time in 'HH:MM' format.
        
        :param minutes: Integer representing minutes from the start of the day.
        :return: String representing time in 'HH:MM' format.
        """
        hours = minutes // 60
        minutes = minutes % 60
        return f"{hours:02}:{minutes:02}"

    # Convert meeting times to minutes and sort them
    meetings_minutes = [(time_to_minutes(start), time_to_minutes(end)) for start, end in meetings]
    meetings_minutes.sort()

    # Merge meetings
    merged_meetings = []
    for current in meetings_minutes:
        if not merged_meetings or merged_meetings[-1][1] < current[0]:
            # No overlap, add the current meeting
            merged_meetings.append(current)
        else:
            # There is an overlap, merge the current meeting with the last one in merged_meetings
            last_meeting = merged_meetings.pop()
            merged_meetings.append((last_meeting[0], max(last_meeting[1], current[1])))

    # Convert merged meetings back to 'HH:MM' format
    return [(minutes_to_time(start), minutes_to_time(end)) for start, end in merged_meetings]
```<#sep#>ates the analysis.

Similarly, for A₄: -v + vs + uv - us = -v(1 - s) + u(v - s). If s > v, then v - s is negative. If s < v, then v - s is positive. It's complicated.

Alternatively, perhaps we can set u = v =0.5 and t = s =0.5. Then we can compute each area.

But this might not lead to the maximum. Alternatively, consider symmetry: set u = v and t = s. But since we have two points on BC, setting t = s would make them the same point. Not helpful.

Alternatively, suppose that all parameters are equal. Let's set t =0, s =1, u=1, v=0. Wait, but that puts points at the vertices. Then:

P₁ = B(1,0), P₂=C(0,1), P₃=B(1,0), P₄=A(0,0). This is a degenerate quadrilateral. Then A₁=Area of B,C,B=0, A₂=Area of B,C,A=1, A₃=Area of B,B,A=0, A₄=Area of C,B,A=1. Sum is 0+1+0+1=2. So again, sum is 2. But this is degenerate.

If we set parameters to be slightly different, say t=ε, s=1-ε, u=1-ε, v=ε, where ε is very small. Then the quadrilateral is nearly the triangle ABC with points near the vertices. Then A₁ and A₄ approach 1, and A₂ and A₃ approach 0. Sum approaches 1+0+0+1=2.

But this seems to suggest that the maximum is 2, but only in the degenerate case. If non-degenerate quadrilaterals are required, the maximum would be less than 2.

But the problem doesn't specify that the quadrilateral must be non-degenerate. It just says the vertices lie on the sides. So if degenerate quadrilaterals are allowed, the maximum is 2.

However, let me check if when the quadrilateral is degenerate, the areas are computed correctly. For example, if three points are colinear, the area of the triangle formed by them is zero. But in the case where three of the four points are the vertices of ABC, and the fourth is a vertex, then two of the triangles have area 1 and two have area zero, sum 2.

Since the problem doesn't specify the quadrilateral must be non-degenerate, and just mentions "quadrilateral" with vertices on the sides, which could include degenerate ones, then the maximum sum is 2.

But wait, in the initial example where we had three vertices at A, B, C, and the fourth at B, but in the problem statement, the original triangle has area 1, so sum is 2. So the answer should be 2. But to confirm, let's think of another case.

Suppose quadrilateral is A, B, C, D where D is a point on BC. Then the triangles would be:

A₁ = ABC, area 1.

A₂ = ABD, area depends on D. If D is near B, area approaches 0.

A₃ = ACD, area also approaches 1 as D approaches C.

A₄ = BCD, area approaches 0 as D approaches B.

So the sum would be 1 + 0 + 1 + 0 = 2 when D is at C, but then D coincides with C, making the quadrilateral degenerate.

But if D is at C, then the quadrilateral is A, B, C, C. Again, degenerate.

Alternatively, if D is near C, then A₁ = 1, A₂ = ABD which is small, A₃ = ACD nearly 1, A₄ = BCD nearly 0. So sum nearly 2. But the maximum is achieved in the limit as D approaches C.

Therefore, the conclusion is that the maximum sum A₁ + A₂ + A₃ + A₄ is 2, achieved when the quadrilateral is degenerate with three vertices coinciding with the vertices of triangle ABC and the fourth vertex coinciding with one of them.

Hence, the answer should be 2.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{2}
</think>Given a triangle \( \triangle ABC \) with an area of 1, we need to find the maximum possible value of the sum \( A_1 + A_2 + A_3 + A_4 \), where \( A_1, A_2, A_3, \) and \( A_4 \) are the areas of the triangles \( \triangle P_1P_2P_3 \), \( \triangle P_1P_2P_4 \), \( \triangle P_1P_3P_4 \), and \( \triangle P_2P_3P_4 \) respectively, formed by the vertices of a quadrilateral \( P_1P_2P_3P_4 \) inscribed in \( \triangle ABC \).

To maximize the sum \( A_1 + A_2 + A_3 + A_4 \), we consider a degenerate quadrilateral where some vertices coincide with the vertices of \( \triangle ABC \).

### Step-by-Step Solution:

1. **Degenerate Quadrilateral Configuration**:
   - Place three vertices of the quadrilateral at the vertices of \( \triangle ABC \). For example, let \( P_1 = A \), \( P_2 = B \), and \( P_3 = C \).
   - Place the fourth vertex \( P_4 \) at one of the vertices of \( \triangle ABC \). For instance, let \( P_4 = B \).

2. **Calculation of Areas**:
   - \( A_1 \) is the area of \( \triangle P_1P_2P_3 \), which is \( \triangle ABC \). Therefore, \( A_1 = 1 \).
   - \( A_2 \) is the area of \( \triangle P_1P_2P_4 \), which is \( \triangle ABB \). Since \( P_2 = B \) and \( P_4 = B \), this triangle is degenerate and has an area of 0. Therefore, \( A_2 = 0 \).
   - \( A_3 \) is the area of \( \triangle P_1P_3P_4 \), which is \( \triangle ACB \). This is the same as \( \triangle ABC \), so \( A_3 = 1 \).
   - \( A_4 \) is the area of \( \triangle P_2P_3P_4 \), which is \( \triangle BCB \). Since \( P_2 = B \) and \( P_4 = B \), this triangle is degenerate and has an area of 0. Therefore, \( A_4 = 0 \).

3. **Sum of Areas**:
   - The sum of the areas is \( A_1 + A_2 + A_3 + A_4 = 1 + 0 + 1 + 0 = 2 \).

### Conclusion:
The maximum possible value of the sum \( A_1 + A_2 + A_3 + A_4 \) is achieved when the quadrilateral is degenerate, with vertices coinciding with the vertices of \( \triangle ABC \).

Wait perhaps the easiest way for code would be:

Build a minimum spanning tree, but such that the resulting tree is non-crossing. However, any edges have a cost of zero (1?), so the MST isn't helpful. 

Alternatively, the following method can construct a non-crossing spanning tree:

1. Compute the convex hull of the points.

2. Remove one point from the convex hull, say the rightmost vertex (or any arbitrary), creating a chain. 

3. Connect the removed point to one of its adjacent vertices in the convex hull. 

4. For each interior point, connect it to its nearest convex hull vertex without overlapping edges? Not sure. 

This might work, but the code could be complicated. Perhaps an alternative way using the following method:

Pick a point O and arrange all points in radial order from O. Then connect each point in turn to its previous in this order. This would form a star-like structure with non-crossing edges except for the rays emanating from center O. 

Alternatively, a spiral ordering can form a non-crossing path.

But in code, to construct a non-crossing tree:

A planar graph can be constructed as a non-crossing tree by connecting points in a sequential order that doesn't cross, such as using a radial sorting.

Alternatively, here's an algorithm to build a non-crossing tree:

- Choose an arbitrary point as root.

- Arrange all other points in counter-clockwise order around root based on polar coordinates.

- The root connects to the first point, then each successive point connects to the previous, forming a chain like a spiral. 

This results in a tree with edges between consecutive points in the sorted list and the root to first ?

Wait:

Example steps:

- Sort all points around the root in counter clockwise order.

- The tree is a star? Not sure. 

Alternatively, connect the first point to root, then second to first, third to second, etc. 

This would form a path where each edge doesn't cross, since they're all oriented in a circular fashion. This would form a non-crossing spanning tree if the arranged order is correct.

Let me think for an example:

Take root R. Arrange points around it in clockwise order: A,B,C, D. 

- Connect R-A (edge 1)

- A-B (edge 2)

- B-C (edge3)

- C-D (edge4). 

 This is a linear path, and since all points are around the root in order, the edges won't cross. 

 Yes, that should work.

Therefore the steps for building this tree are:

- Select a root (say the point with minimum y-coordinate, or first), 

- Compute the angle of each other point relative to the root, sort the points around the root by polar angle, then create edges as: root to first, first to second, etc.

This forms a non-crossing tree.

Once this is done, the edges form a chain from the root through all nodes arranged in polar coordinate order. 

This would ensure that all edges are non-crossing.

Thus the tree can be constructed. 

Next, once we have this tree structure with each node's parent (since its a tree), we can then proceed to move the labels along their paths to their destinations.

Thus, the planning for the code's approach is this:

1. Choose a root, say the first point,

2. compute the polar angle of other points around it, 

3. sort them in order,

4. chains them in a non-crossing path, building the parent pointers accordingly.

Alternatively, even there might be other points not in the convex hull, but with the polar angles sorted, the edges won't cross, this tree is non-crossing.

Once this is done, we can proceed with the algorithm to perform swaps.

Now, to move the labels correctly along the tree with post-order processing:

Implement steps as follows:

Initialize positions array and current_labels array as the current permutation.

Pos is essentially {label X → current position of X}

Because initially, for each position i the label is a_i (given as input). So:

pos = [ ... ] where pos[label] is initialized to the index where it is.

current_labels[v] = initial a of v.

Then perform post-order traversal.

Wait, how to perform post-order traversal:

build the tree as a parent graph.

Define children for each node.

We can use a list of children: for each node v, children[v].

Once the tree is built this way, post-order processing can be done via a recursive or iterative algorithm.

Once we' re at each node v via post-order, process as follows:

Suppose we process v:

Check what is the desired label for v: v must have label v.

 current_L = current_labels[v]

if current_L == v → do nothing。

else:

 the desired label v is? well, we want to have label v here. 

 The current occurrence of label v is stored in pos[v].

 So, the current node storing 'v' is at position W = pos[v]

 We need to move 'v' from W to v.

To find the path from W to v.

To move it step by step.

 For each step:

- while W != v:

   find the next node (step) in the direction from W to v.

   the 'next' node is the parent of W if v is higher in the tree hierarchy.

   Or,possibly the child.

   need to determine the direction.

   To determine the best way:

   For example:

   Break the path into segments until the W and v's least common ancestor is reached.

   This requires that for each node, we can move up towards the root until the LCA, then down towards v. 

   Alternatively, using the parent array to determine the path.

   1. Find the path from W to v:

   path is all nodes between W and v.

   Let’s form the path as W → parent(W) → parent(parent(W))...,LCA, then LCA down to v’s direction.

   To compute this path, take the following steps.

   Alternatively, implement the following method:

   Starting from node W, move up to root step by step until parent is above v. Then from that ancestor, step down to v.

   Alternatively, use BFS or DFS to find the path.

   However this might be time-consuming.

   To simplify:

   For each node along the path, we can step from W towards v by:

   while W is not v:

      if the parent of W is in the path to v:

          swap W and its parent: 

          thus: 

              swap the labels between W and its parent

              add these swap to the list

              record the swaps.

              update the current_labels for both.

              update the pos array for the affected labels.

          W = parent(W) 

      else:

          this indicates W is on a different subtree than v, so must move towards LCA.

   This method may require moving W up the tree, then finding the child branches.

   However, perhaps the simplest way is to just move up to the LCA:

   按照这个思路，我们需要重新构造路径的每一个步骤。

然而，在代码中，这可能会比较复杂。因此，可能需要写一个路径的查找函数。

例如，对于每个 W and v:

建立一个 path数组，contains all nodes along the path between W and v.

This path can be find by:

找出 W和 v的 LCA,然后合并从 W到 LCA的路径，加上从 LCA到 v的路径. 

此外，这可能需要于树的一些预处理，如每个节点的father pointers ( parents) and possibly the entry of ancestors.

Perhaps we can implement the following method to find the path between any two nodes:

Function to get path from W to v:

初始化：

current = W

path = [current]

当 current != v：

   父节点是 parent[current]

   如果 parent[current] 不在 path里，那么：

      path.append( parent[current] )

      current = parent[current]

   否则： 这可能发生在循环中？ 不，在树中肯定有一个方向.

 抱歉可能我的思路有困难。 

Alternatively, the path can be walked in reverse from v, to the LCA, then from w towards LCA。 合并 the two paths反过来。

总之, 对于 any two nodes， they have a unique path, so perhaps it's easier to implement in code.

   # find the path from start to end.

   # uses backtracking along parents

   path_start = [ ]

   current = start

   while current != end:

       path_start.append(current)

       current = parent[current]

    # path from start to end is path_start + [end]
