•  » » » » » » » » » 7 months ago, # ^ |   0 Oh, I see.Well, let's try to 'prove' that the forward approach always gives an optimal answer.What my code is doing is it's going from 0 to n and checking if each character needs to be converted or not. If it does, start a window, otherwise move on to the next index. Notice that when we move on to the next index, we'll never come back to the previous one.So why is this important? That means that when we see an element that needs to be converted, we have to start a window. You might say that there are multiple ways to start a window (for example if we want to convert i+1, we could start a window of size k>=2 at index i) so that an index is converted, but the most optimal way will always be when it's started at the index that needs to be converted, since that way more indices ahead can be affected.If you're convinced that the forward approach is correct, you can see why the backward approach will also be correct from my above reply, thus showing that both approaches will give the same answer.

<#sep#>Implement a class `Book` to represent a book with the following attributes:
- `title`: A string representing the title of the book.
- `author`: A string representing the author of the book.
- `year_published`: An integer representing the year the book was published.
- `publisher`: A string representing the publisher of the book.
- `genre`: A string representing the genre of the book.
- `isbn`: A string representing the ISBN number of the book.

The class should include the following methods:
- `description`: Returns a string in the format `"'<title>' by <author>, published in <year_published>."`.
- `update_publisher(new_publisher)`: Updates the publisher of the book to `new_publisher`.
- `update_genre(new_genre)`: Updates the genre of the book to `new_genre`.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```python
book = Book("1984", "George Orwell", 1949, "Secker & Warburg", "Dystopian", "9780451524935")
print(book.description())  # Output: "'1984' by George Orwell, published in 1949."
book.update_publisher("Signet Classic")
book.update_genre("Science Fiction")
print(book.publisher)  # Output: "Signet Classic"
print(book.genre)      # Output: "Science Fiction"
```

```python
class Book:
    def __init__(self, title, author, year_published, publisher, genre, isbn):
        """
        Initializes a new Book instance with the given attributes.
        
        :param title: The title of the book.
        :param author: The author of the book.
        :param year_published: The year the book was published.
        :param publisher: The publisher of the book.
        :param genre: The genre of the book.
        :param isbn: The ISBN number of the book.
        """
        self.title = title
        self.author = author
        self.year_published = year_published
        self.publisher = publisher
        self.genre = genre
        self.isbn = isbn

    def description(self):
        """
        Returns a string describing the book, including the title, author, and year published.
        
        :return: A string in the format "'<title>' by <author>, published in <year_published>."
        """
        return f"'{self.title}' by {self.author}, published in {self.year_published}."

    def update_publisher(self, new_publisher):
        """
        Updates the publisher of the book to the given value.
        
        :param new_publisher: The new publisher of the book.
        """
        self.publisher = new_publisher

    def update_genre(self, new_genre):
        """
        Updates the genre of the book to the given value.
        
        :param new_genre: The new genre of the book.
        """
        self.genre = new_genre

假设 code中，父节点的数组存有每节点的父节点。

那么， 函数 get_path(start, end) returns the list of nodes in the path from start to end:

def get_path(start, end):

   # find the path from start to end.

   # uses backtracking along parents

   path_start = [ ]

   current = start

   while current != end:

       path_start.append(current)

       current = parent[current]

    # path from start to end is path_start + [end]

    start到 end的 path：

   return path_start + [end]

等等，也许这样就可以构建path。

No, this is only if end is an ancestor of start. 也许 better是：

从双方向上走 until finding a common ancestor, then build path从 start到 ancestor，然后从 ancestor到 end.

But这可能比较复杂.

Another approach: 

To find the path from W to v，：

path_w = []

current = W

while current not root:

   path_w.append(current)

   current = parent[current]

path_w.append(root)

Similarly， path_v is:

path_v = []

current = v

while currnet not root:

    path_v.append(current)

    current = parent[current]

path_v.append(root)

然后， find the point where the paths diverge.

然后 combine the path from W往上到 LCA, then the other way down.

但这可能对于.”

这可能比较复杂。

Alternatively, the simple approach that works even if it takes O(d) steps for each move:

每 step, we can move the current node W向 the desired node v的 direction.

如何确定下一步的 movement:

每当 W is移动到 its parent 或者到 one of its children?

要向 v移动，需要在树中向其 position.

Therefore, the parent of W will be in the path towards v if v is an ancestor of w.

OR, if v is a descendant of W？ 

Thus:

如果 the node v is a descendant of W, then the path from W down towards v, but the child nodes need to be followed. 但是如何找到 child node in the direction towards v ?

This may require recursively searching through children.

这可能是 time consuming.

Alternatively, the following approach, which may work:

在 the tree中，要 move the label from W到 v。

遍历 path each step:

例如， while current position of the label v (W) is not v:

   父 node： parent_W = parent[W]

   所以, the父节点与W之间有一条边.

   如果 the current move towards the direction of v是向父节点的 direction:

      the path from W到 parent_w is closer to v ？

      例如, if v is on the path towards parent of W， then we need to move to parent.

   或者,可能的:

   The path from W到 v includes moving towards its parent. Thus, each step we can move the label towards its parent.

 So, we can do:

 SWAP W和其 parent.

之后, the label v is now at parent of W, so we update W to parent_W.

 repeatedly ，直到到达 v.

这可以处理所有的案子.

因为 the tree是 unique的路径 to root, so the path FROM W到 v must be either going up or down the tree树.

或者,可能 the path是从 W到 ancestor direction, thus upwards是 towards the root方向.

因此，每次我们 are moving towards parent， we are moving up towards the root. 当到达 LCA然后, then the path goes down to v,. 

但是,我们 cannot move down if the current position is the LCA or above.

因此， this method of moving only upwards may not work; / but if the path requires moving down, this approach would~ fail.

因此，必须找到 another way.

Perhaps, the correct path can be represented as an list of steps:

The path from W到 v is known by following parent pointers and the children tree结构. 

Thus 如何 efficiently move?

Perhaps to move the stop W toward v:

 you can move towards the parent until you reach the LCA and the path to v is downwards.

您也可以 move towards a child if it's in the direction of v.

这可能 require knowledge of all children of each node.

一种 possible approach:

与 every node's children are stored.

当 moving a label from W到 v：

当前位置是 W。 我们 want to get as close as possible to v.

 Let's note the path is unique and can be found by BFS 的 tree是的， non-crossing.

 thus, the最 short path is the path in the tree.

 therefore, until we reach v， we choose the next node along the path, whether it is the parent or the child (if moving down).

因此， to compute the next step, we can find the邻接点邻近 which is closer to v.

但 how?

或许 we can compute the next node as:

 the visit the current W.

 the寻找其 adjacent nodes (父 and children).

 which of these is on the path towards v?

 the next step is向着 the node which is向着 v.

这 may be coded as:

 while W != v：

    next_node = next direction towards v：

    we need to decide if we need to move towards the parent or to a certain child.

    suppose current是 W.

   if parent[W] are on the path toward v:

        then the next step is the parent.

    else:

        the path is downward: one of the children is on the path.

        在 this case, the specific孩子 can be found via children array, through遍历 all children to see if they are in the path toward v. 这 may be O(d), but for n=2000， each step可能 be 2000.

 这 may cause too much time.

An alternative is that:

0-based or 1-based node numbering may be important, but perhaps we can precompute for every node and possible destination (v) the path. however this is O(n^2), mungkin manageable for n=2000 but not really.

Alternatively, perhaps there's an easier way to traverse the path from W to v.

Since the parent pointers form the tree, we can represent the path as follows:

The path is W, followed by parent[W], then parent[parent[W]], ... until reaching the lowest common ancestor LCA between W and v.

 Then from the LCA, follow the path down to v. 

 therefore, to find the complete path, we can ’s:

path = []

current = W

# go up to the LCA

 while current is not LCA:

    path.append(current)

    current = parent[current]

#然后， go down to v.

然后， how to find the LCA and the path downward.

然而， menghentikan this requires LCA finding, which may be complex.

 Satu aproach to menghitung LCA bisa menggunakan binary lifting, tetapi ini mungkin time consuming coded.

 Maybe this problem is getting too complicated for the programming competition. 

 Given the time constraint, perhaps the problem requires a different approach entirely. 

Let’s think of the swaps as forming a planar structure.

Suppose every swap is between the current item's current position and its next target in the permutation's path and the connections don't cross.

 For each cycle in the permutation:

 if the cycle is length 1, no action. 

 else, the cycle's elements form a cycle via the permutation. 

 the swaps can be arranged in a way that the edges form a polygon form the cycle. 

 which is planar.

 For example:

 a 3-cycle forms a triangle, whose edges are non-crossing. swapping the elements in the right order will compose the swap into the required permutations.

 thus, the number of swaps is cycle length minus 1.

 similarly, a cycle of length m requires m-1 swaps.

So the total swap count is n- cycles.

 but the edges must be non-crossing.

 thus, the key is that FOR EACH CYCLE, the corresponding edges can form a polygon which is planar. 

 thus, the problem reduces to checking whether the cycles can be rewritten as polygon edges which do not cross with other cycles' edges.

 Additionally，当处理 each cycle as a polygon, the swaps can be done along the edges of those polygons without crossing.

 thus, the algorithm could be:

For each cycle in the permutation:

-取 the cycle's points as a closed polygon which is convex (or non-crossing edges) .

- then the swaps can be along the edges of this polygon.

 thus the steps would be:

1.分解 the permutation into cycles.

2. for each cycle:

   •find the set of points in the cycle. 

   • arrange them in a convex polygon的 order这样 that the edges between the cycle points form a convex polygon. 可能 the points are not convex, but we can choose an ordering of the cycle which form a non-crossing edges.

 3. 对于 each cycle, perform the swaps along the edges of the polygon in the right order to vaiable the cycle into the identity.

 the required swaps for a cycle of length m can be implemented with a transposition of each adjacent pair.

例如在 a 3- cycle (<#sep#>DynamoDb2 create_table not validate ProvisionedThroughput parameter

> If you set BillingMode as PROVISIONED , you must specify this property. If you set BillingMode as PAY_PER_REQUEST , you cannot specify this property.

if you run the code below using `@mock_dynamodb2`, it does not raise an error.

 » 9 months ago, # |   0 Problem E can be solved in $\mathcal{O}(n\log n + \log n\log (a_i+x))$.First, we notice that the permutation of $a[i]$ does not matter at all.So we can sort(a,a+n) first, and then when we want to<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple calendar application in Python. Your application should support the following operations:

1. **Add Event**: Add an event with a title, date, and an optional description.
2. **Remove Event**: Remove an event by specifying its title and date.
3. **View Events**: Retrieve a list of all events.
4. **List Events on Date**: Retrieve a list of all events on a specific date.

The `Event` class should have the following attributes:
- `title`: A string representing the title of the event.
- `date`: A `datetime` object representing the date of the event.
- `description`: An optional string representing the description of the event.

The `Calendar` class should have the following methods:
- `add_event(title, date, description="")`: Adds a new event to the calendar.
- `remove_event(title, date)`: Removes an event from the calendar.
- `view_events()`: Returns a list of all events.
- `list_events_on_date(date)`: Returns a list of events on a specific date.

**Sample Input/Output**:
```python
cal = Calendar()
cal.add_event("Meeting", datetime(2023, 10, 15), "Team meeting at 10 AM.")
cal.add_event("Dentist", datetime(2023, 10, 20), "Check-up.")
print(cal.view_events())
# Expected Output: [Event(title='Meeting', date='2023-10-15', description='Team meeting at 10 AM.'), Event(title='Dentist', date='2023-10-20', description='Check-up.')]

print(cal.list_events_on_date(datetime(2023, 10, 15)))
# Expected Output: [Event(title='Meeting', date='2023-10-15', description='Team meeting at 10 AM.')]

cal.remove_event("Dentist", datetime(2023, 10, 20))
print(cal.view_events())
# Expected Output: [Event(title='Meeting', date='2023-10-15', description='Team meeting at 10 AM.')]
```

```python
from datetime import datetime

class Event:
    def __init__(self, title, date, description=""):
        self.title = title
        self.date = date
        self.description = description

    def __repr__(self):
        return f"Event(title='{self.title}', date='{self.date.strftime('%Y-%m-%d')}', description='{self.description}')"

class Calendar:
    def __init__(self):
        self.events = []

    def add_event(self, title, date, description=""):
        """Adds a new event to the calendar."""
        new_event = Event(title, date, description)
        self.events.append(new_event)

    def remove_event(self, title, date):
        """Removes an event from the calendar."""
        for event in self.events:
            if event.title == title and event.date == date:
                self.events.remove(event)
                break

    def view_events(self):
        """Returns a list of all events."""
        return self.events

    def list_events_on_date(self, date):
        """Returns a list of events on a specific date."""
        return [event for event in self.events if event.date == date]
```<#sep#> apart. Wait, perhaps arranging the black cells in a grid where each black cell is part of a 5x5 square, but arranged in such a way that overlapping squares share cells.

Alternatively, perhaps arranging the black cells in such a way that each row has exactly one black cell every 5 columns. For instance, in each row, the black cells are at columns 1,6,11,... Then in a 5x5 square, if it's aligned with the columns, say from column 1-5, then in each row, there is one black cell (at column 1) in the first row? Wait, but that might not be consistent across rows.

Alternatively, maybe a diagonal arrangement. Suppose that in each row, the black cells are shifted by 1 column each row, so that over 5 rows, they form a diagonal. Then in a 5x5 square, the diagonal would have one black cell per row and column, totaling 5. That could work.

For example, let's consider rows 1 to 5. In row 1, the black cell is at column 1. In row 2, column 2. Row 3, column 3. Row 4, column 4. Row 5, column 5. Then, the 5x5 square from (1,1) to (5,5) would have exactly 5 black cells on the main diagonal. Then, if we shift this pattern every 5 rows and columns, so that in rows 6-10, the black cells are at columns 6-10, etc. Then, any 5x5 square that is aligned with these blocks would have exactly 5 black cells. However, overlapping squares that cross these blocks might have fewer or more?

Wait, let me test this idea. Suppose we have a repeating 5x5 block where each block has a diagonal of black cells. Then, if we take a 5x5 square that is entirely within one block, it has exactly 5 black cells. But if we take a square that overlaps two blocks, say starting at row 3, column 3, spanning to row 7, column 7, then the overlapping regions might have more black cells? Hmm, maybe not. Wait, actually, in this arrangement, the next block starts at row 6, column 6. So the square from (3,3) to (7,7) would include parts of two blocks. In rows 3-5, columns 3-5: the original diagonal has black cells at (3,3), (4,4), (5,5). Then rows 6-7, columns 6-7 would have black cells at (6,6) and (7,7). Wait, but the square from (3,3) to (7,7) is actually a 5x5 square starting at (3,3). Its rows are 3-7 and columns 3-7. The black cells within it would be (3,3), (4,4), (5,5), (6,6), (7,7). That's exactly 5 black cells. Wait a second! So even overlapping blocks in this diagonal arrangement would still have exactly 5 black cells in each 5x5 square?

2. for each cycle:

   •find the set of points in the cycle. 

   • arrange them in a convex polygon的 order这样 that the edges between the cycle points form a convex polygon. 可能 the points are not convex, but we can choose an ordering of the cycle which form a non-crossing edges.

 3. 对于 each cycle, perform the swaps along the edges of the polygon in the right order to vaiable the cycle into the identity.

 the required swaps for a cycle of length m can be implemented with a transposition of each adjacent pair.

例如在 a 3- cycle (a → b → c → a), then swap a and b, then swap a and c. 这些 edges won't cross because they are the sides of a triangle.

 being such the edges are edges of the polygon, which are non-crossing.

 thus, the overall set of edges ( the combination of all cycles' edges) forme a planar graph because each cycle's edges form a polygon and different cycles are disjoint in their nodes, thus their edges do not cross.

 This approach would work only if the cycle's edges form non-crossing polygons, and the cycles are handled independently.

 thus, the key is that each cycle can form a convex or non- crossing polygon.

然而,  the points may not be arranged in convex polygon对于 each cycle. thus, the question becomes whether the points of the cycle are in general position such that they can form a polygon with non-crossing edges. since no three are colinear, the polygon edges can be arranged in such a way that the edges do not cross except at vertices.

 but using the cycle's vertices in order around their convex hull can ensure the polygon edges form a convex polygon, which is non-crossing.

因此 the steps would be：

对每个 cycle：

   将 the nodes in the cycle sorted around their convex hull, then swap along the edges of the convex hull顺序吗?这样获得的 edges are non-crossing. 

这 perhaps would allow the swaps to form non-crossing edges.

 thus the solution is:

 find all the cycles in the permutation.

 for each cycle:

   the cycle's nodes must form a convex polygon, and the edges form the polygon, so the swaps can be made betweem consecutive nodes in the polygon ordering.

 the sequence of swaps for each cycle when viewed as a polygon can be done by applying the needed number of swaps to turn the cycle into identity, such as transpositions along the polygon edges.

 this will ensure all the edges are non-crossing.

 thus, the algorithm is:

1. 分解 permutation into cycles.

2. for each cycle:

   a. if its length is 1, skip.

   b. else:

    获取 the nodes in the cycle, and order them in convex hull order around their convex hull or in a non-crossing manner. 

   然后 perform swaps along the edges of the convex hull order to decompose the cycle.

    the具体 swaps needed for a cycle of length m是 m-1 swaps. for example, the 3- cycle can be fixed with two swaps along the edges of the triangle.

 the total edges used are convex polygon edges for each cycle, thus the total edges form a planar graph (as each cycle's edges form a polygon and different cycles are separate).

 this approach requires that each cycle's points can form a convex or non-crossing polygon边缘.

 however, even if the points are not convex, the ordering of the nodes in a way that the edges do not cross can be achieved via their sorted order around the element of the cycle.

 thus the steps are:

 for each cycle:

   get the list of nodes in the cycle.

   把 them排序 based on their coordinates to form a polygon with non-crossing edges. 

 one method is to compute their convex hull and traverse the vertices in a counter-clockwise manner.

然后 perform the swaps along the consecutive edges of「 their convex hull的 edges.」这样 the edges won't cross.

 then, the necessary swaps are the adjacency swaps, for example. For a cycle of 3, the swaps would be between consecutive pairs, which form the convex hull edges.

具体 steps for each cycle:

假设 the cycic nodes are [a_1, a_2,...,a_k], arranged in a convex hull-order.

 the permutation is .a_1→a_2 →a_3 → ...→a_k →a_1 .

 for the decomposition into transpositions，我们 need to reduce this cycle to identity: 

 the decomposition could be the product (a_1 a_2), (a_1 a_3), ..., (a_1 a_k).

这's like cracking the cycle by swaps that bring each element to their place through the first element. 

 each of these swaps' edges (a_1, a_2), (a_1, a_3), ..., (a_1, a_k), would form a star graph centered at a_1. the star edges are non-crossing.

 thus, the swaps can be done as per this decomposition.

 thus， the steps are:

 for each cycle of length>=2:

    pick a first node, say a_1.

    swap a_1 with a_2 → this gives the permutation ~(a_1 a_2). 

    then swap a_1 with a_3 → which gives permutationした ~(a_1 a_3). 

    continue jusqu到 a_1与 a_k sorted. 

 the final permutation is bahwa the cycle is turned into identity.

 the edges used are a_1 with each other cycle node, which form a star which is planar.

 thus this way of decomposing cycles into a central node and swapping sequentially ensures that the edges are non-crossing if the central node is chosen as the center of the convex hull of the cycle's points.

 thus the steps become:

1. decompose the permutation into cycles.

2. for each cycle:

   if长度 <2: skip. 

 else:

   选择 a central node for the cycle (maybe the first in the sorted list)

   将 cycle's nodes按照 their position relative the central node into o'clock order, so the edges from the central node to others don't cross.

   then perform swaps:

 for i from 2到k:

    交换 the central node与 a_i. 

这样， the edges form a star (or radial） arrangement, ensuring non-crossing edges.

 this 기로 다的 steps will保证 all swaps are non-crossing因为 they are all connected to the central node.

 this 是 the key!

 this approach would work because all edges are from the central node, so they form a star graph with non-crossing edges.

 » 9 months ago, # |   0 Problem E can be solved in $\mathcal{O}(n\log n + \log n\log (a_i+x))$.First, we notice that the permutation of $a[i]$ does not matter at all.So we can sort(a,a+n) first, and then when we want to calculate how much water we use when the height is $h$, we can use idx=lower_bound(a,a+n,h)-a, and then the water we use nowuse=idx*mid-s[idx-1].$s[i]$ is the prefix of sorted $a[i]$, i from 0 to n-1. We can use this because for $idx\le i\le n-1$, we have $a[i]>h$, so the use of $i$ is $0$. By using this way, we can avoid calculating max(0,...) with n times.Since sort takes $\mathcal{O}(n\log n)$ and in every check in binary search we use lower_bound which is $\mathcal{O}(\log n)$, we have $\mathcal{O}(n\log n + \log n\log (a_i+x))$ in all.

 » 9 months ago, # | ← Rev. 3 →   +1 for problem G, as mentioned in the tutorial, we can see the problem as each B choose one direction(left or right) to eat the A's, so we can use dp.dp[i][0] means if the i-th B choose to go left, the maximum A's that the 1-th, 2-th, ..., i-th B can eat. dp[i][1] is similar, it means the i-th B choose to go right.therefore, we can get the transition equation: dp[i][0] = dp[i-1][0] + i-th B's position — (i-1)-th B's position — 1 (because if the (i-1)-th B go right and the i-th B wants to left, it will have conflict, so (i-1)-th B can only choose to go left) dp[i][1] = max(dp[i-1][0], dp[i-1][1]) + (i+1)-th B's position — i-th B's position (if i-th B wants to go right, we don't need to care about the (i-1)-th B's choice)and here is the implementation https://codeforces.com/contest/1873/submission/224561489

The answer is min(5-x, 5-y) per 'X' instead of hard-coding it: #include #define ll long long #define push push_back #define fi first #define se second #define TC int t; cin >> t; for(int u=1; u<=t; u++) using namespace std; int main(){ TC{ int ans = 0; for(int i=0; i<10; i++){ for(int j=0; j<10; j++){ char c; cin >> c; if(c=='X'){ /* (4, 4) (5, 4) (4, 5) (5, 5) */ pair point = make_pair(min(abs(4-i), abs(5-i)), min(abs(4-j), abs(5-j))); ans+=(min(abs(5-point.fi), abs(5-point.se))); } } }cout << ans << "\n"; } } Submission: 224566541

 » 9 months ago, # | ← Rev. 2 →   +8 For the last problem,<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a simple library management system using a Python class. The system should support the following operations:

1. **Add Book**: Add a book to the library with its title, author, and a unique ISBN number. If a book with the same ISBN already exists, do not add it and print an appropriate message.
2. **Borrow Book**: Allow a user to borrow a book by specifying the ISBN and the borrower's name. If the book does not exist or is already borrowed, print an appropriate message.
3. **Return Book**: Allow a user to return a book by specifying the ISBN. If the book was not borrowed, print an appropriate message.

Your class should maintain a record of all books and which books are currently borrowed.

**Input Format**:
- The input will be a series of commands. Each command will be on a new line.
- The first word of each command will be the operation to perform: `ADD`, `BORROW`, or `RETURN`.
- For `ADD` commands, the next three words will be the title, author, and ISBN of the book.
- For `BORROW` commands, the next two words will be the ISBN and the borrower's name.
- For `RETURN` commands, the next word will be the ISBN of the book.

**Output Format**:
- For each command, print a message indicating the result of the operation.

**Sample Input**:
```
ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell 1234567890
ADD ToKillAMockingbird HarperLee 0987654321
BORROW 1234567890 Alice
BORROW 1234567890 Bob
RETURN 1234567890
BORROW 1234567890 Bob
RETURN 0987654321
RETURN 9999999999
ADD 1984 GeorgeOrwell 1234567890
```

**Sample Output**:
```
Book '1984' by GeorgeOrwell (ISBN: 1234567890) added to the library.
Book 'ToKillAMockingbird' by HarperLee (ISBN: 0987654321) added to the library.
Book '1984' borrowed by Alice.
Book with ISBN 1234567890 is currently borrowed.
Book '1984' returned by Alice.
Book '1984' borrowed by Bob.
Book 'ToKillAMockingbird' returned by None.
Book with ISBN 9999999999 was not borrowed.
Book with ISBN 1234567890 already exists in the library.
```

```python
class Library:
    def __init__(self):
        self.books = {}
        self.borrowed_books = {}

    def add_book(self, title, author, isbn):
        """
        Adds a book to the library with the given title, author, and ISBN.
        If the book already exists, prints a message indicating so.
        """
        if isbn in self.books:
            print(f"Book with ISBN {isbn} already exists in the library.")
        else:
            self.books[isbn] = {'title': title, 'author': author, 'available': True}
            print(f"Book '{title}' by {author} (ISBN: {isbn}) added to the library.")

    def borrow_book(self, isbn, borrower_name):
        """
        Allows a user to borrow a book by specifying the ISBN and borrower's name.
        Prints a message indicating the result of the operation.
        """
        if isbn not in self.books:
            print(f"Book with ISBN {isbn} does not exist in the library.")
        elif not self.books[isbn]['available']:
            print(f"Book with ISBN {isbn} is currently borrowed.")
        else:
            self.books[isbn]['available'] = False
            self.borrowed_books[isbn] = borrower_name
            print(f"Book '{self.books[isbn]['title']}' borrowed by {borrower_name}.")

    def return_book(self, isbn):
        """
        Allows a user to return a book by specifying the ISBN.
        Prints a message indicating the result of the operation.
        """
        if isbn not in self.borrowed_books:
            print(f"Book with ISBN {isbn} was not borrowed.")
        else:
            self.books[isbn]['available'] = True
            borrower_name = self.borrowed_books.pop(isbn)
            print(f"Book '{self.books[isbn]['title']}' returned by {borrower_name}.")

•  » » » 9 months ago, # ^ |   0 I think you are right. I thought too deep about it: I thought the winning strategy is to walk along an induced cycle, otherwise, marcel can at some point go along a chord of a cycle and catch Valeriu. Finding a winning strategy is difficult I believe, the question didn't ask for the winning strategy, though. I think the reason I've thought too much about it is that, I overlooked that every vertex that lies on a cycle, also lies on an induced cycle, so I didn't need to check if a vertex is on induced cycle, if it is on a cycle, certainly there is an induced cycle the vertex is on it. So, yes it was easier to decide if he wins but not easy to find a winning strategy. Also one last and less important point: the approach you mentioned in general graphs could be quadratic to #vertices, so if the number of the vertices is big (like in this case), it could fail if it has too many edges (well this case didn't have that).

•  » » » 9 months ago, # ^ |   0 If you don't understand anything just read the offical solution :))) It's much faster, simpler and more elegant.With the given conditions, every bridge in the graph does not belong to the cycle, and thus every non-bridge belongs to the cycle. After we traverse on the graph we will find the full set of vertices that belong to the cycle. Start traversing from Valeriu's positon until you reach one that is in the cycle (possibly Valeriu's original position itself) and calculate the distance between the vertices. As the tutorial above indicates, this is Valeriu's entry to the cycle. We then calculate the distance between that entry vertice and Marcel's. The answer is No if the former distance is not smaller that the latter, or both Valeriu and Marcel share the same starting position.For an implementation of the bridge-finding algorithm, see https://cp-algorithms.com/graph/bridge-searching.html#implementation.

 » 9 months ago, # |   0 Yesterday i submit solution of<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing an `InventoryManager` class to manage a store's inventory. The class should support the following operations:

1. **Add Item**: Add a specified quantity of an item to the inventory. If the item already exists, increase its quantity.
2. **Remove Item**: Remove a specified quantity of an item from the inventory. If the item does not exist or the specified quantity exceeds the available quantity, raise a `ValueError`.
3. **Get Item Quantity**: Return the current quantity of a specified item. If the item does not exist, return 0.

**Input/Output Specification**:
- The `InventoryManager` class will be tested with a series of operations. Each operation will be one of the three types mentioned above.
- For the `add_item` and `remove_item` operations, the input will be the item name (a string) and the quantity (an integer).
- For the `get_item_quantity` operation, the input will be the item name (a string).
- The output for `get_item_quantity` should be the current quantity of the item.

**Sample Input/Output**:
- Operations: `add_item("apple", 10)`, `add_item("banana", 5)`, `remove_item("apple", 3)`, `get_item_quantity("apple")`, `get_item_quantity("banana")`
- Expected Output: `7`, `5`

```python
class InventoryManager:
    def __init__(self):
        """Initialize an empty inventory."""
        self.inventory = {}

    def add_item(self, item, quantity):
        """
        Add a specified quantity of an item to the inventory.
        If the item already exists, increase its quantity.
        
        :param item: The name of the item (str).
        :param quantity: The quantity to add (int).
        """
        if item in self.inventory:
            self.inventory[item] += quantity
        else:
            self.inventory[item] = quantity

    def remove_item(self, item, quantity):
        """
        Remove a specified quantity of an item from the inventory.
        If the item does not exist or the specified quantity exceeds the available quantity, raise a ValueError.
        
        :param item: The name of the item (str).
        :param quantity: The quantity to remove (int).
        :raises ValueError: If the item does not exist or the quantity to remove is greater than available.
        """
        if item not in self.inventory:
            raise ValueError(f"Item '{item}' does not exist in the inventory.")
        if self.inventory[item] < quantity:
            raise ValueError(f"Not enough quantity of item '{item}' in the inventory to remove.")
        self.inventory[item] -= quantity
        if self.inventory[item] == 0:
            del self.inventory[item]

    def get_item_quantity(self, item):
        """
        Return the current quantity of a specified item.
        If the item does not exist, return 0.
        
        :param item: The name of the item (str).
        :return: The current quantity of the item (int).
        """
        return self.inventory.get(item, 0)

Because in the second block (rows 6-10),<#sep#>human: In order to do some research, n^2 labs are built on different heights of a mountain. Let's enumerate them with integers from 1 to n^2, such that the lab with the number 1 is at the lowest place, the lab with the number 2 is at the second-lowest place, …, the lab with the number n^2 is at the highest place.

To transport water between the labs, pipes are built between every pair of labs. A pipe can transport at most one unit of water at a time from the lab with the number u to the lab with the number v if u > v.

Now the labs need to be divided into n groups, each group should contain exactly n labs. The labs from different groups can transport water to each other. The sum of units of water that can be sent from a group A to a group B is equal to the number of pairs of labs (u, v) such that the lab with the number u is from the group A, the lab with the number v is from the group B and u > v. Let's denote this value as f(A,B) (i.e. f(A,B) is the sum of units of water that can be sent from a group A to a group B).

For example, if n=3 and there are 3 groups X, Y and Z: X = \{1, 5, 6\}, Y = \{2, 4, 9\} and Z = \{3, 7, 8\}. In this case, the values of f are equal to:

  * f(X,Y)=4 because of 5 → 2, 5 → 4, 6 → 2, 6 → 4, 
  * f(X,Z)=2 because of 5 → 3, 6 → 3, 
  * f(Y,X)=5 because of 2 → 1, 4 → 1, 9 → 1, 9 → 5, 9 → 6, 
  * f(Y,Z)=4 because of 4 → 3, 9 → 3, 9 → 7, 9 → 8, 
  * f(Z,X)=7 because of 3 → 1, 7 → 1, 7 → 5, 7 → 6, 8 → 1, 8 → 5, 8 → 6, 
  * f(Z,Y)=5 because of 3 → 2, 7 → 2, 7 → 4, 8 → 2, 8 → 4. 



Please, divide labs into n groups with size n, such that the value min f(A,B) over all possible pairs of groups A and B (A ≠ B) is maximal.

In other words, divide labs into n groups with size n, such that minimum number of the sum of units of water that can be transported from a group A to a group B for every pair of different groups A and B (A ≠ B) as big as possible.

Note, that the example above doesn't demonstrate an optimal division, but it demonstrates how to calculate the values f for some division.

If there are many optimal divisions, you can find any.
Input

The only line contains one number n (2 ≤ n ≤ 300).

Output

Output n lines:

In the i-th line print n numbers, the numbers of labs of the i-th group, in any order you want.

If there are multiple answers, that maximize the minimum number of the sum of units of water that can be transported from one group the another, you can print any.

Example

Input


3


Output


2 8 5
9 3 4
7 6 1

Note

In the first test we can divide 9 labs into groups \{2, 8, 5\}, \{9, 3, 4\}, \{7, 6, 1\}.

From the first group to the second group we can transport 4 units of water (8 → 3, 8 → 4, 5 → 3, 5 → 4).

From the first group to the third group we can transport 5 units of water (2 → 1, 8 → 7, 8 → 6, 8 → 1, 5 → 1).

From the second group to the first group we can transport 5 units of water (9 → 2, 9 → 8, 9 → 5, 3 → 2, 4 → 2).

From the second group to the third group we can transport 5 units of water (9 → 7, 9 → 6, 9 → 1, 3 → 1, 4 → 1).

From the third group to the first group we can transport 4 units of water (7 → 2, 7 → 5, 6 → 2, 6 → 5).

From the third group to the second group we can transport 4 units of water (7 → 3, 7 → 4, 6 → 3, 6 → 4).

The minimal number of the sum of units of water, that can be transported from one group to another is equal to 4. It can be proved, that it is impossible to make a better division.

 » 9 months ago, # |   0 I tried solving "ABBC or BACB" problem with this code but I can't find the reason why my code is not working for 2 test cases ???(<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `FileAnalyzer` that processes a text file. The class should be initialized with a file path and provide methods to analyze the file's content. Specifically, the class should include the following methods:

- `count_lines()`: Returns the total number of lines in the file.
- `count_words()`: Returns the total number of words in the file. Words are defined as sequences of alphanumeric characters.
- `count_characters()`: Returns the total number of characters in the file, including whitespace and newline characters.
- `find_most_frequent_word()`: Returns a tuple containing the most frequent word in the file (case-insensitive) and its frequency. If the file is empty or contains no words, return `(None, 0)`.

**Sample Input:**
```
file_path = "sample.txt"
sample.txt contents:
Hello world
Hello Python
world of Python
```

**Sample Output:**
- `count_lines()` should return `3`.
- `count_words()` should return `8`.
- `count_characters()` should return `38`.
- `find_most_frequent_word()` should return `('hello', 2)`.

```python
import re
from collections import Counter

class FileAnalyzer:
    def __init__(self, file_path):
        """
        Initializes the FileAnalyzer with a file path.

        :param file_path: Path to the file to be analyzed.
        """
        self.file_path = file_path

    def count_lines(self):
        """
        Counts the number of lines in the file.

        :return: Number of lines in the file.
        """
        try:
            with open(self.file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                return sum(1 for _ in file)
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error reading the file: {e}")
            return 0

    def count_words(self):
        """
        Counts the number of words in the file. Words are defined as sequences of alphanumeric characters.

        :return: Number of words in the file.
        """
        try:
            with open(self.file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                content = file.read()
                words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', content)
                return len(words)
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error reading the file: {e}")
            return 0

    def count_characters(self):
        """
        Counts the number of characters in the file, including whitespace and newline characters.

        :return: Number of characters in the file.
        """
        try:
            with open(self.file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                return sum(len(line) for line in file)
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error reading the file: {e}")
            return 0

    def find_most_frequent_word(self):
        """
        Finds the most frequent word in the file (case-insensitive).

        :return: A tuple containing the most frequent word and its frequency.
                 Returns (None, 0) if the file is empty or contains no words.
        """
        try:
            with open(self.file_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8') as file:
                content = file.read()
                words = re.findall(r'\b\w+\b', content.lower())
                if not words:
                    return None, 0
                word_count = Counter(words)
                most_common_word, frequency = word_count.most_common(1)[0]
                return most_common_word, frequency
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error reading the file: {e}")
            return None, 0
```<#sep#>-7 are in the second block, so in rows 6-7, column 1 has a black cell. Alternatively, perhaps the problem requires the arrangement where for<#sep#>BUG: mock_dynamodb not handling query key conditions correctly
When making a dynamo query that compares a substituted ExpressionAttributeName against a literal value, moto accepts this comparison with no issue, but real AWS resources reject it as invalid. This allowed a bug in our query logic to get past our test suites before being caught by second stage testing.

example client code:
```
client = boto3.client("dynamodb")
client.query(
      TableName="my_table",
      KeyConditionExpression="#part_key=some_literal_value",
      ExpressionAttributeNames={"#part_key": "type"},
  )
```

Behavior in the real world (moto throws no errors):
```
botocore.exceptions.ClientError: An error occurred (ValidationException) when calling the Query operation: Invalid condition in KeyConditionExpression: Multiple attribute names used in one condition
```



'correct' code (tested in real-world)
```
client = boto3.client("dynamodb")
client.query(
    TableName="my_table",
    KeyConditionExpression="#part_key=:value",
    ExpressionAttributeNames={"#part_key": "type"},
    ExpressionAttributeValues = {":value": {"S": "foo"}}
)
```

As I understand it, a substituted attribute name can only be compared with a substituted attribute value. it is an illegal input to compare with a literal input. This may be due to the difference between low-level and high-level clients (https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/database/exploring-amazon-dynamodb-sdk-clients/) but I am not familiar enough with the dynamo apis to know for sure:

full repro example:
```
with mock_dynamodb():
    client = boto3.client("dynamodb", region_name="us-east-1")
    client.create_table(
        AttributeDefinitions=[
            {"AttributeName": "name", "AttributeType": "S"},
            {"AttributeName": "type", "AttributeType": "S"},
        ],
        TableName="my_table",
        KeySchema=[
            {"AttributeName": "type", "KeyType": "HASH"},
            {"AttributeName": "name", "KeyType": "RANGE"},
        ],
        BillingMode="PAY_PER_REQUEST",
    )

    client.query(
        TableName="my_table",
        KeyConditionExpression="#part_key=some_value",
        ExpressionAttributeNames={"#part_key": "type"},
    )
```

 » 8 months ago<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `Vehicle` class in Python. The class should have the following functionalities:
- Initialize with attributes `make`, `model`, and `year`.
- Provide a method `get_description` that returns a string in the format "year make model".
- Provide a method `update_vehicle` that allows updating the `make`, `model`, and `year` attributes.
- Include a static method `get_instance_count` that returns the total number of `Vehicle` instances created.

Your program should demonstrate the functionality by creating a few instances of the `Vehicle` class, updating one of them, and printing the descriptions and the total instance count.

**Sample Input/Output:**
```
Initial Vehicle Descriptions:
2020 Toyota Corolla
2018 Honda Civic

Updated Vehicle Description:
2021 Toyota Camry

Total Vehicle Instances:
2
```

```python
class Vehicle:
    # Class variable to keep track of the number of Vehicle instances created
    instance_count = 0
    
    def __init__(self, make, model, year):
        """
        Initialize a new Vehicle instance with the given make, model, and year.
        
        :param make: The make of the vehicle.
        :param model: The model of the vehicle.
        :param year: The year of the vehicle.
        """
        self.make = make
        self.model = model
        self.year = year
        Vehicle.instance_count += 1  # Increment the instance count
    
    def get_description(self):
        """
        Return a full description of the vehicle in the format "year make model".
        
        :return: A string describing the vehicle.
        """
        return f"{self.year} {self.make} {self.model}"
    
    def update_vehicle(self, make=None, model=None, year=None):
        """
        Update the attributes of the vehicle.
        
        :param make: The new make of the vehicle (optional).
        :param model: The new model of the vehicle (optional).
        :param year: The new year of the vehicle (optional).
        """
        if make is not None:
            self.make = make
        if model is not None:
            self.model = model
        if year is not None:
            self.year = year
    
    @staticmethod
    def get_instance_count():
        """
        Return the total number of Vehicle instances created.
        
        :return: The count of Vehicle instances.
        """
        return Vehicle.instance_count

# Create a few instances of the Vehicle class
vehicle1 = Vehicle("Toyota", "Corolla", 2020)
vehicle2 = Vehicle("Honda", "Civic", 2018)

# Print descriptions of the vehicles
print("Initial Vehicle Descriptions:")
print(vehicle1.get_description())  # Output: 2020 Toyota Corolla
print(vehicle2.get_description())  # Output: 2018 Honda Civic

# Update attributes of one of the vehicles
vehicle1.update_vehicle(year=2021, model="Camry")
print("\nUpdated Vehicle Description:")
print(vehicle1.get_description())  # Output: 2021 Toyota Camry

# Show the total number of Vehicle instances created
print("\nTotal Vehicle Instances:")
print(Vehicle.get_instance_count())  # Output: 2

// By: TESFAMICHAEL TAFERE

# include <bits/stdc++.h>

using namespace std;

int main() { ios::sync_with_stdio(false); cin.tie(nullptr);

int t; cin >> t; while (t--) { char s[10][10];

int count = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < 10; i++)
{
for (int j = 0; j < 10; j++)
{
cin >> s[i][j];
if (s[i][j] == 'X')
{
if (i == 0 || 10 - 1 == i || j == 0 || 10 - 1 == j)
count++;
else if (i == 1 || 10 - 2 == i || j == 1 || 10 - 2 == j)
count+=2;
else if ((i == 2 || 10 - 3 == i) || j == 2 || 10 - 3 == j)
count+=3;
else if (i == 3 || 10 - 4 == i || j == 3 || 10 - 4 == j)
count+=4;
else
count+=5;
}
}
}
cout << count << "\n";

}

return 0;`

}

 » 3 weeks ago, # |   0 Problem G becomes way easy you just have to find smallest consecutive A substring . The ans = Number of A(s) — Length of smallest consecutive A(s) substring . Submission = https://codeforces.com/contest/1873/submission/264596469
 » 9 days ago, # |   0 Problem G was very good for the beginners and thanks for the lovely Editorial .But the given implementation part (code) is bit complex and not easy to understand. I think I have written a code which is very easy to understand code out of all the solutions I've watched till now Here's my submission link for those who are struggling in the implementation part : https://codeforces.com/contest/1873/submission/265911111Approach is simple : If the string start with 'B' or ends with 'B' or if it has any two consecutive 'B' in it , then the answer is simply the sum of all A's in the string. Otherwise we have to eat the A's where it occurs more in a chunk , hence the chunk with minimum no. of A's would be ignore , so in this case we can simply print, Sum of all A's — (minimum number of A's in any chunk/group);<#sep#>You are given a string representing a mathematical expression in infix notation. Your task is to implement a function `evaluate_expression` that evaluates this expression and returns the result as a floating-point number. The expression consists of non-negative integers, decimal numbers, and the operators `+`, `-`, `*`, and `/`. Spaces may appear between numbers and operators. You can assume that the input expression is always valid and does not contain any invalid characters.

**Input:**
- A string `expression` (1 ≤ len(expression) ≤ 100) representing a valid mathematical expression.

**Output:**
- A floating-point number representing the result of the evaluated expression.

**Sample Input:**
```
"3 + 5 * (2 - 8)"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
-19.0
```

**Sample Input:**
```
"10 + 2 * 6.5"
```

**Sample Output:**
```
23.0
```

```python
def evaluate_expression(expression):
    """
    Evaluates a mathematical expression given in infix notation and returns the result as a floating-point number.
    
    :param expression: A string representing a valid mathematical expression.
    :return: A floating-point number representing the result of the evaluated expression.
    """
    def apply_operator(operators, values):
        operator = operators.pop()
        right = values.pop()
        left = values.pop()
        if operator == '+':
            values.append(left + right)
        elif operator == '-':
            values.append(left - right)
        elif operator == '*':
            values.append(left * right)
        elif operator == '/':
            values.append(left / right)

    def precedence(op):
        if op in ('+', '-'):
            return 1
        if op in ('*', '/'):
            return 2
        return 0

    expression = expression.replace(" ", "")
    operators = []
    values = []
    i = 0

    while i < len(expression):
        if expression[i] in '0123456789.':
            j = i
            while j < len(expression) and expression[j] in '0123456789.':
                j += 1
            values.append(float(expression[i:j]))
            i = j
        elif expression[i] == '(':
            operators.append(expression[i])
            i += 1
        elif expression[i] == ')':
            while operators and operators[-1] != '(':
                apply_operator(operators, values)
            operators.pop()  # Remove '('
            i += 1
        else:
            while (operators and precedence(operators[-1]) >= precedence(expression[i])):
                apply_operator(operators, values)
            operators.append(expression[i])
            i += 1

    while operators:
        apply_operator(operators, values)

    return values[0]
```<#sep#>336. Same.

H=15:

C≈33.33. Let's say 33.

Then squares: (15-4)*(33-4)=11*29=319 <336.

H=25, C=20 gives same as H=20,C=25.

Therefore maximum at H=20,C=25 or vice versa.

So with H=20 rows (4 blocks of 5 rows each) and C=25 columns (each block has 25 columns, so 5 columns per block's black spacing? Wait, no, the number of blacks per block is C. Wait, in the arrangement where each 5-row block has blacks every 5 columns, so per block, number of blacks per row is C/5. Since there are 5 rows in a block, total blacks per block is 5*(C/5)=C. Therefore, with 20 rows, we have 4 blocks, so total blacks are 4*C. To get 100 blacks, 4C=100 ⇒ C=25. Exactly. So that works.

Therefore, a grid of 20 rows and 25 columns arranged in 4 blocks of 5 rows each, each block shifted by 1 column from the previous. This uses 4*25=100 blacks. The number of 5x5 squares is (20-4)*(25-4)=16*21=336. 

Similarly, a 25x20 grid would give the same. 

Therefore, the maximum number of squares is 336? But wait, let me confirm that this arrangement indeed ensures each 5x5 square has exactly 5 blacks.

Take a square in the middle of the grid. Suppose rows 10-14 (which is the third block, shifted by 2 columns) and columns 5-9.

In rows10-14 (third block, shift2), blacks are at columns (2 +5k). So columns 2,7,12,17,22,... In columns5-9, the relevant column is 7 (since 2+5=7). So in each of the 5 rows (10-14), there's a black at column7. Thus, the square columns5-9 includes column7, so each row has one black at column7, totaling 5. 

Another example: a square spanning two blocks. Say rows5-9 (rows5 is end of first block, rows6-9 start of second block). Columns1-5.

Rows1-5 (first block): blacks at columns1,6,11,... So in rows5, column1 has a black.

Rows6-9 (part of second block, shift1): blacks at columns2,7,... So in columns1-5, column2 has blacks in rows6-9.

Thus, in the square rows5-9, columns1-5:

Row5: column1 (1 black).

Rows6-9: column2 (4 blacks).

Total: 1+4=5. Perfect.

Another example: square rows17-21 (but rows21 is beyond 20, so invalid in our 20-row grid). Let's pick rows16-20 (fourth block, shift3). Columns3-7.

Shift3 means blacks at columns3,8,13,18,... In columns3-7, column3 is present. So each row 16-20 has a black at column3. Thus, 5 blacks. 

Therefore, this arrangement works perfectly. Thus, with 20 rows and 25 columns, we can achieve 336 squares.

Is there a better arrangement? Let me see if we can get higher than 336.

Suppose we don't restrict to the shifted block arrangement, but find another pattern with higher density?

Alternatively, what if we arrange the black cells in both directions? For example, a 2D grid where both rows and columns are spaced appropriately so that each 5x5 square has exactly 5 blacks, but arranged in a way that allows more overlap.

Wait, but in the shifted rows method, we already achieve a very efficient use of black cells, giving 336 squares with 100 blacks. Perhaps this is the maximum.

Alternatively, let's think of each black cell being part of as many squares as possible. In the shifted rows arrangement, each black cell is in how many squares?

A black cell at (row r, column c). Since the rows are arranged in shifts, the vertical position affects how many vertical overlaps it can have.

In the 20x25 grid, a black cell in column c, row r. The number of squares that include this cell is determined by how many 5x5 squares can be placed such that they include this cell.

The number of vertical positions: the cell can be in any of the 5 rows of a 5-row block. The vertical range for squares that include row r is from max(1, r-4) to min(20, r+1). So the number of vertical starting positions is (r's row can be in a square starting at row a where a ≤ r ≤ a+4). The number of such a is from (r-4) down to 1, and up to (r) up to 20-4.

Wait, more precisely, the number of vertical starting rows for squares containing row r is:

The earliest starting row is 1, the latest is 20-4=16. For a particular row r, the starting rows can be from (r -4) to r, but clamped between 1 and 16.

Wait, actually, the starting row a must satisfy a ≤ r ≤ a+4 ⇒ a ≤ r and a ≥ r-4. Thus, a can range from max(1, r-4) to min(16, r). So the number of vertical starting positions is min(16, r) - max(1, r-4) +1.

Similarly for columns: the cell is in column c. The number of horizontal starting columns is from max(1, c-4) to min(25-4, c). So similar calculation.

However, this might get complex, but roughly, each black cell is part of about 5 vertical positions and 5 horizontal, totaling 25 squares. But with 100 black cells, total incidences would be 2500, so the number of squares would be 2500 /5=500. But this is higher than 336. But in reality, due to edge effects, it's less.

Wait but in our 20x25 grid, each interior black cell is indeed in 5 vertical and 5 horizontal, so 25 squares. But on edges, less. Let me compute for a central cell.

Take cell at (10,10). Vertical starting rows can be from 10-4=6 to 10 ⇒ 6,7,8,9,10 ⇒ 5 starting rows. Horizontal starting columns from 10-4=6 to 10 ⇒ 5 starting columns. Thus total squares:5*5=25. 

Total incidences for all cells would be roughly 20*25*25/(5x5)=? Wait total incidences is sum over all cells of their count. For interior cells, it's 25, edge cells less. Let's approximate. The total number of squares is 16*21=336. Each square has 5 blacks, so total incidences is 336*5=1680. Therefore, average per black cell is 1680/100=16.8. Which is less than 25, because edge cells have fewer.

The PN Junction
 Most semiconductor devices contain at least one
junction between p-type and n-type semiconductor
regions.
 Semiconductor device characteristics and operation are
intimately connected to these PN junctions, therefore
considerable attention is devoted initially to this basic
device.
 The PN junction diode provides characteristics that
are used in rectifiers and switching circuits and will
also be applied to other devices.
 The electrostatics of the PN junction is considered in
this chapter and the current-voltage characteristics of
the PN junction diode are developed in the next
chapter.
Tennessee Technological University

5
Basic Structure of the PN Junction
 The entire semiconductor is a single-crystal material
with one region doped with acceptor impurity atoms
“p-region” and the adjacent region doped with donor
atoms to form the “n-region”. The interface
separating n and p regions is the metallurgical
junction.
 We consider a step junction (the doping
concentration is uniform in each region and there is
an abrupt doping change at the junction.)
 Initially, at the metallurgical junction, there is a very
large density gradient in both the electron and hole
concentrations.
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013
6
Basic Structure of the PN Junction
 Majority carrier electrons in the n region will begin
diffusing into the p-region and majority carrier holes
in the p-region will begin diffusing into the n region.
 As electrons diffuse from the n region, positively
charged donor atoms are left behind. Similarly, as
holes diffuse from the p region, they uncover
negatively charged acceptor atoms.
 The net positive and negative charges induce an
electric field in the region near the metallurgical
junction from the positive to the negative charge, or
from the n to the p region.
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013
7
Basic Structure of the PN Junction
Fig. 7.1: Basic Structure of the PN Junction
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013
8
Basic Structure of the PN Junction
 The two regions are referred to as the space charge
region or depletion region. Essentially all electrons and
holes are swept out of the space charge region by the
electric field.
 Density gradients still exist in the majority carrier
concentrations at each edge of the space charge region.
We can think of a density gradient as producing a
"diffusion force" that acts on the majority carriers.
 The electric field in the space charge region produces
another force on the electrons and holes which is in
the opposite direction to the diffusion force for each
type of particle. In thermal equilibrium, the diffusion
force and the E-field force exactly balance each other.
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013

external excitation is applied are studied.
Assuming no voltage is applied across the PN junction, then
the junction is in thermal equilibrium and the Fermi energy
level is constant.
The conduction and valance band energies must bend as we go
through the space charge region, since the relative position of
the conduction and valence bands with respect to the Fermi
energy changes between p and n regions.
Electrons in the conduction band of the n region see a
potential barrier in trying to move into the conduction band of
the p region. This barrier is the built-in potential barrier and
is denoted by Vbi.
The potential Vbi maintains equilibrium, therefore no current is
produced by this voltage.

ln �<#sep#>You are tasked with designing a class hierarchy for managing employees and their salaries within a company. Implement a base class `Employee` with the following attributes: `employee_id`, `name`, `department`, and `salary`. The class should have an initializer method that accepts four parameters to set these attributes. Additionally, it should include a method `give_raise` that increases the `salary` by a specified percentage.

Next, create a subclass `Manager` that inherits from `Employee` and includes an additional attribute `team_size` to denote the number of employees managed by the manager. The `Manager` class should also have a method `add_employee_to_team` that increments the `team_size` by one.

**Sample Input/Output:**
- Input: 
  - Create an `Employee` object with `employee_id=1`, `name="John Doe"`, `department="Finance"`, `salary=50000`.
  - Call `give_raise(10)` on the `Employee` object.
  - Create a `Manager` object with `employee_id=2`, `name="Jane Smith"`, `department="IT"`, `salary=70000`, `team_size=5`.
  - Call `add_employee_to_team()` on the `Manager` object.
- Output:
  - The `Employee` object's salary should be 55000.
  - The `Manager` object's team size should be 6.

```python
class Employee:
    def __init__(self, employee_id, name, department, salary):
        """
        Initialize an Employee object with the given attributes.

        :param employee_id: Unique identifier for the employee.
        :param name: Name of the employee.
        :param department: Department where the employee works.
        :param salary: Salary of the employee.
        """
        self.employee_id = employee_id
        self.name = name
        self.department = department
        self.salary = salary

    def give_raise(self, percentage):
        """
        Increase the employee's salary by a specified percentage.

        :param percentage: The percentage by which to increase the salary.
        """
        self.salary += self.salary * (percentage / 100)

class Manager(Employee):
    def __init__(self, employee_id, name, department, salary, team_size=0):
        """
        Initialize a Manager object with the given attributes.

        :param employee_id: Unique identifier for the manager.
        :param name: Name of the manager.
        :param department: Department where the manager works.
        :param salary: Salary of the manager.
        :param team_size: Number of employees managed by the manager.
        """
        super().__init__(employee_id, name, department, salary)
        self.team_size = team_size

    def add_employee_to_team(self):
        """
        Increment the team size by one.
        """
        self.team_size += 1
```<#sep#>5, a=5. Then f(5,5)=1, since you need one 5x5 square with 5 blacks. Indeed, in our arrangement, a 5x5 square would require 5 blacks, so yes.

Zero applied bias
 At this time, we should note a subtle but important
point concerning notation.
 Previously in the discussion of a semiconductor
material, Nd and Na denoted donor, and acceptor
impurity concentrations in the same region, thereby
forming a compensated semiconductor.
 From this point on, Nd and Na will denote the net
donor and acceptor concentrations in the individual n
and p regions, respectively. If the p region, for
example, is a compensated material, then Na will
represent the difference between the actual acceptor
and donor impurity concentrations.
 The parameter Nd is defined in a similar manner for
the n region.

abruptly ends in the p region at x = -xp (xp is a
positive quantity).

Na = 1016cm‐<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a `BankAccount` class that simulates basic banking operations. The class should support the following functionalities:
- **Deposit**: Add a specified amount to the account balance. The deposit amount must be positive.
- **Withdraw**: Subtract a specified amount from the account balance. The withdrawal amount must be positive and should not exceed the current balance.
- **Check Balance**: Return the current balance of the account.

Your implementation should handle invalid operations gracefully by printing appropriate error messages.

**Input:**
- The first line contains an integer `n` (1 ≤ n ≤ 100), the number of operations.
- The next `n` lines describe the operations. Each operation is either:
  - `deposit X` where `X` is the amount to deposit.
  - `withdraw X` where `X` is the amount to withdraw.
  - `check_balance` to check the current balance.

**Output:**
- For each operation, print the result as specified in the method descriptions.

**Sample Input:**
```
5
deposit 100
withdraw 30
check_balance
withdraw 150
check_balance
```

**Sample Output:**
```
Deposited: 100. New Balance: 100
Withdrew: 30. Remaining Balance: 70
Current Balance: 70
Insufficient funds for withdrawal.
Current Balance: 70
```

```python
class BankAccount:
    def __init__(self, initial_balance=0):
        self.balance = initial_balance

    def deposit(self, amount):
        """
        Adds the specified amount to the account balance if the amount is positive.
        Prints an error message if the amount is not positive.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            self.balance += amount
            print(f"Deposited: {amount}. New Balance: {self.balance}")
        else:
            print("Deposit amount must be positive.")

    def withdraw(self, amount):
        """
        Subtracts the specified amount from the account balance if the amount is positive and does not exceed the current balance.
        Prints an error message if the amount is not positive or if there are insufficient funds.
        """
        if amount > 0:
            if self.balance >= amount:
                self.balance -= amount
                print(f"Withdrew: {amount}. Remaining Balance: {self.balance}")
            else:
                print("Insufficient funds for withdrawal.")
        else:
            print("Withdrawal amount must be positive.")

    def check_balance(self):
        """
        Prints the current balance of the account.
        """
        print(f"Current Balance: {self.balance}")

# Example usage:
# account = BankAccount(100)
# account.deposit(50)
# account.withdraw(30)
# account.check_balance()
# account.withdraw(150)
```<#sep#>5x5 squares that include a particular cell (i,j) is the number of positions where the square's top-left corner is from (i-4, j-4) to (i, j). But since the grid is infinite, it's infinite. But with finite blacks, we need to arrange them such that each black is in as many squares as possible without exceeding the total.

Alternatively, if we arrange all 100 blacks in a 5x20 grid as rows, then each black is in 1 row, so vertically only 1 dimension. The number of squares would be 16 (as before). But that's worse.

**Final Answer**
\boxed{336}
</think>To solve the problem of finding the maximum number of \(5 \times 5\) squares that can be formed with exactly 5 black cells each using 100 black cells on an infinite grid, we need an efficient arrangement of the black cells. Here is a clean and detailed version of the solution:

1. **Key Insight**:
   - Arrange the black cells in a periodic pattern such that every \(5 \times 5\) square contains exactly 5 black cells.
   - This can be achieved by spacing black cells every 5 columns in each row and shifting the columns of black cells by 1 in each subsequent set of 5 rows.

2. **Grid Arrangement**:
   - Divide the grid into blocks of 5 rows each.
   - In each block of 5 rows, place black cells every 5 columns.
   - Shift the starting column of black cells by 1 in each subsequent block of 5 rows.

3. **Example Calculation**:
   - For 100 black cells, we can arrange them in a grid of 20 rows and 25 columns.
   - This arrangement uses 4 blocks of 5 rows each, with each block shifted by 1 column from the previous block.
   - Each block of 5 rows with 25 columns contributes 25 black cells (since \(25 / 5 = 5\) per row, and \(5 \times 5 = 25\) per block).

4. **Counting the Squares**:
   - The number of vertical positions for a \(5 \times 5\) square in 20 rows is \(20 - 4 = 16\).
   - The number of horizontal positions for a \(5 \times 5\) square in 25 columns is \(25 - 4 = 21\).
   - The total number of \(5 \times 5\) squares is \(16 \times 21 = 336\).

Therefore, the maximum number of \(5 \times 5\) squares that can be formed with exactly 5 black cells each using 100 black cells is \(\boxed{336}\).<#sep#> group (2,5,8):

Similarly, the third group's elements are 6,7,1?<#sep#>DynamoDB: Using DELETE fails if attribute does not exist
I believe there is a mismatch in behavior between moto and the real DynamoDB:

- On the real DynamoDB, when using the DELETE operator in `update_item`  to remove an item from an attribute that does not exist, it will just be ignored.
- In moto, a cryptic error message is raised instead.

This is important, because it means that deleting the final element of a set is idempotent in AWS, but unfortunately not in moto.
See also https://github.com/getmoto/moto/issues/3296

### Example program

Requires `pip install moto boto3 pytest`. The following test should pass. It uses `DELETE` on an attribute that does not exist. DynamoDB will simply ignore this, but moto raises an exception (see below).
```
import pytest
import boto3
import moto

@pytest.fixture()
def boto_table():
   # This just sets up a table using "name" as primary key.
    with moto.mock_aws():
        dynamodb_resource = boto3.resource("dynamodb", region_name="eu-west-1")

        yield dynamodb_resource.create_table(
            TableName="test-table",
            AttributeDefinitions=[
                {"AttributeName": "name", "AttributeType": "S"},
            ],
            KeySchema=[{"AttributeName": "name", "KeyType": "HASH"}],
            BillingMode="PAY_PER_REQUEST",
        )

def test_remove_user_that_does_not_exist(boto_table):
    name = "name"
    user_name = "karl"
    initial_item = {"name": name}  # Note: this does not contain a 'users' attribute!

    boto_table.put_item(Item=initial_item)

    # Nevertheless, we now try to remove a user from it.
    response = boto_table.update_item(
        Key={"name": name},
        UpdateExpression="DELETE #users :users",
        ExpressionAttributeValues={":users": {user_name}},
        ExpressionAttributeNames={"#users": "users"},
        ReturnValues="ALL_NEW",
    )

    assert response["Attributes"] == initial_item
```

### Expected Behavior
The test should pass. (It does pass, if you add a users attribute, e.g. `initial_item={"name": name, "users":{"otto"}}`.)

### Actual Behaviour
I receive an error `botocore.exceptions.ClientError: An error occurred (ValidationException) when calling the UpdateItem operation: The provided key element does not match the schema`.

25
Reverse Applied Bias
 If we apply a potential between the p and n regions, we will no
longer be in an equilibrium condition and the Fermi energy level
will no longer be constant through the system.
 If a positive voltage is applied to the n-region with respect to the
p-region, as the positive potential is downward, the Fermi level on
the n side is below the Fermi level on the p side. The difference
between the two is equal to the applied voltage in units of energy.
 The total potential barrier, indicated by Vtotal has increased. The
applied potential is the reverse-bias condition. The total potential
barrier is now given by:
Vtotal  | Fn |  | Fp | VR
Vtotal  Vbi  VR
 where VR is the magnitude of the applied reverse-bias voltage and
Vbi is the same built-in potential barrier defined earlier.
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013

Reverse and Forward Applied Bias
Fig. 7.5: Energy band diagram of a PN Junction under reverse and forward bias
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013
27
Space Charge Width and Electric Field
 The electric fields in the neutral P and N regions are
essentially zero, or at least very small, which means that the
magnitude of the electric field in the space charge region
must increase above the thermal-equilibrium value due to
the applied voltage.
 The electric field originates on positive charge and
terminates on negative charge; this means that the number
of positive and negative charges must increase if the
electric field increases.
 For given impurity doping concentrations, the number of
positive and negative charges in the depletion region can be
increased only if the space charge width W increases.
 The space charge width W increases with an increasing
reverse-bias voltage VR.
Tennessee Technological University
Monday, October 21, 2013

case of reverse-bias can be written as:

Monday, October 21, 201<#sep#>You are tasked with implementing a Python class `CSVManipulator` to handle basic operations on CSV files. The class should support the following functionalities:

1. **Initialization**: The constructor `__init__(self, filename)` should initialize the object with the filename of the CSV file.
2. **Filtering**: The method `filter_by_column(self, column_name, value)` should filter rows in the CSV file where the specified column matches the given value. The method should return a list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a row in the CSV file.
3. **Summation**: The method `sum_column(self, column_name)` should sum up all the values in the specified column and return the result. Assume that the CSV file contains only numeric values in the columns that will be summed.

**Sample Input**:
Consider a CSV file `data.csv` with the following content:
```
name,age,score
Alice,25,85.5
Bob,30,90.0
Charlie,25,78.5
```

**Sample Output**:
- `filter_by_column('age', 25)` should return `[{'name': 'Alice', 'age': '25', 'score': '85.5'}, {'name': 'Charlie', 'age': '25', 'score': '78.5'}]`
- `sum_column('score')` should return `254.0`

```python
import csv

class CSVManipulator:
    def __init__(self, filename):
        """
        Initializes the CSVManipulator with the filename of the CSV file.
        
        :param filename: str, the name of the CSV file
        """
        self.filename = filename

    def filter_by_column(self, column_name, value):
        """
        Filters rows in the CSV file where the specified column matches the given value.
        
        :param column_name: str, the name of the column to filter by
        :param value: the value to match in the specified column
        :return: list of dictionaries, where each dictionary represents a row in the CSV file
        """
        filtered_rows = []
        with open(self.filename, mode='r', newline='') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            for row in reader:
                if row[column_name] == str(value):
                    filtered_rows.append(row)
        return filtered_rows

    def sum_column(self, column_name):
        """
        Sums up all the values in the specified column.
        
        :param column_name: str, the name of the column to sum
        :return: float, the sum of the values in the specified column
        """
        total = 0
        with open(self.filename, mode='r', newline='') as file:
            reader = csv.DictReader(file)
            for row in reader:
                total += float(row[column_name])
        return total
```<#sep#>How many ordered triples $(a, b, c)$ of positive integers are there such that none of $a, b, c$ exceeds $2010$ and each of $a, b, c$ divides $a + b + c$?

<think>
Okay, let's try to tackle this problem. We need to find the number of ordered triples (a, b, c) of positive integers where each of a, b, c is at most 2010, and each of them divides the sum a + b + c. Hmm, that seems a bit tricky, but let's break it down step by step.

First, let's understand the conditions. For each element in the triple (a, b, c), it must divide the sum of all three. So, a | (a + b + c), b | (a + b + c), and c | (a + b + c). Also, all a, b, c are positive integers not exceeding 2010.

Let me think about what these divisibility conditions imply. If a divides a + b + c, then a divides (b + c), right? Because a divides the sum, so when you subtract a from the sum, a must divide the remaining part. Wait, let me check that. If a divides a + b + c, then (a + b + c) mod a = 0. But (a + b + c) mod a is equal to (b + c) mod a, so yes, a divides (b + c). Similarly, b divides (a + c), and c divides (a + b). So each variable divides the sum of the other two. That seems like a key insight.

So now, the problem reduces to finding triples (a, b, c) such that a | (b + c), b | (a + c), c | (a + b), and 1 ≤ a, b, c ≤ 2010.

Hmm, how do we approach this? Maybe we can consider cases where some or all of a, b, c are equal. For example, maybe all three are equal? If a = b = c, then each divides 3a, which is true because a divides 3a. So in that case, all triples where a = b = c are valid. Since each is at most 2010, the number of such triples is 2010. So that's a start. But there are probably many more triples where the numbers are not equal.

Alternatively, maybe two are equal? Let's say a = b. Then, we need a | (a + c) ⇒ a divides c, and c | (2a). Also, a | (a + c) ⇒ since a divides c, let c = ka for some integer k. Then, since c | (2a), ka | 2a ⇒ k | 2. So k can be 1 or 2. Therefore, c = a or 2a. But c has to be ≤2010. So if c = a, then that's the case when all three are equal. If c = 2a, then since c ≤2010, a ≤1005. So in this case, if a = b, then c can be a or 2a (if 2a ≤2010). So for a from 1 to 1005, c can be 2a. Then for a from 1 to 2010, c can be a. Wait, but if a = b = c, that's already counted in the all equal case. So maybe when a = b ≠ c, we have two possibilities for c: either c = a or c = 2a. Wait, but c = a would make all three equal, which is a separate case. So maybe we need to consider these as separate cases.

b divides (a + c)

Junction Capacitance
 Since we have a separation of positive and negative
charges in the depletion region, a capacitance is
associated with the PN Junction.
 An increase in the reverse-bias voltage dVR will
uncover additional positive charges in the n region
and additional negative charges in the p region. The
junction capacitance is defined as:

 In the PN Junctions considered so far, we have
assumed that each semiconductor region has been
uniformly doped.
 In actual PN Junctions, this isn’t always true.
 In some electronic applications, specific nonuniform doping profiles are used to obtain special
PN Junction capacitance characteristics.
 Different types of doping profiles are used in:
 Uniformly Doped Junctions
 Hyper-abrupt Junctions
